The venerable Upāli asked the Blessed One, the Buddha: "Venerable one, the Blessed One has declared that if a monk, without an owner, causes a dwelling to be built for his own sake, that monk must build the dwelling within the prescribed measure — and the measure of the dwelling is this: twelve spans of the Sugata in length and seven spans in width, measured on the interior. He must bring fellow monks to inspect the site, and those monks who are brought must check that the site is suitable, free from dispute, and proper to build upon. If a monk, though the site is unsuitable, or is in dispute, or is improper to build upon, still causes a dwelling to be built for himself without an owner, or does not bring fellow monks to inspect the site, or does not show the site to the monks who have been brought, then if he exceeds the measure it becomes a Saṅgha-remainder offense." What is meant by this?

"Venerable one, if a monk causes a dwelling to be built without having examined the site — what does that become?"

"It becomes a serious misdeed."

"If he examines the site and then causes the dwelling to be built — what does that become?"

"It becomes a wrongdoing."

"If he obtains permission for the dwelling but does not build it — what does that become?"

"It becomes a wrongdoing."

"If he begins it himself and then abandons it — what does that become?"

"It becomes a serious misdeed."

"If he causes another to abandon it — what does that become?"

"It becomes a serious misdeed."

"If he causes an uncovered structure to be built — what does that become?"

"It becomes a serious misdeed."

"If he deliberately falls into it — what does that become?"

"It becomes a serious misdeed."

Likewise: if one has parted from life, or dies while still a householder, or dies on account of being a wandering ascetic, or on account of food, or on account of being a paṇḍaka, or on account of having expelled a nun, or on account of not having committed a killing, or having killed one's father, or having killed one's mother, or having killed an arhat, or having caused a schism in the Saṅgha, or having drawn blood from a Tathāgata with malicious intent, or being a non-Buddhist adherent, or having joined the non-Buddhists, or dwelling in concealment, or being one who should not remain, or being initially incompatible, or dwelling separately, or being one who avows —

"If one seizes the training itself as a loan, what will come of it?" "It becomes a grave offense."

"If one who has not received full ordination performs an act of joining, and one causes that to stand as not having received full ordination, what will come of it?" "It is an offense."

"If one who has not received full ordination performs an act of joining, and one causes that to stand as having received full ordination in the process, what will come of it?" "It is an offense."

"If one who has not received full ordination performs an act of joining, and one causes that to stand as having fully received ordination, what will come of it?" "It becomes a grave offense."

"If one who is in the process of receiving full ordination performs an act of joining, and one causes that to stand as being in the process of receiving full ordination, what will come of it?" "It is an offense."

"If one who is in the process of receiving full ordination performs an act of joining, and one causes that to stand as having fully received ordination, what will come of it?" "It becomes a grave offense."

"If one who has fully received ordination performs an act of joining, and one causes that to stand as having fully received ordination, what will come of it?" "It entails a Saṅgha remainder."

"If one who has fully received ordination performs an act of joining, and one causes that to stand as not having received full ordination, what will come of it?" "It is an offense."

"Venerable One, suppose a monk begins to build a cell, and in the course of that his status changes, and he becomes a nun — if one then causes that to stand as valid, what will come of it?" "It is an offense."

"Venerable One, suppose a nun begins to build a cell, and in the course of that her status changes, and she becomes a monk — if one then causes that to stand as valid, what will come of it?" "It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable One, are there cases in which a monk builds a cell and yet it does not entail a Saṅgha remainder?" "There are. For one who has previously incurred a downfall, one who dwells in concealment, one who is initially incompatible, a paṇḍaka, and one who has expelled a nun — in those cases it becomes merely an offense."

"If one who has been granted the training grants permission to build, what will come of it?" "It entails a Saṅgha remainder." "If a nun grants permission to build, what will come of it?" "It is an offense." "For a probationer, a male novice, and a female novice — it is an offense."

The venerable attendant retinue asked the Blessed One, the buddha: "Venerable One, you have said that when a monk who has an owner undertakes to build a great monastery hall for the sake of the Saṅgha, he should lead monks to inspect the site, and the monks who are led should examine whether the site is suitable, free of disputes, and fit to begin upon. Yet if a monk deems the site unsuitable, or involving disputes, or not fit to begin upon, and the owner nevertheless proceeds to build the great monastery hall for the sake of the Saṅgha, or does not lead monks to inspect the site, or, even having led monks, does not show them the site — in such a case, you have said it entails a Saṅgha remainder. What does that mean?" "With the exception of exceeding the limit and the act of raising up itself, the remainder of the rule is to be stated for a great monastery hall in the same way as it has been set out for a Saṅgha-remainder in the case of a cell."

The venerable attendant retinue asked the Blessed One, the buddha: "Venerable One, you have also said: when a monk, angry and with aversion, displeased and wishing by any means that another may be separated from the holy life, accuses monks of a groundless defeat — a matter of no foundation — and when that dispute is subsequently examined, whether at another time or not, whether asked or not asked, and is found to be groundless, and that monk is established to have spoken out of aversion — what does it mean when you said that this entails a Saṅgha remainder?"

"Venerable One, if a monk accuses another monk through a messenger, groundlessly, of a matter that would constitute a defeat — what comes of that?" "It becomes a grave offense." "Likewise, if he accuses him by means of a written letter, or by hand signal, or by attributing words to another, or by accusation through an intermediary — what comes of that?" "It becomes a grave offense."

"Venerable One, if a monk accuses a monk who is insane, groundlessly, of a matter that would constitute a defeat — what comes of that?" "It becomes a grave offense." "Likewise, if he accuses a monk who is mentally disturbed, or who is overcome by feeling, or who is deaf, or who is mentally deficient, or who is overcome by sleep, or who is occupied with work, or who is deeply intoxicated, or who is in meditative absorption, or who is dwelling somewhere else — groundlessly, of a matter that would constitute a defeat — what comes of that?" "It becomes a wrongdoing."

The first group concerns extremes and what lacks a boundary,

"Venerable One, if a monk accuses — groundlessly and without basis — a monk who has himself confessed: 'I have engaged in sexual misconduct, in a matter arisen from sexual union,' whether accusing him of theft, of murder, or of having taught the Dharma as a superior — what comes of that?" "It entails a Saṅgha remainder."

"Venerable One, if a monk accuses — groundlessly — a monk who has himself confessed: 'I have stolen a major valuable,' whether accusing him of murder, of having taught the Dharma as a superior, or of sexual misconduct — what comes of that?" "It entails a Saṅgha remainder."

"Venerable One, if a monk who has himself confessed: 'I have taken a human life' —

— accuses him, groundlessly, of having taught the Dharma as a superior, of sexual misconduct, or of theft — what comes of that?" "It entails a Saṅgha remainder."

"Venerable One, if a monk who has himself confessed: 'I have taught the Dharma as superior' — if another monk accuses him, groundlessly, of sexual misconduct, of theft, or of taking a life — what comes of that?" "It entails a Saṅgha remainder."

"Venerable One, when the Blessed One declared that if a monk groundlessly accuses another monk by means of a defeat — what was meant by that?"

"Venerable One, if a monk groundlessly accuses another monk by means of a heinous act — if he accuses him of killing his mother, his father, or an arhat — it entails a Saṅgha remainder. But if he accuses him, groundlessly, of causing schism in the Saṅgha, or of drawing blood with malicious intent, that is a grave offence. Why is that? One who accuses another of killing his father, his mother, or an arhat has levelled the accusation by means of a defeat. One who accuses another of causing schism in the Saṅgha or of drawing blood with malicious intent has levelled the accusation by means of a grave offence; and a grave offence does not render one no longer a monk — it is something to be confessed."

"Venerable One, if a monk groundlessly accuses another monk by means of a defeat, and that entails a Saṅgha remainder — then if he accuses him, groundlessly, by means of a Saṅgha-remainder offence, what comes of that?" "It becomes a transgression."

"If he accuses him, groundlessly, by means of what is to be individually confessed, and by means of a grave offence — what comes of that?" "It is a grave offence."

"Likewise, if he accuses him, groundlessly, by means of a grave offence — what comes of that?" "It is a grave offence."

"Venerable One, when the Blessed One declared that if a monk groundlessly accuses another monk by means of a defeat it entails a Saṅgha remainder — what was meant by that?"

"Venerable One, if a monk groundlessly accuses a female trainee, a novice, or a novice nun by means of a defeat — what comes of that?" "It becomes a grave offence."

"If a female trainee groundlessly accuses a fully ordained nun by means of a defeat — what comes of that?" "It entails a Saṅgha remainder."

"If a fully ordained nun groundlessly accuses a fully ordained monk by means of a defeat — what comes of that?" "It entails a Saṅgha remainder."

"If a female trainee groundlessly accuses a fully ordained nun, a novice, or a novice nun by means of a defeat — what comes of that?" "It becomes a grave offence."

"If a fully ordained nun groundlessly accuses a fully ordained monk, a fully ordained nun, a female trainee, a novice, or a novice nun by means of a defeat — what comes of that?" "It is a grave offence."

"If a novice groundlessly accuses a fully ordained monk, a fully ordained nun, a female trainee, a novice, or a novice nun by means of a defeat — what comes of that?" "It is a grave offence."

"If a novice nun groundlessly accuses a fully ordained monk, a fully ordained nun, a female trainee, a novice, or a novice nun by means of a defeat — what comes of that?" "It is a grave offence."

"Venerable One, if a monk himself writes a letter and says: 'From this letter I am telling you — you have incurred the defeat of sexual misconduct' — what comes of that?" "It becomes a grave offence."

"Venerable One, is there a case where a monk groundlessly accuses another monk by means of a defeat, and yet the Saṅgha does not increase?" "Yes, there is: when a previous transgression has occurred, when one is residing by stealth, when one was originally incompatible, when one is a eunuch, and when one has expelled a nun — in those cases it is a misdeed."

Then the venerable Upāli addressed the Blessed One, the Buddha: "Venerable One, the Blessed One has also stated: when a monk, having become angry and full of aversion, thinking, 'By any means I will separate this one from the holy life,' falsely accuses monks who have no transgression, in a manner not consistent with the facts, by means of an allegation of defeat — whether or not, at some other time, he is questioned about it — and that allegation is also not consistent with the facts, and since that allegation is not consistent with the facts, from having thought of it only briefly and having taken up some matter, and the monk too, while remaining in aversion, speaks out of aversion — what is meant by the declaration 'the Saṅgha increases'?" "The remainder of the main rule: concerning groundlessness, it is just as it stands — what is said briefly there should be stated at length."

Summary verse:

Admonishment and petition,
the foolish and the mindful two;
fourth: sitting without appointment,
with resolve one, and in open space —
sitting should be stated in full.

Then the venerable Upāli addressed the Blessed One, the Buddha: "Venerable One, when a monk works to undermine and create schism in order to divide a harmonious Saṅgha, fully takes up that contentious matter and persists in it — the monks should instruct that monk as follows: 'Venerable one, do not work to undermine the harmonious Saṅgha in order to divide it. Do not fully take up and persist in a contentious matter that would lead to schism. Be in harmony with the Saṅgha, venerable one. Let the Saṅgha not be divided. Rejoice together, without conflict, supreme — become like water and milk mingled together, illuminate the Teacher's teaching, and abide in comfort and ease. Venerable one, please abandon this kind of basis for dividing the Saṅgha.' If, when the monks instruct that monk in this way, he abandons that basis — good. If he does not abandon it, the monks should instruct and admonish him a second and a third time for the sake of his abandoning it. If, when instructed and admonished a second and a third time, he abandons that basis — good. If he does not abandon it — the Saṅgha increases. So it has been declared. What is meant by this?"

"Venerable One, if a monk, without first admonishing by means of a friendly word, proceeds to admonish by means of the fourfold formal act — is it called an admonishment or is it not called an admonishment?" The Blessed One declared: "It is not called an admonishment." "Does the admonishment take effect?" "Upāli, it does not take effect, and it constitutes a serious offence against the Saṅgha as well."

"Venerable One, if a monk first admonishes by means of a friendly word and then admonishes by means of the fourfold formal act — is it called an admonishment or not?" The Blessed One declared: "It is called an admonishment." "Does the admonishment take effect?" "Upāli, it does take effect, and it does not constitute a serious offence against the Saṅgha."

"Venerable One, if, without having performed the act of expulsion, one proceeds to give a friendly admonishment — is it called an admonishment or not?" The Blessed One declared: "It is not to be called an admonishment." "Does the admonishment take effect?" "Upāli, it does not take effect, and it constitutes a serious offence against the Saṅgha as well."

"Venerable One, if the act of expulsion has been performed and one then admonishes by means of a friendly word — is it called an admonishment or not?" The Blessed One declared: "It is called an admonishment."

"Does the admonishment take effect? Is it a proper admonishment?" "It does take effect as a friendly admonishment, and it does not constitute a serious offence against the Saṅgha either."

"Venerable One, if, without having performed the act of expulsion, one approaches the Saṅgha with the intention of leaving — saying 'I am going' — whether one goes out of aversion, goes out of delusion, or goes out of fear: what does this become?" "It is a transgression. Should one admonish, or should one not admonish?" The Blessed One declared: "One should not admonish." "Does the admonishment take effect? Is it a proper admonishment?" "It does not take effect as a friendly admonishment, and it also constitutes a serious offence against the Saṅgha."

"Having performed the act of expulsion, if one then approaches the Saṅgha with the intention of leaving — saying 'I am going' — whether one goes out of aversion, goes out of delusion, or goes out of fear: what does this become?" "It is a transgression. Should one admonish, or should one not admonish?" The Blessed One declared: "One should admonish." "Does the admonishment take effect? Is it a proper admonishment?" "It does take effect as a friendly admonishment, and it does not constitute a serious offence against the Saṅgha either."

"Venerable One, if without having first discussed the matter or reminded the person, one causes oneself to abide in a state of discomfort with regard to the instruction — what does this become?" "It is a transgression. Should one admonish, or should one not admonish?" The Blessed One declared: "One should not admonish." "Does the admonishment take effect? Is it a proper admonishment?" "It does not take effect as a friendly admonishment."

"Venerable One, if, having first discussed the matter and reminded the person, one then causes oneself to abide in a state of discomfort with regard to the instruction — what does this become?" "It is a transgression. Should one admonish, or should one not admonish?" The Blessed One declared: "One should admonish." "Does the admonishment take effect? Is it a proper admonishment?" "It does take effect as a friendly admonishment."

"If those who are within the boundary admonish those who are outside the boundary, is it called an admonishment? Is it a proper admonishment?" "It is not a proper admonishment." "If those who are outside the boundary admonish those who are within the boundary, is it called an admonishment? Is it a proper admonishment?" "It is not a proper admonishment."

"Venerable One, if the monk who performs the act, being within the boundary, admonishes those who are outside the boundary — is it called an admonishment? Is it a proper admonishment?" "It is not a proper admonishment." "Venerable One, if the monk who performs the act, being outside the boundary, admonishes those who are within the boundary — is it called an admonishment? Is it a proper admonishment?" "It is not a proper admonishment."

"Venerable One, if the monk who performs the act presses upon the boundary line and, having compressed it, admonishes while situated at the boundary — is it called an admonishment or not?" "It is a proper friendly admonishment." "Does the admonishment take effect? Is it a proper admonishment?" "It does take effect as a friendly admonishment."

"Venerable One, if someone who has not been appointed as the monk to perform the act expels four individuals who are within the boundary — is it called an expulsion? Is it a proper admonishment?" "It is not a proper admonishment." "Venerable One, if one who has been appointed as the monk to perform the act expels four individuals who are within the boundary — is it called an expulsion? Is it a proper admonishment?" "If he has expelled them one by one, two by two, or three by three, but not all four together, it is called an expulsion."

"Venerable One, if the monk who performs the act admonishes four monks who are dwelling equally in the four directions within the boundary — does the admonishment both take effect and entail no downfall whatsoever, while also not causing disharmony in the Saṅgha?" "Yes, Upāli, that is the case — provided they confer intention upon one another and he admonishes them one by one, two by two, and three by three, but not four by four. Why is that? Because it is not permissible for an assembly to perform the acts of an assembly."

"If the monk who performs the act is situated on one side only, is it permissible to perform all acts pertaining to all four dwelling-places?" "Upāli, if the monk who performs the act presses down upon the boundary marker — whether a board, a small board, a key, or an elongated key — of all four quarters simultaneously, and if at each of those dwelling-places there are acts of the following kinds nearby — acts requiring a quorum of four, acts requiring a quorum of five, acts requiring a quorum of ten, and acts requiring a quorum of twenty — then it is permissible."

"Why is that?" Because some of them perform the act, while others maintain the quorum. If one who is on the ground gives a directive to those who are in the air, is that considered a proper directive? It is not a valid quorum. And conversely, venerable sir, if the one who performs the act is in the air and gives a directive to those who are on the ground, is that considered a proper directive? It is not a valid quorum.

Just as the Saṅgha constitutes a surplus in relation to the monk, and the training has been conferred accordingly, so too in relation to the fully ordained nun there is a Saṅgha surplus. For the female trainee, the novice monk, and the novice nun, it constitutes an offence. For one in whom a transgression or the like has previously arisen, it is likewise only an offence, as before. Thus this section is also to be set forth as is appropriate for the three Saṅgha-surplus cases. The Undetermined Cases from the larger recension of Upāli's Questions is complete.

Summary verse:
The boundary, the path, and the royal lineage,
the aggregate, and likewise the surplus,
the mouth, and the sugar, and hvags,
molasses, ghee, and the two undetermined cases.

Venerable Upāli then asked the Blessed One, the Buddha: "Venerable sir, the Blessed One has also spoken of this: a monk who, together with a woman, sits alone in a secluded and sheltered place, in a posture suitable for sensual conduct — and if thereupon a trustworthy laywoman, one whose words are credible, speaks to him, saying that he has committed any one of three things: a root defeat, a Saṅgha-surplus offence, or any offence whatsoever entailing a formal act — and the monk, while seated there, acknowledges it, thereby causing him to commit any one of those three: a root defeat, a Saṅgha-surplus offence, or any offence entailing a formal act — and the trustworthy laywoman with credible words causes that monk to act in accordance with whatever she has said — that is an undetermined case.

"And again, a monk who, together with a woman, sits alone in a secluded and sheltered place, in a posture not suitable for sensual conduct — and if thereupon a trustworthy laywoman, one whose words are credible, speaks to him, saying that he has committed any one of two things: a Saṅgha-surplus offence or any offence whatsoever entailing a formal act — and the monk acknowledges being so seated — and thereby commits any one of those two: a Saṅgha-surplus offence or any offence entailing a formal act — and the trustworthy laywoman with credible words causes that monk to act in accordance with whatever she has said — that too is an undetermined case." What does this mean?

Venerable sir, if a trustworthy laywoman with credible words comes before the monks and says: "Venerable ones, I saw this monk commit a first extreme act," should one act on the word of a trustworthy laywoman with credible words, or should one not proceed? One should investigate the matter itself, not proceed immediately. First, one should ask whether there is a companion of his present there. If she says "Yes, there is," then both should be questioned. If their accounts agree, one should proceed on the word of the trustworthy laywoman with credible words, and inquire into the matter itself. But if she says: "That first extreme act did not occur, yet I did see the second, third, or fourth extreme act," then the monk should be led to seek resolution. In the same way as for the second, third, and fourth, so likewise should the matter be set forth.

Venerable sir, if a trustworthy laywoman with credible words comes before the monks and says: "Venerable ones, I saw a certain monk engaging in corruption through the mouth" —

the monks should neither authorize the trustworthy laywoman with credible words to act, nor prevent her from acting. They should neither authorize her to inquire into the matter itself, nor prevent her from doing so. For the time being, one should ask whether the monk has any senior companion. If she says: "Yes, he does," one should question both of them. If their accounts agree, the trustworthy laywoman with credible words should be authorized to act, and she should be directed to inquire into the matter itself. But if she says: "He did not engage in corruption through the anal passage; rather, he engaged in corruption through the urinary passage" — then the monk should be led to seek resolution. Just as the case of the oral passage was set forth in full, so likewise should the anal passage and the urinary passage be set forth in the same way.

Venerable sir, if a trustworthy laywoman with credible words comes before the monks and says: "Venerable ones, I saw a certain monk engaging in corruption together with a woman of the warrior class (kṣatriya)" — then the monks should neither authorize the trustworthy laywoman with credible words to act, nor prevent her from acting; they should neither authorize her to inquire into the matter itself, nor prevent her from doing so. For the time being, one should ask whether the monk has any senior companion. If she says: "Yes, he does," one should question both of them. If their accounts agree, the trustworthy laywoman with credible words should be authorized to act, and she should be directed to inquire into the matter itself. But if she says: "He did not engage in corruption with the warrior-class woman; rather, he engaged in corruption with a brahmin woman" — then the monk should be led to seek resolution. Just as the case of the warrior-class woman was set forth in full, so likewise should the case of the brahmin-class woman, a woman of the merchant class, a woman of the commoner class, a white woman, a dark woman, a dark-blue woman, a tall woman, a short woman, a beautiful woman, a thin woman, a brown-complexioned woman, a well-formed woman, a walking woman, a sitting woman, a standing woman, and a lying-down woman all be set forth in full detail.

Venerable sir, if a trustworthy laywoman with credible words comes before the monks and says: "Venerable ones, I saw a certain monk committing the first extreme act of drawing forth a heap" — then the monks should neither authorize the trustworthy laywoman with credible words to act, nor prevent her from acting; they should neither authorize her to inquire into the matter itself, nor prevent her from doing so. For the time being, one should ask whether the monk has any senior companion. If she says: "Yes, he does," one should question both of them. If their accounts agree, the trustworthy laywoman with credible words should be authorized to act, and she should be directed to inquire into the matter itself. But if she says: "That first extreme heap did not occur; however, the second, third, fourth, and fifth transgressive heap did occur" — this should be set forth in full detail.

Venerable sir, if a trustworthy laywoman with credible words comes before the monks and says: "Venerable ones, I saw a certain monk committing the first act of taking from what belongs to the Saṅgha" — then the monks should neither authorize the trustworthy laywoman with credible words to act, nor prevent her from acting; they should neither authorize her to inquire into the matter itself, nor prevent her from doing so. For the time being, one should ask whether the monk has any senior companion. If she says: "Yes, he does," one should question both of them. If their accounts agree, the trustworthy laywoman with credible words should be authorized to act, and she should be directed to inquire into the matter itself.

However, if the monk says: "The first act of taking from what belongs to the Saṅgha did not occur, but the second, third, fourth, and so on up to the thirteenth did" — then the matter should be inquired into by the monk. Just as with the first, so too should it be stated with regard to the second, third, and on up to the thirteenth act of taking from what belongs to the Saṅgha.

If a venerable, trustworthy laywoman with credible words comes before the monks and says: "Venerable ones, I have seen a monk committing an act of transgression by mouth" — then the monks should neither authorize the trustworthy laywoman with credible words to act, nor prevent her from acting; they should neither authorize her to inquire into the matter itself, nor prevent her from doing so. For the time being, one should ask whether the monk has any senior companion. If she says: "Yes, he does," one should question both of them. If their accounts agree, the trustworthy laywoman with credible words should be authorized to act, and she should be directed to inquire into the matter itself.

However, if she says: "He did not commit a transgression by mouth, but he did cause seminal emission to flow from outside, into the inner part of the lip" — then the matter should be inquired into by the monk.

If a venerable, trustworthy laywoman with credible words comes before the monks and says: "Venerable ones, I have seen a monk committing a transgression in the passage used for urination" — then the monks should neither authorize the trustworthy laywoman with credible words to act, nor prevent her from acting; they should neither authorize her to inquire into the matter itself, nor prevent her from doing so. For the time being, one should ask whether the monk has any senior companion. If she says: "Yes, he does," one should question both of them. If their accounts agree, the trustworthy laywoman with credible words should be authorized to act, and she should be directed to inquire into the matter itself.

However, if she says: "He did not commit a transgression in the passage used for urination, but he did cause seminal emission to flow onto the inner thigh" — then the matter should be inquired into by the monk.

If a venerable, trustworthy laywoman with credible words comes before the monks and says: "Venerable ones, I have seen a monk committing a transgression in the passage used for defecation" — then the monks should neither authorize the trustworthy laywoman with credible words to act, nor prevent her from acting; they should neither authorize her to inquire into the matter itself, nor prevent her from doing so. For the time being, one should ask whether the monk has any senior companion. If she says: "Yes, he does," one should question both of them. If their accounts agree, the trustworthy laywoman with credible words should be authorized to act, and she should be directed to inquire into the matter itself.

However, if she says: "He did not commit a transgression in the passage used for defecation, but he did cause seminal emission to flow onto the fold of the buttocks" — then the matter should be inquired into by the monk.

If a venerable, trustworthy laywoman with credible words comes before the monks and says: "Venerable ones, I have seen a monk drinking beer at an improper time" — then the monks should neither authorize the trustworthy laywoman with credible words to act, nor prevent her from acting; they should neither authorize her to inquire into the matter itself, nor prevent her from doing so. For the time being, one should ask whether the monk has any senior companion. If she says: "Yes, he does," one should question both of them. If their accounts agree, the trustworthy laywoman with credible words should be authorized to act, and she should be directed to inquire into the matter itself.

However, if she says: "He did not drink beer at an improper time, but he did drink meat-broth" — then the matter should be inquired into by the monk.

Venerable ones, if a trustworthy laywoman with credible words comes before the assembly of monks and says: "Venerable ones, I have seen a monk drinking sugarcane beer" — then the trustworthy laywoman with credible words should neither be authorized to act nor prevented from acting; she should neither be authorized to inquire into the matter itself, nor prevented from doing so. For the time being, one should ask whether the monk has any senior companion. If she says: "Yes, he does," one should question both of them. If their accounts agree, the trustworthy laywoman with credible words should be authorized to act, and she should be directed to inquire into the matter itself. However, if she says: "He did not drink sugarcane beer at an improper time, but he did eat meat" — then the matter should be inquired into by the monk.

The Vinaya: the Supreme Text, the Fourth Fascicle.

Venerable ones, if a trustworthy laywoman with credible words comes before the assembly of monks and says: "Venerable ones, I have seen a monk eating meat at an improper time" — then the trustworthy laywoman with credible words should neither be authorized to act nor prevented from acting; she should neither be authorized to inquire into the matter itself, nor prevented from doing so. For the time being, one should ask whether the monk has any senior companion. If she says: "Yes, he does," one should question both of them. If their accounts agree, the trustworthy laywoman with credible words should be authorized to act, and she should be directed to inquire into the matter itself. However, if she says: "He did not eat meat at an improper time, but he did eat sugarcane" — then the matter should be inquired into by the monk.

Venerable ones, if a trustworthy laywoman with credible words comes before the assembly of monks and says: "Venerable ones, I have seen a monk eating food at an improper time" — then the trustworthy laywoman with credible words should neither be authorized to act nor prevented from acting; she should neither be authorized to inquire into the matter itself, nor prevented from doing so. For the time being, one should ask whether the monk has any senior companion. If she says: "Yes, he does," one should question both of them. If their accounts agree, the trustworthy laywoman with credible words should be authorized to act, and she should be directed to inquire into the matter itself. However, if she says: "He did not eat food at an improper time, but he did eat butter" — then the matter should be inquired into by the monk.

In all such cases of uncertain matters, those trustworthy laywomen with credible words who speak of what they have genuinely seen — the matter should be inquired into by the monk. The section from the Kashgar recension dealing with uncertain matters is complete.

Summary verse:

The matter itself being lost, or burned,
mind disturbed, without consecration,
being suitable, and having passed the time —
the mark, the basis, and the having been kept.

The venerable Upāli asked the Blessed One, the buddha: "Venerable one, the Blessed One has declared that a monk who has received a robe, having stretched it out to stiffen it, must keep any surplus robe for up to ten days; if he keeps it beyond that, it constitutes a transgression requiring relinquishment. Venerable one, could it be that the very same robe has passed ten days and also lacks a single day?" Upāli said: "It is so. Thus, if a monk consecrates a robe that has passed ten days, and then, without any robe at all, he is present outside the boundary at the moment of dawn, that robe has both passed ten days and is lacking one day."

"Venerable one, for the time being, if a monk's robe requiring relinquishment becomes damaged, should he confess the transgression?" Upāli replied: "He should confess it with only the bare confession."

"Venerable one, if the transgression has already been confessed and the robe is then recovered, should it be relinquished?" Upāli replied: "It need not be relinquished. One should confess with only the bare confession."

"Venerable one, for the time being, if a monk's robe requiring relinquishment is burned by fire or eaten away, whether in fragments or entirely — should those remnants be relinquished?" Upāli replied: "It need not be relinquished. One should confess with only the bare confession. However, if any piece remaining is a full cubit in extent, that should be relinquished."

"Venerable one, for the time being, if a monk obtains a robe and five nights have elapsed, and his mind then becomes disturbed, from what point should the ten nights be counted?" Upāli replied: "From the point at which he recovers his own mind."

"Venerable one, could it be that a monk who has neither consecrated nor made proper use of a robe — whether it be the eighth or ninth robe — and yet keeps it for the remainder of his life, incurs no transgression?" Upāli replied: "It does incur a transgression. The way it works is this: each day the monk should take the robe out and have another consecrate it."

"Venerable one, what is meant by the Blessed One's statement that a monk must relinquish a robe that has passed ten days?" Upāli replied: "Venerable one, could it be that even if a monk keeps an extra robe for more than ten nights, it does not become a transgression requiring relinquishment?" Upāli replied: "It does incur such a transgression. The cases are these: where the fiber of the cloth is acceptable but the thread is not; where the thread is acceptable but the fiber is not; and where neither is acceptable. Unacceptable materials are camel wool, cow hair, goat wool, deer wool, blended materials, and unwoven fabric. A robe made of such materials, if kept for more than ten days, becomes a serious offense."

"Venerable one, what is meant by the Blessed One's statement that monks should keep an extra robe for up to ten days?" Upāli replied: "Venerable one, could it be that a monk who keeps an extra robe for the remainder of his life incurs no transgression?" Upāli replied: "It does incur a transgression — if the time limit of ten days is exceeded."

"Venerable one, could it be that keeping that very same robe is both a transgression and not a transgression?" Upāli replied: "It is so. If that monk keeps a precious robe, there is no transgression; but if he undergoes the transformation of ordination status and reverts to being a monk, then keeping it becomes a transgression."

"Venerable one, for the time being, if a monk obtains a robe when it is the tenth night, and then he undergoes the transformation of ordination status and reverts to being a nun, and the tenth night then passes — what is the outcome?" Upāli replied: "It becomes a transgression requiring relinquishment."

"Venerable one, for the time being, if a nun obtains a robe when it is the tenth night, and then she undergoes the transformation of ordination status and reverts to being a monk, and the tenth night then passes — what is the outcome?" Upāli replied: "It becomes a transgression requiring relinquishment."

"Venerable one, for the time being, if a monk perceives the tenth night as the tenth night, and dawn breaks — what is the outcome?" Upāli replied: "It becomes a transgression requiring relinquishment."

"If he perceives the tenth night as not the tenth night, what is the outcome?" Upāli replied: "There is no transgression."

"If it is not the tenth night and he perceives it as the tenth night, what is the outcome?" Upāli replied: "It becomes a serious offense."

"If it is not the tenth night and he perceives it as not the tenth night, what is the outcome?" Upāli replied: "There is no transgression."

"Venerable one, for the time being, if a monk finds a robe in a place where a formal act of deprivation has been imposed — could that robe also be subject to relinquishment?" Upāli replied: "It is so. The case is this: the robe is one requiring relinquishment, and the monk has neither relinquished it, nor separated himself from it, nor confessed the offense. In that situation, the Saṅgha has imposed the formal act of deprivation. After that deprivation has been imposed, the monk then finds another robe."

"Venerable one, for the time being, if one who has been given the training keeps an extra robe for more than ten nights — what is the outcome?" Upāli replied: "It becomes a transgression requiring relinquishment."

"Venerable one, for the time being, if a nun keeps an extra robe for more than ten nights — what is the outcome?" "It becomes a transgression requiring relinquishment."

"If a female probationer, a novice monk, or a novice nun keeps an extra robe for more than ten nights — what is the outcome?" "There is no transgression. As for those who previously had a transgression arise and so forth — it is a misdeed, just as before."

Summary verse:

Of the Saṅgha, and of the boundary, and of the zone,
Of pressing the boundary, and of not permitting, and of not demarcating,
Of not covering, of sets of four, and likewise of night —

The venerable Upāli asked the Blessed One: "Venerable one, the Blessed One has said that when a monk who has received robes extends them for hanging to dry, and if for even a single night he parts with any one of the three robes and separates it beyond the boundary zone — except by the Saṅgha's permission — it becomes a transgression requiring relinquishment. Now, is it the case that the Saṅgha may grant consecration of robes?" Upāli replied: "It is not permitted." "Venerable one, but if a particular circumstance arises, is it then permitted?" "It is permitted."

"Venerable one, for the time being, if a monk has had the Saṅgha consecrate his robes and then, in the absence of robes, separates himself beyond the boundary zone — is this called 'parted from his robes' or 'not parted from his robes'?" He declared: "It is called 'parted from his robes.'" "Should those robes then be relinquished?" "They should not. It is simply a misdeed that is to be confessed."

"Venerable one, if a monk places his robes on the ground and lets the dawn break in the sky above, or places his robes in the sky and lets the dawn break on the ground below — is this called 'parted from his robes' or 'not parted from his robes'?" He declared: "It is called 'parted from his robes.'"

"Venerable one, for the time being, if a monk places his robes within the boundary zone and lets the dawn break beyond the boundary zone, or places his robes beyond the boundary zone and lets the dawn break within the boundary zone — is this called 'parted from his robes' or 'not parted from his robes'?" He declared: "It is called 'parted from his robes.'"

"Venerable one, for the time being, if a monk is pressing upon a boundary, grasping a boundary, or standing at a boundary, and lets the dawn break with his robes placed at the boundary between — is this called 'parted from his robes' or 'not parted from his robes'?" He declared: "It is called 'not parted from his robes.'"

"Venerable one, when the monk is within the boundary zone and his robes are also within the boundary zone — can it come about that he becomes parted from those robes?" Upāli replied: "It can. If a demarcated boundary zone has not been given the vow of not-separation from robes."

"Venerable one, for the time being, in a place where the boundary zone has not been demarcated, up to what extent does one say that a monk is parted from his robes?" Upāli replied: "So far as the encircling wall of the monastery, or the latrine enclosure, or the gateway courtyard, or a small building, or the assembly courtyard, or what the monks make use of in common, or a fence, or a hedge, or a moat."

"Venerable one, when a monk is in a single dwelling that has one roof covering it, and his robes are also there — can it come about that he becomes parted from those robes?" Upāli replied: "It can. That is the case when the dwelling belongs to various non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), wanderers, brahmins, practitioners, wandering ascetics, or those who share inheritance and wealth."

"Venerable one, for the time being, if a monk has a perception that beyond the boundary zone is beyond the boundary zone, and lets the dawn break there — what is the outcome?" "It becomes a transgression requiring relinquishment." "If, while within the boundary zone, he has a perception that he is beyond the boundary zone — what is the outcome?" "It is a misdeed." "If, while beyond the boundary zone, he has a perception that he is within the boundary zone — what is the outcome?" "There is no transgression." "If, while within the boundary zone, he has a perception that he is within the boundary zone — what is the outcome?" "There is no transgression."

"Venerable one, suppose a monk has left his robe within the boundary zone, and then, beyond the boundary zone, the dawn breaks on him while he has become a nun — what is the outcome?" "It becomes a transgression requiring relinquishment." "Venerable one, suppose a nun has let the dawn break beyond the boundary zone while she has become a monk — what is the outcome?" "It becomes a transgression requiring relinquishment."

"Venerable one, before whom should a monk have his robe consecrated?" "A monk in the immediate vicinity should consecrate it before a monk." "Venerable one, before whom should a nun have her robe consecrated?" "A nun in the immediate vicinity should consecrate it before a nun; and the same applies to taking it up and making it fitting."

"Venerable one, suppose one who has received the training lets the dawn break beyond the boundary zone without a robe — what is the outcome?" "It becomes a transgression requiring relinquishment; and in the same way, for a nun it becomes a transgression requiring relinquishment in respect of the five robes. For a probationer nun, a novice monk, and a novice nun, there is no offence." As for previous transgressions and the like, the misdeed and what precedes it are as before.

Summary verse:
A disturbed mind, and exceeding the time,
likewise fitness and unfitness,
setting aside, the measure, the sets of four, and the dawn —

The venerable Upāli asked the Blessed One, the buddha: "Venerable one, the Blessed One has said that a monk who holds robes may, after stretching and drying out a completed robe set, take robes obtained out of season if he wishes. Having taken them, if he then rises, he should promptly make them into robes and keep them. If he does not rise, and if he has expectation of supplementary robes to complete what is lacking, that monk may set those robes aside for up to one month. The Blessed One has said that if he keeps them beyond that it becomes a transgression requiring relinquishment — what does this mean?"

"Venerable one, suppose a monk has just obtained a robe and his mind becomes disturbed — from what point does one count that month?" "From the point at which his own mind returns to itself."

"Venerable one, the Blessed One has said that a monk may set robes aside for up to one month — is there a case in which a monk could keep robes for his entire lifetime without incurring a transgression?" "Upāli, there is: if he has observed the time limit of one month."

"Venerable one, the Blessed One has said that a monk may set robes aside for up to one month — what kind of robes are meant?" "Whatever is among the seven kinds that I have permitted and not prohibited."

"Venerable one, the Blessed One has said that a monk may set robes aside for up to one month — is there a case in which a monk could keep robes for more than one month without incurring a transgression requiring relinquishment?" "Upāli, there is: when the fiber of the robe material is fitting but the weft thread is not fitting; or the fiber is not fitting but the weft thread is fitting; or both are not fitting. What is not fitting is: camel hair, cattle hair, deer wool, and goat wool mixed in, or material that has not been woven. A robe of that kind, even if kept for more than one month, becomes only a misdeed."

"Venerable one, the Blessed One has said that a monk may set those robes aside for up to one month — is there a case in which a monk could keep robes for six months without incurring a transgression?" "Upāli, there is —"

after having obtained that robe and thirty days having elapsed, and the Saṅgha having formally declared its forfeiture.

"Venerable one, regarding what the Blessed One has said — that when a monk lacks the complete set of three robes, he may keep a robe for up to one month — in that case, venerable one, if a monk who already has his three robes obtains a robe he has not yet accepted, may he likewise keep it for up to one month?" "He may not. Why is that? Upāli, when a monk has his three robes complete, any other robe he obtains — whether he has accepted it or not — is subject to the ten-day rule. If, from among the three robes that are not yet complete, he obtains something he has accepted as a robe, that too is subject to the ten-day rule. But if he obtains something he has not yet accepted, he may keep it for up to one month. If he keeps it beyond that, it becomes a transgression entailing forfeiture."

"Venerable one, if a monk who has obtained a robe, with one month remaining, perceives it as one month and then lets the dawn break on the last day — what becomes of it?" "It becomes a transgression entailing forfeiture." "If, with one month remaining, he perceives it as not one month — what becomes of it?" "There is no transgression." "If, with something that is not one month remaining, he perceives it as one month — what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If, with something that is not one month remaining, he perceives it as not one month — what becomes of it?" "There is no transgression."

"Venerable one, if a monk obtains a robe, and before the end of that one month the night falls and he disrobes to become a nun, and thereby the one month passes — what becomes of it?" "It becomes a transgression entailing forfeiture." "Venerable one, if a nun obtains a robe, and before the end of that one month the night falls and she takes ordination as a monk, and thereby the one month passes — what becomes of it?" "It becomes a transgression entailing forfeiture."

"Venerable one, if someone who has been given a training rule keeps a robe beyond one month — what becomes of it?" "It becomes a transgression entailing forfeiture." "Venerable one, if a nun keeps a robe beyond one month — what becomes of it?" "It becomes a transgression entailing forfeiture." "What becomes of it if a female trainee, a novice monk, or a novice nun keeps a robe for up to one month?" "There is no transgression." As for cases where a transgression had already arisen and so forth — those are misdeeds, as before.

A summary verse:

Through messenger, written message, hand signal,
through imputing something to another, or through an intermediary,
for an insane woman, one mentally disturbed,
one afflicted in her feelings, a mute woman, a foolish woman,
a laywoman and so forth —
this is the last training precept given.

The venerable Upāli addressed the Blessed One, the Buddha: "Venerable one, regarding what the Blessed One has said — that a monk who has a nun who is not his relative wash, dye, or card an old robe incurs a transgression entailing forfeiture — venerable one, if a monk through a messenger has a nun who is not his relative wash an old robe — what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "Likewise if through a written message, a hand signal, imputing something to another, or through an intermediary he has a nun who is not his relative wash an old robe — what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed."

"Venerable one, if a monk has an insane nun who is not his relative wash an old robe — what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "Likewise if a nun who is not his relative and is mentally disturbed, afflicted in her feelings, a mute woman, or a foolish woman washes an old robe — what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed."

"Venerable one, if a monk has a laywoman who is not his relative wash an old robe — what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "Likewise a nun who dwells separately,

a nun who has taken vows provisionally, a hermaphrodite, a nun who has been expelled from the Saṅgha, one who has killed her mother, one who has killed her father, one who has killed an arhat, a tīrthika, one who has joined the tīrthikas, one who dwells as a thief in the order, one who dwells separately, one who was in a state of discord from the beginning, or one who had previously incurred a downfall — if a monk has any of these women, none of whom is his relative, wash an old robe, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed."

"Venerable one, if a monk has a nun who is not his relative and who has caused a schism in the Saṅgha (saṃgha), or one who has drawn blood from a Tathāgata with malicious intent, or a nun who is not his relative and who has been given a training — if he has her wash an old robe, what becomes of it?" "It becomes a transgression entailing forfeiture."

A summary verse:

One to be finished, one already washed,
one belonging to the Saṅgha, and two named cases,
one unsuitable and one set aside,
one belonging to another, one being worn,
those arranged in groups of four,
and a nun — these are the final cases.

"Venerable one, if a monk has a nun who is not his relative wash an old robe that still needs to be finished, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If a monk has a nun who is not his relative wash an old robe that has already been washed, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed."

"Venerable one, if a monk has a nun who is not his relative wash an old robe belonging to the Saṅgha, what becomes of it?" "If it has been used by an individual until it has become soiled, it becomes a misdeed. However, if a furnishing item is given to be washed, there is no transgression."

"Venerable one, if a monk gives an old robe to a nun who is not his relative to wash and she then becomes ordained as a nun, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "Venerable one, if a nun gives an old robe to a monk who is not her relative to wash and he then becomes ordained as a monk, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed."

"Venerable one, if a monk gives an old robe to a nun who is not his relative to wash, and while it is being washed that nun becomes ordained as a monk and then washes it — what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed. And likewise the same applies to a nun."

"Venerable one, are there cases in which a monk gives an old robe to a nun who is not his relative to wash and yet it does not become a transgression entailing forfeiture?" "There are such proximate cases. These concern the fabric of that robe: the fiber may be suitable but the material may not be suitable; or the material may be suitable but the fiber may not be suitable; or both may not be suitable. What is unsuitable is: mixed with felt, with cattle hair, with deer wool, with goat wool, or not dyed. If such a robe is given to be washed, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed."

"Venerable one, if a monk gives a nun who is not his relative an old robe to wash that he has already set aside for abandonment, what becomes of it?" "If what is given to be sent is one that has not yet been formally abandoned, it becomes a transgression entailing forfeiture. However, if what is sent is one that has been formally abandoned, it is a misdeed."

"Venerable one, if a monk has a nun who is not his relative wash an old robe belonging to someone else, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed."

"Venerable one, are there cases in which a monk would have a suitable robe washed and yet a transgression entailing entry into a dwelling would arise?" "There are such proximate cases. If a monk who has entered a household has a robe that has been defiled by excrement, urine, mud, mire, or other impurities, and has that robe given to a nun who is not his relative to be washed — what becomes of it?" "It becomes a transgression entailing forfeiture."

"If a monk has made preparations to give it to one not yet fully ordained, and then carries through with treating her as one not fully ordained, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed."

"If he made preparations treating her as one not yet fully ordained, and then treats her as one fully ordained in the act itself, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed."

"If he made preparations treating her as one not yet fully ordained, and then treats her as one fully ordained after she has become fully ordained, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed."

"If he made preparations treating her as one fully ordained in the act itself, and then treats her as one fully ordained in the act itself, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed."

"If he made preparations treating her as one fully ordained in the act itself, and then treats her as one fully ordained after she has become fully ordained, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed."

"If he made preparations after she was fully ordained, and then treats her as fully ordained, what becomes of it?" "It becomes a transgression entailing forfeiture."

"If he made preparations after she was fully ordained, and then treats her as one not yet fully ordained, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed."

"Venerable sir, if a monk, perceiving a nun who is not his relative and who is fully ordained to be fully ordained, has her wash an old robe, what becomes of it?" "It becomes a transgression entailing forfeiture."

"If he perceives a nun who is not his relative and who is fully ordained to be not yet fully ordained, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed."

"If he perceives one who is not a relative and who is fully ordained to be fully ordained, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed."

"If he perceives one who is not a relative and who is not yet fully ordained to be fully ordained, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed."

"Venerable sir, if a monk, perceiving a nun who is not his relative to be not his relative, has her wash an old robe, what becomes of it?" "It becomes a transgression entailing forfeiture."

"If he perceives a nun who is not his relative to be his relative, what becomes of it?" "There is no transgression."

"If he perceives a relative to be not his relative, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed."

"If a monk perceives a nun who is his relative to be his relative, what becomes of it?" "There is no transgression."

"Venerable sir, the Blessed One has declared that whichever monk has an old robe washed by a nun who is not his relative incurs a transgression entailing forfeiture. What, then, is the case regarding having it washed by a female probationer, a novice nun, a laywoman, a female tīrthika adherent, a female ascetic, a brahmin woman, a wandering female ascetic, or a female mendicant?" "It is a misdeed."

"Likewise, if he has it washed by one dwelling in another destiny, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed."

"If a nun has a robe washed by a monk, a male novice, a male tīrthika adherent, a male ascetic, a brahmin, a wandering male ascetic, a male mendicant, or one dwelling in another destiny, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed."

"If she has it washed by a layman or a laywoman, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed."

"If she has it washed by a female probationer, a male novice, or a novice nun, it is a misdeed." As for prior transgressions and so forth, they are misdeeds, just as before.

Likewise, the full exposition is also to be given for rubbing and scrubbing.

Summary verse:

Through messenger, letter, hand-signal,
through others as go-betweens for requests,
the mad woman, the mentally disturbed,
the one who cannot feel, the simpleton,
the one asleep, the one vomiting, the thoroughly intoxicated,
and likewise one in meditative absorption, one in transit,
one who accepts and desires, and so on —
these mark the final precepts that have been given.

The venerable Nyeba Khor asked the Blessed One, the Buddha: "Venerable sir, the Blessed One has declared that whichever monk receives a robe from a nun who is not his relative — excepting exchange — incurs a transgression entailing forfeiture. What of this? If a monk solicits a robe through a messenger from a nun who is not his relative, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed."

"If he obtains the robe, what becomes of it?" "It remains a misdeed only."

Likewise, if a monk solicits a robe from a nun who is not his relative by means of a written message, by hand signals, or by having another person make the request on his behalf — what becomes of it? It is a misdeed.

"Venerable sir, if a monk solicits a robe from a nun who is not his relative and who is mad — what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If he obtains the robe, what becomes of it?" "It remains a misdeed only."

Likewise, the same applies whether the nun is mentally disturbed, overcome by feeling, foolish, mute, asleep, incapacitated by work, thoroughly intoxicated, in meditative absorption, in transit, dwelling elsewhere, one who accepts and desires — and so on through the case of a nun who has been expelled from the Saṅgha — all the way up through the full elaboration ending with the case of one who is adverse from the outset: if a monk solicits a robe from such a nun, what becomes of it? It is a misdeed. If he obtains the robe, what becomes of it? It remains a misdeed only.

"Venerable sir, if a monk solicits a robe from a nun who is not his relative but who is causing a schism in the Saṅgha, or from a nun who is not his relative and who has, with malicious intent, drawn blood from a Tathāgata, or from one who has been suspended from the training — what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If he obtains the robe, what becomes of it?" "It incurs a transgression entailing forfeiture."

Summary verse:

The group of four concerning what is improper,
the second group, and likewise the marks,
monk and nun alike —
all have been rightly set forth in the assembly.

"Venerable sir, are there cases in which a monk might accept a robe from a nun who is not his relative without it incurring a transgression entailing forfeiture?" "There are, and they are as follows. A robe may be suitable in its fiber yet unsuitable in its thread; suitable in its thread yet unsuitable in its fiber; or unsuitable in both fiber and thread. What is unsuitable is this: fiber mixed with the wool of a camel, ox-hair, deer wool, or goat wool; or that which has not been woven. If a robe of such a kind is accepted, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed."

"Venerable sir, if a monk perceives a nun who is his relative as his relative, what becomes of it?" "There is no transgression." "If he perceives a nun who is not his relative as his relative, what becomes of it?" "There is no transgression." "If he perceives a nun who is his relative as not his relative, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If he perceives a nun who is his relative as his relative, what becomes of it?" "There is no transgression."

"Venerable sir, if a monk accepts a robe from a nun who is not his relative but who is fully ordained, perceiving her as fully ordained — what becomes of it?" "It incurs a transgression entailing forfeiture." "If he perceives one who is not a relative and is fully ordained as not fully ordained — what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If he perceives one who is not a relative and is not fully ordained as not fully ordained — what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If he perceives one who is not a relative and is not fully ordained as fully ordained — what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed."

"Venerable sir, if one who is not fully ordained accepts a robe from a nun who is not his relative, and that acceptance is completed while he is still not fully ordained — what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If the acceptance was initiated while he was not fully ordained but completed once he has become fully ordained — what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If it was accepted while not fully ordained and completed after full ordination — what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If it was accepted in the course of becoming fully ordained and completed in the course of becoming fully ordained — what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If it was accepted in the course of becoming fully ordained and completed after full ordination — what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If it was accepted after full ordination and completed after full ordination — what becomes of it?" "It incurs a transgression entailing forfeiture." "If it was accepted by one who is fully ordained but completed while no longer fully ordained — what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed."

"Venerable one, suppose a nun who is not a relative receives a robe of Dharma from another nun, and in the course of that, her status as a nun changes — if she is then a nun when it is received, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "Venerable one, suppose a monk who is not a relative of a nun receives a robe of Dharma from her, and in the course of that, the nun's status changes — if it was accepted while he was a monk, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "Venerable one, suppose a nun who is not a relative of a monk receives a robe of Dharma from that monk, and his status changes — if it was accepted while she was a nun, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "Venerable one, suppose a nun who is not a relative of a monk receives a robe of Dharma from that monk, and his status changes — if she is then a nun when it is received, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed."

"Venerable one, if one who has received the training accepts a robe of Dharma from a nun who is not a relative, what becomes of it?" "It incurs a transgression entailing forfeiture."

"Venerable one, as for what the Blessed One declared — 'If a monk accepts a robe of Dharma from a nun who is not a relative, except in exchange, it incurs a transgression entailing forfeiture' — when it comes to accepting from a female trainee or a novice nun, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If a fully ordained nun accepts from a monk or a novice monk, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "Likewise, if she accepts from a female trainee or a novice nun, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If a novice monk accepts a robe of Dharma from a fully ordained nun, a female trainee, or a novice nun, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." As for previous cases involving transgressions and so forth, it is a misdeed, as before.

By messenger, written note, hand-signal,

one who is mad, one whose mind is disturbed,
