Venerable Nyeba Khor then addressed the Buddha, the Blessed One: "Venerable one, what the Blessed One declared — 'If a monk goes to the home of a male householder or of a female householder's wife who is not a relative and solicits a robe of Dharma, except at the proper time, it incurs a transgression entailing forfeiture' — and 'the proper time' is this: when a monk's robe is snatched away, damaged, burned, carried off, or lost, that is the proper time — as for that:

"Venerable one, suppose a monk solicits a robe of Dharma by means of a messenger from a male householder or a female householder's wife who is not a relative — what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If the robe is obtained, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed alone." "Likewise, if it is done by written note, by hand-signal, by imputing it through another, or through an intermediary — what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed."

"Venerable one, suppose a monk solicits a robe of Dharma from a madwoman who is a householder's wife or female companion — what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If the robe is obtained, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed alone."

"Likewise, if he solicits from a householder's wife or female companion who is not a relative and who is mentally disturbed, overcome by pain, deaf, dull-witted, asleep, occupied with work, thoroughly intoxicated, in meditative absorption, dwelling elsewhere — what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If the robe is obtained, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed alone."

"As for soliciting from those outside this — other non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), wandering ascetics, brahmins, those who practice austerities, wanderers — who are not relatives — what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If the robe is obtained, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed alone."

Summary verse:
The four groups of those who are unsuitable,
two together with status-change,
likewise a eunuch and a hermaphrodite,
a female hermaphrodite and a fully ordained nun.

"Venerable one, suppose a monk, a male householder or —"

"Venerable one, suppose a monk solicits a robe from a male householder's attendant who is not a relative — could it be that obtaining it would not constitute a transgression involving forfeiture?" "There is a related case. It is as follows: the robe may be suitable in respect of its thread while being unsuitable in respect of its material; or suitable in respect of its material while being unsuitable in respect of its thread; or unsuitable in both respects. What is unsuitable is this: if the robe is woven from a mixture of mare's wool, ox hair, deer wool, and goat wool, or if it has not been woven on a loom — if one solicits a robe of such a kind, it is a misdeed. If the robe is obtained, it is a misdeed alone."

"Venerable one, if a monk, perceiving a male householder or a male householder's attendant who is not a relative as not being a relative, solicits a robe — what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If the robe is obtained, what becomes of it?" "It becomes a transgression involving forfeiture." "If one perceives someone who is not a relative as a relative, what becomes of it?" "There is no transgression." "If one perceives a relative as not being a relative, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If the robe is obtained, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed alone." "If one perceives a relative as a relative, what becomes of it?" "There is no transgression."

"Venerable one, if a monk solicits a robe from a human male householder or a male householder's attendant, perceiving them to be human — what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If the robe is obtained, what becomes of it?" "It becomes a transgression involving forfeiture." "If one perceives a human as not being human, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If the robe is obtained, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed alone." "If one perceives a non-human as human, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If the robe is obtained, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed alone."

"If the soliciting was done while one was not fully ordained and the obtaining also occurs while not fully ordained, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If the soliciting was done while not fully ordained and the obtaining occurs at the moment of full ordination, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If the soliciting was done while not fully ordained and the obtaining occurs after full ordination, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If the soliciting was done at the moment of full ordination and the obtaining also occurs at the moment of full ordination, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If the soliciting was done at the moment of full ordination and the obtaining occurs after full ordination, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If the soliciting was done after full ordination and the obtaining occurs while fully ordained, what becomes of it?" "It becomes a transgression involving forfeiture." "If the soliciting was done after full ordination and the obtaining occurs while not fully ordained, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed."

"Venerable one, if a monk solicits a robe from a male householder or a male householder's attendant who is not a relative, and in the course of that solicitation his status changes — having become a fully ordained nun and the robe is then obtained — what becomes of it?" "It becomes a transgression involving forfeiture." "Venerable one, if a fully ordained nun solicits a robe from a male householder or a male householder's attendant who is not a relative, and in the course of that solicitation her status changes — having become a monk and the robe is then obtained — what becomes of it?" "It becomes a transgression involving forfeiture."

"Venerable one, if a monk solicits a robe from a male householder who is not his relative, and in the course of that solicitation the householder's status changes — having become a woman and the robe is then received from her — what becomes of it?" "It becomes a transgression involving forfeiture." "Venerable one, if a monk solicits a robe from the attendant of a male householder, that attendant not being his relative, and in the course of that solicitation the attendant's status changes — having become a man and the robe is then received from him — what becomes of it?" "It becomes a transgression involving forfeiture." The same is to be said also for a fully ordained nun.

The Exalted Vinaya Text, fifth fascicle.

"Venerable one, if a monk solicits a robe from one who is not a eunuch — what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If the robe is obtained, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed alone." Likewise, beginning from a hermaphrodite and a female hermaphrodite, through to a monk and a novice — if the robe is obtained in each such case, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If the robe is obtained, what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed alone."

"Venerable one, if a fully ordained nun solicits a robe from one who is not a relative of a householder or of a householder's wife — what becomes of it?" "It is a misdeed." "If the robe is obtained, what becomes of it?" "It becomes a transgression involving forfeiture." The same is to be said also for the training given to a fully ordained nun. Beginning from a female probationer, a novice, a female novice, and those in whom a prior transgression has arisen, and so forth — in each case it is a misdeed; this is to be stated in full.

The venerable Anuruddha (Upananda) asked the Blessed One, the Buddha: "Venerable one, the Blessed One has declared that if a monk's robe is snatched, or damaged, or burned, or lost, or carried off, he may go to one who is not a relative — a householder or a householder's wife — and request a robe. And if there is among them a brahmin or a faithful householder who wishes to give many robes in good time, that monk may accept from him at most an upper garment and a lower garment together. If he accepts more than that, it becomes a transgression involving forfeiture. You have said this. With respect to the earlier rule on forfeiture concerning soliciting, which applies to an upper garment and a lower garment together — that case is the same. What then is the difference? A set of upper and lower garments is of two kinds: a householder's upper and lower garments, and a monk's upper and lower garments. If a monk accepts a householder's set of upper and lower garments and then solicits beyond what he has already received as a householder's set, that act of soliciting is a misdeed. If he obtains them, it becomes a transgression involving forfeiture. If a monk accepts a monk's set of upper and lower garments and solicits beyond what he has already received as a monk's set, that act of soliciting is a misdeed. If he obtains them, it becomes a transgression involving forfeiture. If a monk accepts a householder's set of upper and lower garments and what he has obtained from the householder's set turns out to be insufficient, he should not ask for more. If there is a surplus, he should not give it away. If a monk accepts more than the householder's set of upper and lower garments, it becomes a misdeed. If a monk accepts a monk's set of upper and lower garments and what he has obtained from the monk's set turns out to be insufficient, he may request more. If there is a surplus, he may give it away. If a monk does not give away what exceeds the set of upper and lower garments, it becomes a misdeed."

The remainder of the text is to be stated in the same way as the earlier training rule was stated for this training rule likewise.

Summary verse:

Through messenger, letter, gesture and sign,
attributing offense to another through an intermediary,
the mad, and those whose minds are disturbed,
those who are deaf, those who are dull-witted,
those asleep, those intoxicated, those utterly drunk,
those in meditative absorption, and those who are non-Buddhists (tīrthikas).

The venerable Anuruddha asked the Blessed One, the Buddha: "Venerable one, the Blessed One has declared that a householder or a householder's wife who is not a relative, acting on behalf of a monk, sets aside funds for a robe, thinking: 'With these funds for a robe I shall purchase such-and-such a robe, and when the monk known by such-and-such a name arrives, I shall present him with a suitable robe in good time.' Now in that case, if the monk, not having been previously instructed by that donor, becomes aware of certain intentions and, out of desire for something better, goes to the householder or —

— the householder's wife, who is not a relative, and says to her: "Venerable one, certain funds for a robe have been set aside on my behalf. With those funds for a robe, please purchase such-and-such a robe for this venerable one and present me with a suitable robe in good time." If the robe is then completed, it constitutes a transgression requiring forfeiture, as the Blessed One has declared.

Now, venerable one, if a monk solicits a robe from a householder or a householder's wife who is not a relative, having become aware of her intentions without having been previously instructed — what is the outcome?

It is an offence.

And if the robe is obtained?

An offence alone.

And if he does so in the same way by letter, by gesture, by hint to another, or through an intermediary — what is the outcome?

An offence.

Venerable one, what if a monk solicits a robe from a householder or a householder's wife who is not a relative — one who is mentally disturbed — having become aware of her intentions?

It is an offence.

And if the robe is obtained?

An offence alone.

And likewise from one whose mind is agitated, one who is distressed by feeling, one who is mute, one who is foolish, one who has fallen asleep, one who is occupied with work, one who is thoroughly intoxicated, one who has entered into meditative absorption — and further, from other outsiders: non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), wandering ascetics, brahmins, wandering practitioners, and roaming mendicants — if a monk solicits a robe having become aware of their intentions, what is the outcome?

It is an offence.

And if the robe is obtained?

An offence alone.

A summary verse:

Those who are unsuitable — four pairs of two,
and likewise by name, and also a panḍaka,
a eunuch-like woman, and a paṇḍaka himself,
and a monk.

Venerable one, is there any case in which a monk solicits a robe from a householder or a householder's wife who is not a relative, having become aware of her intentions and having obtained it, and yet does not incur a transgression requiring forfeiture?

There is a neighbouring case: if the robe's thread is unsuitable while the weave is suitable, or the thread is suitable while the weave is unsuitable, or both are unsuitable — unsuitable meaning mixed with hemp fibre, ox hair, deer wool, or goat wool, or that the cloth has not been woven. If a monk solicits such a robe and obtains it having become aware of these intentions, what is the outcome?

It is an offence.

Venerable one, if a monk solicits a robe from a householder or a householder's wife who is not a relative, perceiving her with the perception of a non-relative, having become aware of her intentions — what is the outcome?

It is an offence.

And if the robe is obtained?

It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture.

And if he perceives one who is not a relative as a relative, what is the outcome?

No transgression.

If he perceives a relative as a non-relative, what is the outcome?

An offence.

If he perceives a relative as a relative, what is the outcome?

No transgression.

Venerable one, if a nun solicits a robe from a householder or a householder's wife who is not a relative, perceiving her as a person, having become aware of her intentions — what is the outcome?

It is an offence.

And if the robe is obtained?

It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture.

If she perceives a person as a non-person, what is the outcome?

An offence.

If she perceives a non-person as a person, what is the outcome?

An offence.

If she perceives a non-person as a non-person, what is the outcome?

An offence.

Venerable one, if a monk solicits and obtains a robe having become aware of the intentions of one who has not received full ordination, and if it is completed through one who has not received full ordination — what is the outcome?

It is an offence.

If he is aware that she has received full ordination and solicits it, and it is completed through one who has received full ordination — what is the outcome?

It becomes an offence.

If he perceives one who has not received full ordination as having received full ordination, becomes aware of her intentions, solicits, and it is completed through one who has received full ordination — what is the outcome?

An offence.

If he perceives one who has received full ordination as having received full ordination, becomes aware of her intentions, solicits, and it is completed through one who has received full ordination — what is the outcome?

An offence.

If he perceives one who has received full ordination as having received full ordination, becomes aware of her intentions, solicits, and it is completed through one who has not received full ordination — what is the outcome?

An offence.

If he perceives one who has fully received ordination as such, becomes aware of her intentions, solicits, and it is completed through one who has fully received ordination — what is the outcome?

It becomes an offence entailing forfeiture.

If he perceives one who has fully received ordination as such, becomes aware of her intentions, solicits, and it is completed through one who has not received full ordination — what is the outcome?

An offence.

Venerable one, if a monk becomes aware of the intentions of a layman or of one who is not a close relative of a layman's household, and having done so solicits from a monk with a mark of distinction, and it is completed through a nun — what is the outcome?

It becomes an offence entailing forfeiture.

Venerable one, if a nun becomes aware of the intentions of a layman or of one who is not a close relative of a layman's household, and having done so solicits from a novice with a mark of distinction, and obtains it through a monk — what is the outcome?

It becomes an offence entailing forfeiture.

Venerable one, if a monk becomes aware of the intentions of a layman or of one who is not a close relative of a layman's household, and having done so solicits, and that layman's wife appears with a mark of distinction and it is obtained through a woman — what is the outcome?

It becomes an offence entailing forfeiture.

Venerable one, if a monk becomes aware of the intentions of a layman or of one who is not a close relative of a layman's household, and having done so solicits, and a male attendant of that layman's household appears with a mark of distinction and it is obtained through a man — what is the outcome?

It becomes an offence entailing forfeiture.

The same is to be applied in the case of a nun as well.

Venerable one, if a monk becomes aware of the intentions of a layman's wife and solicits a robe — what is the outcome?

An offence.

If he obtains the robe — what is the outcome?

An offence.

Likewise in the case of a paṇḍaka and a female paṇḍaka, it is equally an offence.

Venerable one, if a monk, having become aware of the intentions, obtains other requisites apart from robes — what is the outcome?

An offence.

If he obtains them — what is the outcome?

An offence alone.

Venerable one, if a monk, having become aware of the intentions, solicits a robe on behalf of a monk, or on behalf of a nun, a female probationer, a novice monk, or a novice nun — what is the outcome?

An offence.

If the robe is obtained — what is the outcome?

An offence alone.

Just as it becomes an offence entailing forfeiture for a monk to solicit having become aware of the intentions, so too it becomes an offence entailing forfeiture for one who has given the training, and for a nun as well. In the case of a female probationer, a novice monk, a novice nun, and those for whom a previous transgression has arisen and so forth, it is an offence — and this is to be stated in full.

The venerable Upali then put a question to the Buddha, the Blessed One: Venerable one, when the Blessed One set out, on behalf of a monk, separate cloth-prices from a layman or from a layman's wife who is not a relative, with the thought, "We two have set aside these separate cloth-prices and with them we will each separately buy such and such a robe and, when the monk named such-and-such arrives, we two will each separately, at the right time, present him with a suitable robe" — if at that point, before any arrangement had been made with that monk, the monk becomes aware of some intention, and out of a desire for something fine approaches the layman

or the layman's wife who is not a relative, and says the following: "Venerable ones have set aside, on my behalf, separate cloth-prices for a robe. With those separate cloth-prices, let the venerable ones each separately buy such and such a robe, and then together present me with a single suitable robe at the right time" — then, if the robe is completed, it becomes an offence entailing relinquishment. Such was the Blessed One's declaration. As for the remainder of the rule, this training rule is to be set out in full, just as it was in the preceding training rule.

Summary verse:

Messenger, letter, hand-signal,
attributing to another and conveying,
the mad one, the disturbed one,
the one in pain, the simpleton,

the sleeping, the delirious, the thoroughly drunk,
the one in meditative absorption, and the outsider.

Venerable Upāli (the close attendant) then addressed the blessed Buddha: Venerable one, when the Blessed One furthermore sends robe-cloth prices in the hands of a messenger on behalf of a monk — whether from a king, a great minister, a brahmin, a householder, a villager, a countryman, a wealthy person, or a caravan-leader — and that messenger, carrying those robe-cloth prices, goes to wherever that monk is and says to him: "May the noble one please be informed: a king, a great minister, a brahmin, a householder, a villager, a countryman, a wealthy person, a merchant, or a caravan-leader sends you these robe-cloth prices out of kindness for the noble one — please accept them." If the monk then says to that messenger: "Friend, messenger, monks are not permitted to accept robe-cloth prices. If we find a suitable robe at the right time, we will accept it" — if the messenger then says: "Does the noble one have anyone who attends to him, who carries out his personal service?" — then the monk, desiring a robe, should point out either an ānanda-pillar keeper or a lay male attendant, saying: "This one is the attendant of monks; this one carries out the personal service of monks."

The messenger then takes those robe-cloth prices, goes to wherever that attendant is, and says to the attendant: "Friend, attendant, please take note. With these robe-cloth prices of yours, buy such and such a robe; and when the monk named such-and-such arrives, present him with a suitable robe at the right time." Having given full and precise instructions and clear indication to the attendant, the messenger then goes to wherever the monk is and says to him: "The attendant of the noble one whom the noble one designated as one who carries out his service — I have given him full indication. Go to him, and he will present you with a suitable robe at the right time." If then the monk, desiring a robe, goes to the attendant and says, "Friend, attendant, I want a robe. Friend, attendant, I want a robe" — he should prompt and remind him two or three times. If, after prompting and reminding him two or three times, the robe is obtained, that is well and good. If it is not obtained, he should remain silent in one spot without saying anything further, up to four, five, or six times.

If the robe is obtained while remaining silent in one spot without saying anything further up to four, five, or six times, that is well and good. If it is not obtained, thereafter one may seek to procure the robe through other means, and if the robe is obtained in that way, it becomes a transgression entailing relinquishment. If it is not obtained, one should go to wherever the funds for the robe have come from. Alternatively, if venerable ones have sent robe funds via a trusted messenger on behalf of some particular monk, and nothing at all has been accomplished for that monk, they should be informed of this. The venerable ones should send word saying, "Do not let your own wealth be wasted." That is the procedure in such a case.

What then, Venerable Ones, is the procedure that has been declared? If a monk, in order to procure a robe, prompts a messenger up to four, five, or six times, and the robe is then obtained, what is the result? It is a transgression. Likewise, if for the sake of procuring a robe a monk prompts a messenger up to four, five, or six times by means of a written message, a hand signal, hinting to another, or sending a go-between, and the robe is obtained, what is the result? It is a transgression.

Venerable Ones, if a monk, in order to procure a robe, prompts a madman up to four, five, or six times and the robe is obtained, what is the result? It is a transgression. Likewise, if one prompts a mentally disturbed person, one afflicted by painful sensations, a simpleton, or a deaf-mute up to four, five, or six times and the robe is obtained, what is the result? It is a transgression.

Venerable Ones, if a monk, in order to procure a robe, prompts someone who is lying asleep up to four, five, or six times and the robe is obtained, what is the result? It is a transgression. Likewise, if for the sake of procuring a robe one prompts someone who is busy with work, someone thoroughly intoxicated, someone in meditative absorption, someone residing in another destination, or someone from outside this community — a non-Buddhist adherent (tīrthika), an ascetic, a brahmin, a wandering mendicant, or a wandering beggar — up to four, five, or six times, and the robe is obtained, what is the result? It is a transgression.

Summary verse:
The unsuitable ones — four categories —
and likewise the matter of the marks,
the intermediate-born of the female kind,
and the female intermediate-born, last.

Venerable Ones, is there a case in which a monk, seeking to procure a robe, prompts up to four, five, or six times, and even if the robe is obtained it does not become a transgression entailing relinquishment? There is a related case: namely, when the material of the robe is unsuitable — the cause is unsuitable, the constituent is unsuitable, the cause is suitable but the constituent is unsuitable, or both are unsuitable. What is meant by "unsuitable"? It means mixed with camel hair, ox hair, wild-animal wool, or goat wool, or left unspun. If one prompts up to four, five, or six times for a robe of such material, and the robe is obtained, what is the result? It is a transgression.

Venerable Ones, if a monk, in order to procure a robe, prompts a messenger up to four, five, or six times while perceiving a person as a person and the robe is obtained, what is the result? It becomes a transgression entailing relinquishment. If one perceives a person as a non-person, what is the result? It is a transgression. If one perceives a non-person as a person, what is the result? It is a transgression. If one perceives a non-person as a non-person, what is the result? It is a transgression.

Venerable Ones, if one prompts someone who has not received full ordination and what is obtained is obtained by one who has not received full ordination, what is the result? It is a transgression. If one prompts someone who has not received full ordination and what is obtained is obtained by one who has received full ordination, what is the result? It is a transgression.

If one prompts someone who has not received full ordination and what is obtained is obtained by one who has received full ordination, what is the result? It is a transgression. If one prompts someone who has received full ordination and what is obtained is obtained by one who has received full ordination, what is the result? It is a transgression. If one prompts someone who has received full ordination and what is obtained is obtained by one who has not received full ordination, what is the result? It is a transgression. If one prompts someone who has received full ordination and what is obtained is obtained by one who has received full ordination, what is the result? It is a downfall requiring expiation. If one prompts someone who has received full ordination and what is obtained is obtained by one who has not received full ordination, what is the result? It is a transgression.

Venerable Ones, if a monk prompts someone under a nun four, five, or six times, and through that a mark arises and she becomes a nun, and something is obtained thereby, what is the result? It is a downfall requiring expiation. Venerable Ones, if a monk prompts someone under a nun four, five, or six times, and through that a mark arises and she becomes a monk, and something is obtained thereby, what is the result? It is a downfall requiring expiation. Venerable Ones, if a monk, for the sake of procuring monastic robes, prompts a lay patron four, five, or six times, and through that the mark of that lay patron arises and he becomes a woman, and something is taken, what is the result? It is a downfall requiring expiation. Venerable Ones, if a monk, for the sake of procuring monastic robes, at the very moment of a lay patron's making the offering prompts him four, five, or six times, and through that a mark arises and the one who made the offering becomes a man, and something is taken, what is the result? It is a downfall requiring expiation. The same is to be applied in the case of a nun as well.

Venerable Ones, if a monk, for the sake of procuring monastic robes, prompts a cook who is making an offering four, five, or six times, and those robes are obtained, what is the result? It is a transgression. Similarly, what is the result in the case of a eunuch or a female eunuch? It is a transgression.

Venerable Ones, if a monk, for the sake of a monk's own benefit, and for the benefit of a nun, a female novice-trainee, a novice monk, or a novice nun, prompts someone four, five, or six times to procure monastic robes and those robes are obtained, what is the result? It is a transgression. Venerable Ones, if a monk, for the sake of articles other than monastic robes, prompts someone four, five, or six times, what is the result? It is a transgression. If something is obtained, what is the result? It is only a transgression.

Venerable Ones, if one who has given training prompts someone four, five, or six times for the sake of procuring monastic robes and those robes are obtained, what is the result? It is a downfall requiring expiation. If a nun prompts someone four, five, or six times for the sake of procuring monastic robes and those robes are obtained, what is the result? It is a downfall requiring expiation. In the case of a female novice-trainee, a novice monk, a novice nun, and those in whom a downfall has previously arisen, and so forth, it is said to be a transgression.

Summary verse:

Another's remainder, and
the householder himself, and the non-Buddhist going forth,
the ineligible one, and marks,
the group of four, and the Sugata's group of seven.

The venerable Upāli asked the Blessed One, the buddha: "Venerable One, the Blessed One has said that whatever monk uses a woolen mat stuffed with black antelope hair incurs a downfall requiring expiation — what exactly is that?" Venerable Ones, if a monk allows another to make a mat stuffed with black antelope hair, what is the result? It is a transgression. If he allows it to be made for others, what is the result? It is a transgression. Venerable Ones, if a monk makes use of the remainder of a mat stuffed with black antelope hair made by another, what is the result? It is a transgression.

If he allows a layperson in his presence to make it, what is the result? It is a transgression requiring expiation. If he allows a female probationer, a novice, a female novice, one who has been given the training rules, one who is in an incompatible situation from the outset, one who has previously incurred a downfall, one who is dwelling as a thief, one who is dwelling separately, or one who ought not to be dwelling there — up to and including one who has made a confession — to make it, what is the result? It is a transgression requiring expiation.

Venerable Ones, if a monk allows those outside this teaching — other non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), wandering ascetics, brahmins, practitioners, and wanderers — to make a mat stuffed with black antelope hair, what is the result? It is a transgression requiring expiation. Venerable Ones, if a monk allows those dwelling in other destinies to make a mat stuffed with black antelope hair, what is the result? It is a wrongdoing.

Venerable Ones, are there cases in which a monk makes a mat stuffed with black antelope hair yet does not incur a transgression requiring expiation? There are borderline cases. If the mat's wool content is suitable but the material is not permissible, or the material is permissible but the wool content is not suitable, or both are not suitable — in those cases it does not incur expiation. If what is impermissible is mixed with goat wool, cow hair, or wild animal wool and made into a mat, what is the result? It is a wrongdoing.

Venerable Ones, if a monk allows a mat to be made from wool, or hemp, or cotton-wool, or healing-wool mixed together, what is the result? It is a wrongdoing. Venerable Ones, if a monk allows a mat to be made from fine black antelope hair or from black antelope hair mixed with Kashmiri silk, what is the result? It is a wrongdoing. If he allows it to be made from unspun fiber, what is the result? It is a wrongdoing.

Venerable Ones, if a monk, intending to commission a mat stuffed with black antelope hair, changes his mind and has it made properly as a nun's mat, what is the result? It is a transgression requiring expiation. Venerable Ones, if a nun, intending to commission a mat stuffed with black antelope hair, changes her mind and has it made properly as a monk's mat, what is the result? It is a transgression requiring expiation. Venerable Ones, if a nun allows something that is not a mat stuffed with black antelope hair to be made while perceiving it as black antelope hair, what is the result? It is a transgression requiring expiation. If she perceives black antelope hair as not being black antelope hair and allows it to be made, what is the result? There is no transgression. If she perceives what is not black antelope hair as black antelope hair and allows it to be made, what is the result? It is a wrongdoing. If she perceives what is not black antelope hair as not being black antelope hair and allows it to be made, what is the result? There is no transgression.

Venerable Ones, if a monk allows a mat to be made to the measure of the Sugata's Dharma robe, what is the result? It is a wrongdoing. Venerable Ones, what are the prescribed measurements of a mat? There are borderline cases — mats come in three measurements: large, medium, and small. The measurement of a large mat is two and a half cubits. The measurement of a small mat is one and a half cubits. Between these is the medium. Venerable Ones, if a monk makes preparations to make a mat before having received full ordination, and then makes it properly before having received full ordination, what is the result? It is a wrongdoing. If he makes preparations before having received full ordination and then makes it properly at the moment of receiving full ordination, what is the result? It is a wrongdoing. If he makes preparations before having received full ordination and then makes it properly after having received full ordination, what is the result? It is a wrongdoing. If he makes preparations at the moment of receiving full ordination and then makes it properly at the moment of receiving full ordination, what is the result? It is a wrongdoing. If he makes preparations at the moment of receiving full ordination and then makes it properly after having received full ordination, what is the result?

It is a wrongdoing. If he makes preparations after having received full ordination and then makes it properly after having received full ordination, what is the result? It is a transgression requiring abandonment. If he makes preparations after having received full ordination and then makes it properly before having received full ordination, what is the result? It is a wrongdoing.

Summary verse:
Through a messenger, a letter, a hand-signal,
through attributing a name to another, through an intermediary,
through a madman, through one whose mind is disturbed,
through one overcome by pain, and through a fool.

Now then: if a monk instructs a messenger to make a sitting mat of sheep's black wool alone, what is the result? It is a wrongdoing. Likewise, through a letter, a hand-signal, attributing a name to another, through an intermediary, through a madman, through one whose mind is disturbed, through one incapacitated by pain, through someone overcome by sensation, and through a fool — what is the result? It is a wrongdoing. Just as when a monk has a sitting mat of sheep's wool made it becomes a transgression requiring abandonment, so too it becomes a transgression requiring abandonment when one gives up the training, and likewise for a fully ordained nun. For a novice monk in training, a novice monk, a novice nun, and for one in whom a transgression has already arisen and so forth, it is a wrongdoing.

The venerable Upāli asked the Blessed One, the Buddha: Venerable one, the Blessed One has declared that if a monk makes a sitting mat from nothing but black sheep's wool, it becomes a transgression requiring abandonment — what is the meaning of that? The remaining text is to be elaborated in full for the transgression requiring abandonment for black wool alone, just as it was for the transgression requiring abandonment for sheep's wool. As for what distinguishes the two: in the group of four cases, one should say, "If, perceiving what is nothing but black sheep's wool to be nothing but black, he instructs that a sitting mat be made" — and so it is to be rendered in groups of four.

The venerable Upāli asked the Blessed One, the Buddha: Venerable one, the Blessed One has declared: a monk who makes a sitting mat shall have two parts made from nothing but black sheep's wool, the third part from white wool, and the fourth part from coarse wool. What is the meaning of that? If a monk makes a sitting mat without having the two parts made from nothing but black sheep's wool, or the third part from white wool, or the fourth part from coarse wool, it is a transgression requiring abandonment — what does that mean? The remaining text is to be elaborated in full for the transgression requiring abandonment of the two-parts rule, just as it was for the transgression requiring abandonment for sheep's wool. As for what distinguishes this case: one should say in groups of four, "If a monk, perceiving white sheep's wool and coarse wool as being small in amount to be small, and perceiving nothing-but-black sheep's wool being large in amount to be large, instructs that a sitting mat be made." In both cases, for a fully ordained nun it is to be stated as a wrongdoing.

Summary verse:
From another's, the continuation of another's,
going to a householder who is a non-Buddhist adherent,
the improper, the nocturnal,
groups of four, and groups of seven for the Sugata.

The venerable Upāli asked the Blessed One, the Buddha: Venerable one, the Blessed One has declared that a monk who makes a sitting mat shall keep the old mat unwillingly for six years. If within those six years a monk makes another new sitting mat — whether he has discarded the old mat or not — it is a transgression requiring abandonment, except when the Saṅgha has given permission. What is the meaning of that? Venerable one, first: if a monk instructs that a sitting mat be made from another's materials, what is the result? It is a transgression requiring abandonment. If he instructs that a sitting mat be made for the sake of another, what is the result? It is a wrongdoing. Venerable one, if a monk makes properly from the continuation of another's sitting mat, what is the result? It is a wrongdoing. If he instructs that a sitting mat be made in front of a householder, what is the result? It is a transgression requiring abandonment.

Likewise, if a monk instructs that a sitting mat be made for a female probationer, a novice monk, a novice nun, one who has been given the precepts, one who is initially discordant, one in whom a transgression has previously arisen, one who abides in concealment, one who abides separately, or, from "one who should not abide" through to "one who has accepted," then what is the result? It is a transgression requiring abandonment.

Venerable one, if a monk instructs that a sitting mat be made for outsiders from this community, namely non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), wandering ascetics, brahmins, or wandering practitioners, what is the result? It is a transgression requiring abandonment.

Venerable one, if a monk instructs that a sitting mat be made for one who has gone to dwell elsewhere, what is the result? It is a wrongdoing.

Venerable one, is there any case in which a monk instructs that a sitting mat be made for one who is under six years of age, and yet it does not become a transgression requiring abandonment? There is a closely related case: if the material of that mat happens to be suitable but the cause is not suitable, or if the cause is suitable but the material is not suitable, or if both are not suitable. As for "not suitable": if he instructs that it be made from a mixture of felt from a drum, cow hair, wild animal wool, and goat wool, what is the result? It is a wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if a monk who has been characterized by instructing that a sitting mat be made for one under six years of age is then recognized and the sitting mat is properly made for a nun, what is the result? It is a wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if a nun who has been characterized by instructing that a sitting mat be made for one under six years of age is then recognized and the sitting mat is properly made for a monk, what is the result? It is a transgression requiring abandonment.

Venerable one, if a monk instructs that a sitting mat be made for one who has reached six years of age, with the perception that she has reached it, what is the result? There is no transgression. If he perceives one who has reached it as not having reached it, what is the result? It is a wrongdoing. If he perceives one who has not reached it as having reached it, what is the result? There is no transgression. If he perceives one who has not reached it as not having reached it, what is the result? It is a transgression requiring abandonment.

Venerable one, if a monk instructs that a sitting mat be made for one under six years of age, sized according to the measure of the Sugata's Dharma robe, what is the result? It is a wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if one who has not received full ordination prepares to make a sitting mat for one under six years of age, and then completes the making without having received full ordination, what is the result? It is a wrongdoing.

These are to be set forth extensively in groups of ten.

In summary:
Through messenger, letter, hand-signal,
through imputing a name to another, through an intermediary,
through a madman, through one whose mind is disturbed,
through one tormented by pain, and through a fool.

Venerable one, if a monk under six years of age instructs that a sitting mat be made through a messenger, what is the result? It is a wrongdoing. Likewise, if one instructs through a messenger, a letter, a hand-signal, by imputing a name to another, through an intermediary, through a madman, through one whose mind is disturbed, through one tormented by pain, through a fool, or through a mute, that a sitting mat be made, what is the result? It is a wrongdoing.

Just as a monk who makes a sitting mat for one under six years of age incurs a transgression requiring abandonment, so too does one who gives the precepts incur a transgression requiring abandonment. For a nun, it is a wrongdoing. For a female probationer, a novice monk, a novice nun, one in whom a transgression has previously arisen, and so forth, it is to be stated as a wrongdoing.

In summary:
From another's continuation of another's,
a householder, a non-Buddhist, one gone elsewhere,
the unsuitable, the characterized,
and likewise the groups of four and seven.

The venerable Ñe wa 'Khor then addressed the Blessed One, the Buddha: Venerable one, the Blessed One has said that a monk, in making the border of a sitting mat, should make the border of the old mat in a color that is unpleasing to the eye, by surrounding it with the Sugata's span of measure of old cloth.

If a monk, without surrounding the border of a new sitting mat with the Sugata's span of old cloth in order to make the color of the new mat unpleasing to the eye, were to use a new sitting mat with a new border, this would constitute an offense entailing forfeiture.

The venerable Ñe wa 'Khor then asked: Venerable one, if a monk, falling short of the Sugata's span by one finger-breadth or half a finger-breadth, were to have a new sitting mat made by another and then use it, what would that be? A wrongdoing. If he were to have a new sitting mat made on behalf of another, what would that be? A wrongdoing. If he were to finish what another had begun and then use it, what would that be? A wrongdoing. If he were to have a new sitting mat made and use it in front of a householder, what would that be? It would be an offense entailing forfeiture. Likewise, if he were to have a new sitting mat made and use it in front of a female probationer, a novice, a female novice, one who has been given training, one initially unsuitable, one in whom a transgression has previously arisen, one who lives by theft, one who lives apart, one who should not be resident, and so forth down to one who has made a confession — what would that be? It would be an offense entailing forfeiture.

Venerable one, if a monk were to have a new sitting mat made for a non-Buddhist outsider, a tīrthika, or for an ascetic, a brahmin, a wandering practitioner, or a wandering mendicant, what would that be? It would be an offense entailing forfeiture. If he were to have a new sitting mat made and use it for those gone to another destination, what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Venerable one, is there a case in which a monk, falling short of the Sugata's span by one finger-breadth or half a finger-breadth, were to have a new sitting mat made and use it without it constituting an offense entailing forfeiture? Ñe wa 'Khor replied: There is. It is as follows: if the border of that new sitting mat were suitable but the material were unsuitable; if the border were unsuitable but the material were suitable; or if both were unsuitable. What is unsuitable? A mat mixed with camel hair, cattle hair, wild-animal wool, or goat wool, or one that is unspun — if one were to have such a new sitting mat made and use it, it would be a wrongdoing.

The Supreme Vinaya. The Sixth Fascicle.

Venerable one, if a monk who has had a new sitting mat made falling short of the Sugata's span by one finger-breadth or half a finger-breadth, and it becomes marked as belonging to a nun, and he then uses it — what would that be? A wrongdoing. Venerable one, if a nun who has had a new sitting mat made falling short of the Sugata's span by one finger-breadth or half a finger-breadth, and it becomes marked as belonging to a monk, and she then uses it — what would that be? It would be an offense entailing forfeiture.

Venerable one, if a monk were to surround a mat with the Sugata's span in a place that does not require the span, perceiving it as not requiring the span, and then use the new sitting mat — what would that be? It would be an offense entailing forfeiture. If he were to surround it perceiving it as even more than required and then use it, what would that be? No transgression. If he were to surround it with less than the span perceiving it as not requiring the span and then use it, what would that be? A wrongdoing. If he were to surround it with more than required perceiving it as exceeding the requirement and then use it, what would that be? No transgression.

Venerable one, if one who has not received full ordination were to undertake the preparation for having a new sitting mat made falling short of the Sugata's span by one finger-breadth or half a finger-breadth, and were to complete that task while still not fully ordained — what would that be? A wrongdoing. If the preparation were undertaken while not fully ordained and the task were completed at the moment of full ordination, what would that be? A wrongdoing. If the preparation were undertaken while not fully ordained and the task were completed after full ordination, what would that be? A wrongdoing.

If the preparation were undertaken at the moment of full ordination and the task completed at the moment of full ordination, what would that be? A wrongdoing. If the preparation were undertaken at the moment of full ordination and the task completed after full ordination, what would that be? A wrongdoing. If the preparation were undertaken after full ordination and the task completed after full ordination, what would that be? An offense entailing forfeiture. If the preparation were undertaken after full ordination and the task completed before full ordination, what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Summary verse: messenger, letter, hand-signal; imputing it through another, through an intermediary; the mad one and the disturbed mind; the afflicted in sensation, and the dull-witted.

Venerable one, if a monk, while traveling on the road, were to find some sheep's wool and, wishing to have it, were to have it rendered fit and make use of it, falling short of the Sugata's span by one finger-breadth or half a finger-breadth — what would that be? A wrongdoing. Likewise, if he were to have it rendered fit and make use of it through a letter, through a hand-signal, through imputing it to another, through an intermediary, through a madman, through one of disturbed mind, through one afflicted in sensation, through a dull-witted person, or through an infant — what would that be? A wrongdoing. Just as a monk who has something rendered fit and makes use of it incurs an offense entailing forfeiture, so too it is the same for one who has received the training precepts. For a nun, if she has it rendered fit and makes use of it, it is a wrongdoing. For a female probationer, a novice monk, a novice nun, and one in whom a prior transgression has occurred, and so forth, it is a wrongdoing.

Summary verse: traversing the sky by supernatural power, by emanation; likewise mantras, and three others; two categories of the unfit, and characteristics; and four categories last.

The venerable Upāli asked the Blessed One, the buddha: Venerable one, the Blessed One has declared that if a monk traveling on the road finds sheep's wool and wishes to have it, that monk may take it; that having taken it, if there is no one to carry it, he may carry it himself up to a distance of three leagues; and that if he carries it beyond that, it becomes an offense entailing forfeiture. Venerable one, if a monk were to seek sheep's wool from the sky beyond three leagues, what would that be? If he carries it by supernatural power, there is no offense. But if he carries it by some other means, it becomes a wrongdoing. If one causing it to be rendered fit were to seek it by means of an emanation beyond three leagues, what would that be? A wrongdoing. Venerable one, if a monk were to render himself invisible by means of a secret mantra or a medicine, and were then to seek sheep's wool beyond three leagues, what would that be? A wrongdoing. If he were to seek sheep's wool beyond three leagues on another's behalf, what would that be? A wrongdoing. If another were to send sheep's wool from beyond three leagues, what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Venerable one, is there a case in which a monk might seek sheep's wool beyond three leagues and yet not incur an offense entailing forfeiture? Upāli, yes there is. It is as follows: a monk traveling on the road finds some sheep's wool and carries it himself for the duration of three leagues; then he finds yet another piece of sheep's wool, and he abandons the first and proceeds carrying only the second.

Venerable one, if a monk were to seek, beyond three leagues, wool that is not pure sheep's wool but is mixed with other unsuitable kinds — the wool of a deer, the hair of cattle, the wool of wild animals, or goat's wool — what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if a monk seeking sheep's wool beyond three leagues were recognized and were to flee to a nun and cross the boundary, what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if a nun seeking sheep's wool beyond three leagues were recognized and were to flee to a monk and cross the boundary, what would that be? A forfeiture and wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if a monk seeking sheep's wool beyond three leagues were to perceive a league as a league, what would that be? A forfeiture and wrongdoing. If he were to perceive what is not a league as a league, what would that be? A wrongdoing. If he were to perceive what is a league as not a league, what would that be? A wrongdoing. If he were to perceive what is not a league as not a league, what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Summary verse:

By messenger, by letter, by hand-signal,
by having another convey it, by one who is mad,
by one whose mind is disturbed,
by one overcome by feeling, by a fool —

Venerable one, if a monk were to send sheep's wool beyond three leagues by means of a messenger, what would that be? A wrongdoing. Likewise, if he were to send it by letter, by hand-signal, by having another convey it, through an intermediary, through a madman, through one whose mind is disturbed, through one afflicted and overcome by feeling, through a fool, or through one who is mentally impaired, beyond three leagues — what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Just as it is a forfeiture and wrongdoing for a monk to carry sheep's wool beyond three leagues, so too it is a forfeiture and wrongdoing in relation to one who has been given a training. For a nun, a probationer, a male novice, or a female novice, and for one in whom a prior transgression has arisen and so forth, it is a wrongdoing.

Summary verse:

By messenger, by letter, by hand-signal,
by having another convey it, through an intermediary,
by one who is mad, by one whose mind is disturbed,
by one overcome by feeling, by one mentally impaired — these are the cases.

Laypeople and so forth,
and one who has been given a training — these are the last.

The venerable Upāli then asked the Blessed One, the buddha: "Venerable one, the Blessed One has declared that if a monk causes a nun who is not a blood-relative to wash, card, or spin sheep's wool, it is a forfeiture and wrongdoing. What does that mean precisely?"

Venerable one, if a monk were to cause a nun who is not a blood-relative to wash sheep's wool by means of a messenger, what would that be? A wrongdoing. Likewise, if he were to cause her to do so by letter, by hand-signal, or by having another convey the instruction — what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if a monk were to cause a nun who is not a blood-relative and who is mad to wash sheep's wool, what would that be? A wrongdoing. Likewise, if he causes a nun who is not a blood-relative and whose mind is disturbed, or one overcome by feeling, or a fool, or one who is mentally impaired, to wash sheep's wool — what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if a monk were to cause a laywoman who is not a blood-relative to wash sheep's wool, what would that be? A wrongdoing. Likewise, if he causes to do so a nun who has acknowledged her vows, a paṇḍaka, a nun who has been expelled, one who has killed her mother, one who has killed her father, one who has killed an arhat, a non-Buddhist adherent (tīrthika), one who has joined the non-Buddhists, one who abides by means of theft, one who is not to remain, or one who was from the outset of incompatible status — what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if a monk were to cause a nun who has split the Saṅgha, or one who with malicious intent has drawn blood from a Tathāgata, or one who is living in schism, or one who has been given a training, to wash sheep's wool — what would that be? A forfeiture and wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if a monk who has been given a training were to cause a nun who is not a blood-relative to wash sheep's wool, what would that be?

A forfeiture and wrongdoing.

Summary verse:

One who has washed only a portion, the Saṅgha's wool, the two marks,
one who is of incompatible status, one who has been dismissed,
likewise one who is a blood-relative,
those arranged in groups of four,
and the nun — she is the last.

Venerable one, if a monk were to cause a nun who is not a blood-relative to wash only a portion of sheep's wool, what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if a monk were to cause wool of the Saṅgha to be washed, what would that be? If it has been used by a particular person and thereby made soiled, it would be a wrongdoing. If, however, it is an item of bedding that is caused to be washed, there is no transgression.

Venerable one, if a monk causes a nun who is not a blood-relative to wash wool and she, in the course of doing so, becomes a monk — what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if a nun causes a nun who is not a blood-relative to wash wool and she, in the course of doing so, becomes a monk — what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Venerable one, the Blessed One has declared that if a monk causes a nun who is not a blood-relative to wash sheep's wool, it is a forfeiture and wrongdoing. Are there cases, venerable one, where even though a monk causes a nun who is not a blood-relative to wash sheep's wool, it would not be a forfeiture and wrongdoing? There are related cases. Causing wool mixed with incompatible material to be washed: if it is wool mixed with incompatible material — camel hair, ox hair, deer wool, or goat wool — and one causes it to be washed, it would be a wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if a monk causes wool that has been forfeited to be washed, what would that be? If it has not yet been forfeited, it would be a forfeiture and wrongdoing. If it has already been forfeited, it would be a wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if a monk causes another's sheep's wool to be washed, what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if one makes the preparation while not yet fully ordained, and it is done while still not fully ordained, what would that be? A wrongdoing.
If the preparation is made while not yet fully ordained, but it is done while in the process of receiving full ordination, what would that be? A wrongdoing.
If the preparation is made while not yet fully ordained, but it is done after full ordination has been received, what would that be? A wrongdoing.
If the preparation is made while in the process of receiving full ordination, and it is done while in the process of receiving full ordination, what would that be? A wrongdoing.
If the preparation is made while in the process of receiving full ordination, and it is done after full ordination has been received, what would that be? A wrongdoing.
If the preparation is made after full ordination has been received, and it is done after full ordination has been received, what would that be? A forfeiture and wrongdoing.
If the preparation is made after full ordination has been received, but it is done while not yet fully ordained, what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if a monk causes a nun who is not a blood-relative to wash sheep's wool, perceiving her as not a blood-relative, what would that be? A forfeiture and wrongdoing.
If he perceives one who is not a blood-relative as a blood-relative, what would that be? No transgression.
If he perceives a blood-relative as not a blood-relative, what would that be? A wrongdoing.
If he perceives a blood-relative as a blood-relative, what would that be? No transgression.

Venerable one, if a monk causes a nun whom he perceives as fully ordained to wash that wool, what would that be? A forfeiture and wrongdoing.

If he perceives a fully ordained nun as not fully ordained, what would that be? A wrongdoing.
If he perceives one who is not fully ordained as not fully ordained, what would that be? A wrongdoing.
If he perceives one who is not fully ordained as fully ordained, what would that be? A wrongdoing.

If a monk causes a woman who is not a blood-relative — whether a female probationer (śikṣamāṇā), a novice nun, a laywoman, a non-Buddhist adherent of another way, a wandering ascetic, a brahmin, a wanderer, a mendicant, or one residing in another district — to wash wool, what would that be? A wrongdoing.

If a nun causes a monk, a novice monk, an outsider who is a non-Buddhist adherent of another way, a wandering ascetic, a brahmin, a wanderer, a mendicant, or one residing in another district to wash wool, what would that be? A wrongdoing. If she causes a layman or a laywoman to wash it, what would that be? A wrongdoing. In the cases of a female probationer, a novice nun, a novice monk, and one who has already incurred a transgression and so forth, it is a wrongdoing. In the same way, the cases of wringing and rinsing should also be stated.

Summary verse:
Through a messenger, a letter, a hand-sign,
through imputing to another, through an intermediary,
the insane, the mentally disturbed,
the afflicted, the deaf-mute, and one in another district.

The venerable Upāli then asked the Buddha, the Blessed One: Venerable one, the Blessed One has declared that whatever monk receives gold and silver with his own hand, or causes another to receive it, incurs a forfeiture. What is the substance of that ruling? Venerable one, first: if a monk causes a messenger to receive gold and silver, what would that be? A wrongdoing. Likewise, if he causes them to be received by means of a letter, a hand-sign, imputation to another, or through an intermediary, what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if a monk causes an insane person to receive gold and silver, what would that be? A wrongdoing. Likewise, if he causes a mentally disturbed person, one overwhelmed by pain, a simpleton, a deaf-mute, or one residing in another district to receive gold and silver, what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Summary verse:
Secretly, at night, in the flesh-hall,
in groups of four, in groups of seven, in seclusion,
a laywoman and others — body and voice —
receiving and setting down — a nun.

Venerable one, are there cases in which a monk takes gold and silver and yet does not incur a forfeiture? Upāli answered: There are. If he causes it to be received from a hidden direction, or if he perceives it as some other object.

Venerable one, if a monk who is in the act of receiving gold and silver changes sex in the process and becomes a nun, what would that be? It becomes a forfeiture. Venerable one, if a nun who is in the act of receiving gold and silver changes sex and becomes a monk, what would that be? It becomes a forfeiture.

Venerable one, if a monk receives, or causes another to receive, a māṣa weight of gold or a māṣa weight of silver, what would that be? It becomes a forfeiture. If he receives, or causes another to receive, a māṣa weight of bell-metal or a māṣa weight of copper, what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if a monk perceives gold and silver as gold and silver and takes them, what would that be? It becomes a forfeiture. If he perceives gold and silver as not being gold and silver, what would that be? No transgression. If he perceives what is not gold and silver as not being gold and silver, what would that be? A wrongdoing. If he perceives what is not gold and silver as not being gold and silver, what would that be? No transgression.

Venerable one, if one who is not fully ordained makes the preparation for receiving gold and silver and then receives them while still not fully ordained, what would that be? A wrongdoing. If one who is not fully ordained makes the preparation and then receives them after having become fully ordained, what would that be? A wrongdoing.

If one who is not fully ordained makes the preparation and then receives them after having become fully ordained, what would that be? A wrongdoing. If one who is fully ordained makes the preparation and then receives them while still fully ordained, what would that be? A wrongdoing. If one who is fully ordained makes the preparation and then receives them after having become fully ordained, what would that be? An offense requiring forfeiture. If one who is fully ordained makes the preparation and then receives them without being fully ordained, what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if a monk, perceiving a place as secluded, were to appoint someone to receive gold and silver, what would that be? A wrongdoing. If he perceives what is secluded as not secluded, what would that be? A wrongdoing. If he perceives what is not secluded as secluded, what would that be? A wrongdoing. If he perceives what is not secluded as not secluded, what would that be? An offense requiring forfeiture.

Venerable one, if a monk were to directly hand over gold and silver to a layperson in person, what would that be? An offense requiring forfeiture. Likewise, in the case of one who has killed his mother, one who has killed his father, one who has killed an arhat, one who has caused a schism in the Saṅgha, one who has drawn the blood of a Tathāgata with malicious intent, a tīrthika, one who has entered the life of a tīrthika, one who dwells by means of theft, one who dwells separately, one who is made not to remain, one who has given acknowledgment, one who was initially discordant, one in whom a prior transgression has arisen, and one who has been given a training — if gold and silver is directly handed over to them in person, what would that be? An offense requiring forfeiture.

Venerable one, if a monk were to authorize the receiving of gold and silver through a bodily gesture, what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if a monk from the central region, speaking in a border dialect, were to authorize a person from the central region to receive gold and silver, and that person from the central region does not understand the border dialect, what would that be? A wrongdoing. If a person from the border region, speaking in the language of the central region, were to authorize a person from the border region to receive gold and silver, and that person from the border region does not understand the language of the central region, what would that be? A wrongdoing. If a person from the border region, speaking in a border dialect, were to authorize a person from the central region to receive gold and silver, and that person from the central region does not understand the border dialect, what would that be? A wrongdoing. If a person from the central region, speaking in the language of the central region, were to authorize a person from the border region to receive gold and silver, and that person from the border region does not understand the language of the central region, what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Venerable one, is there any case in which a monk who receives gold and silver with his own hand, or authorizes another to receive it, would not incur an offense? A close attendant may be present. In such a case, when receiving gold and silver, one does not give rise to the thought of making it one's own, but rather does so thinking it is some other unsuitable object belonging to another. And even so, one must not say a word about it.

Venerable one, if a monk receives gold and silver on behalf of another monk, or authorizes another to do so, what would that be? It incurs an offense. Venerable one, if a nun receives gold and silver or authorizes another to receive it, what would that be? An offense requiring forfeiture. Venerable one, if one who has been given a training receives gold and silver with her own hand, or authorizes another to receive it, what would that be? An offense requiring forfeiture. Venerable one, if a female trainee receives gold and silver with her own hand, or authorizes another to receive it, what would that be? A wrongdoing. For a novice monk and a novice nun there is no transgression. For one in whom a prior transgression has arisen, and so forth, it is a wrongdoing.

Through a messenger, a written message, a hand signal,
through an intermediary imputing a designation to another,
through madness, through mental disturbance,
through experiencing sensation — these are the cases.

Thus did the venerable close attendant present these questions to the Blessed One, the Buddha.

"Venerable one, the Blessed One declared that when a monk engages in various kinds of lewd conduct, it is a transgression entailing expulsion. What are those cases?

Venerable one, first: if a monk engages in various kinds of lewd conduct with a messenger, what would that be? A wrongdoing. Likewise, the same should be stated for a written message, a hand signal, acting on behalf of another, imputing a designation to another, and through an intermediary.

Venerable one, first: if a monk engages in various kinds of lewd conduct together with a mad person, what would that be? A wrongdoing. Likewise: if he does so with one who is mentally disturbed, with one overcome by sensation, with one who is deaf, with one who is dull-witted, or with beings dwelling in another destiny — what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Summary verse:

The marks, only groups of four,
groups of seven, and on behalf of another,
one in training, and one like a relative,
and a nun — these are the last.

Venerable one, first: if a monk engages in various kinds of lewd conduct and thereby comes to bear the mark, and then, having been ordained as a nun, obtains the aim — what would that be? It would be a transgression entailing expulsion.

Venerable one, first: if a nun engages in various kinds of lewd conduct and thereby comes to bear the mark, and then, having been ordained as a monk, obtains the aim — what would that be? It would be a transgression entailing expulsion.

Venerable one, first: if a monk, perceiving a human being as a human being, engages in various kinds of lewd conduct — what would that be? It would be a transgression entailing expulsion. If he perceives a human being as not a human being, what would that be? A wrongdoing. If he perceives one who is not a human being as a human being, what would that be? A wrongdoing. If he perceives one who is not a human being as not a human being, what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Venerable one, first: if one not yet fully ordained performs the act preparatory to lewd conduct and then regards oneself as not yet fully ordained — what would that be? A wrongdoing. If one not yet fully ordained performs the preparatory act and then regards oneself as in the process of receiving full ordination, what would that be? A wrongdoing. If one not yet fully ordained performs the preparatory act and then regards oneself as already fully ordained, what would that be? A wrongdoing. If one in the process of receiving full ordination performs the preparatory act and then regards oneself as in the process of receiving full ordination, what would that be? A wrongdoing. If one in the process of receiving full ordination performs the preparatory act and then regards oneself as already fully ordained, what would that be? A wrongdoing. If one already fully ordained performs the preparatory act and then regards oneself as already fully ordained, what would that be? It would be a transgression entailing expulsion. If one already fully ordained performs the preparatory act and then regards oneself as not yet fully ordained, what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Venerable one, first: if a monk engages in various kinds of lewd conduct on behalf of another, what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Venerable one, first: if one who has given back the training engages in various kinds of lewd conduct, what would that be? It would be a transgression entailing expulsion.

Venerable one, if one engages in various kinds of lewd conduct together with a relative, what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Venerable one, first: is there a case in which a monk engages in various kinds of lewd conduct and yet does not incur a transgression? There is something close to it: if one who is like exchanges with one who is like, there is no transgression.

Venerable one, first: if a nun engages in various kinds of lewd conduct, what would that be? It would be a transgression entailing expulsion. For a female trainee, a novice monk, a novice nun, and —"

— for a previous transgression and so forth, it is a wrongdoing.

Venerable one, the Blessed One, the Buddha, declared that if a monk engages in various kinds of trade and commerce, it becomes a transgression entailing forfeiture. What exactly is the scope of this? Venerable one, Upāli asked this of the Blessed One, the Buddha. As for the remaining text: just as it was explained in the section on conduct involving explicit characteristics, so too with respect to trade and commerce this should be expounded in full.

Summary verse:

The same rule — diminished, another district,
non-Buddhist, surrendered, not yet, white,
insane, marked, messenger,
possessing or earning a living — for nuns.

Venerable one, the Blessed One, the Buddha, declared: "A monk may keep an alms-bowl beyond the allotted number for up to ten days; if kept beyond that, it becomes a transgression entailing forfeiture." What exactly is the scope of this?

Venerable one, is there a case in which keeping that very bowl both does and does not constitute a transgression? Upāli answered: There is such a case. If a monk is keeping a large alms-bowl and, in the course of doing so, there is a change of status and he becomes a nun, and she then keeps it, it becomes a wrongdoing.

Venerable one, first: if a monk has forfeited a bowl and it is then lost, should the transgression be confessed? Upāli answered: It should be confessed. After confessing the transgression, if it is recovered, should it be relinquished? Upāli answered: No, it need not be.

Venerable one, the Blessed One, the Buddha, declared that a monk may keep an alms-bowl beyond the allotted number for up to ten days. Is there a case in which keeping one well beyond the count of ninety days does not constitute a transgression? Upāli answered: There is such a case. If a monk from one district sends an alms-bowl to another monk in the hand of a monk in another district, and that monk keeps the bowl for an exceedingly long time, there is no transgression.

Venerable one, first: if a monk keeps a vessel belonging to a non-Buddhist, what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Venerable one, first: if a monk keeps an alms-bowl that has not yet been surrendered beyond the allotted number, what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Venerable one, first: if a monk keeps a plain, undyed bowl, what would that be? A wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if a monk's mind becomes disturbed immediately upon receiving an alms-bowl, from what point should the count of ten days begin? From the point when his own mind returns to normal.

Venerable one, is there a case in which a monk keeps an alms-bowl beyond the count of ninety days past the allotted number and yet does not incur a transgression entailing forfeiture? Upāli answered: There is such a case. If a monk receives an alms-bowl, ten days pass, and then there is a change of status and he becomes a nun, and she then keeps it — in that case there is no transgression.

Venerable one, is there a case in which a nun keeps an alms-bowl beyond the allotted number of more than one and yet does not incur a transgression? Upāli answered: There is such a case. If a nun receives an alms-bowl and, immediately upon receiving it, there is a change of status and she becomes a monk, and he then keeps it — in that case there is no transgression.

Venerable one, is there a case in which a monk keeps an alms-bowl beyond the allotted number for a very long time and yet does not incur a transgression? Upāli answered: There is such a case. If a monk asks many monks on behalf of another monk for an alms-bowl, and that monk takes the bowl and keeps it for a very long time, there is no transgression.

Venerable one, is there a case in which a monk possesses only a single alms-bowl and yet that very bowl becomes subject to forfeiture? Upāli answered: There is such a case: if it is kept without having been consecrated.

Venerable one, is there a case in which a monk keeps an alms-bowl beyond the allotted number for as long as he lives and yet does not incur a transgression? Upāli answered: There is such a case —

— if that monk has let more than ten days pass without consecrating it.

Venerable one, what is the result if one who has received the training keeps an extra alms-bowl beyond the number ninety? It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture.

If a nun keeps an extra alms-bowl beyond one day, it becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture.

For a probationer nun, a novice monk, and a novice nun, there is no transgression. For one who has previously incurred a transgression and so forth, it is a misdeed.

Summary verse:

Through a messenger, written note, hand signal,
through having another designate, through an intermediary,
through one who is mad, through one whose mind is disturbed,
through one overcome by feeling — these are the cases of going.

The venerable Upāli then put a question to the Blessed One, the Buddha: Venerable one, Bhagavān has stated that when a monk who possesses an adequate alms-bowl nonetheless seeks another, superior bowl out of desire for something better, and once the bowl is obtained that becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture — that monk must then surrender the bowl to the community of monks, and whichever bowl is the last in that community of monks should be given back to him with the words: "Monk, you must not consecrate this bowl, must not discard it, must not give it to another; you must use it gently and carefully until it breaks beyond repair." Bhagavān declared that this is the proper procedure. Venerable one, in that context, what is the result if a monk seeks a second alms-bowl through a messenger? It is a misdeed. What if he obtains the bowl? It is a misdeed only.

What is the result if, through a messenger, a monk seeks a second alms-bowl by means of a written note, a hand signal, having another designate, or through an intermediary? It is a misdeed. What if he obtains the bowl? It is a misdeed only.

Venerable one, what is the result if a monk seeks a second alms-bowl from one who is mad? It is a misdeed. What if he obtains the bowl? It is a misdeed only.

Likewise, if he seeks a second alms-bowl from one whose mind is disturbed, from one overcome by suffering, from one who is dull-witted, from one who is mute, or from one who has gone elsewhere — what is the result? It is a misdeed. What if he obtains the bowl? It is a misdeed only.

Summary verse:

From a non-Buddhist, and from food,
likewise from one of ambiguous sex,
from that which is shared, and from a set of four,
from implements, and from a nun.

Venerable one, what is the result if a monk seeks a second alms-bowl from those outside this teaching — non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), wandering ascetics, brahmins, practitioners, and wanderers? It is a misdeed. What if he obtains the bowl? It is a misdeed only.

Venerable one, what is the result if a monk accepts a vessel belonging to a non-Buddhist? It is a misdeed. What if he obtains the vessel? It is a misdeed only.

Vinaya: The Principal Text, Seventh Fascicle.

Venerable one, what is the result if a monk seeks a second alms-bowl from food? It is a misdeed. What if he obtains the bowl? It is a misdeed only.

Likewise, if he seeks a second alms-bowl from a paṇḍaka man, from a paṇḍaka woman, or from a person of ambiguous sex — what is the result? It is a misdeed. What if he obtains the bowl? It is a misdeed only.

Venerable one, what is the result if a monk, while possessing an adequate alms-bowl, accepts a second bowl and then undergoes a change of sex and is reclassified as a nun, and thereafter obtains it? It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture.

Venerable one, what is the result if a nun, while possessing an adequate alms-bowl, accepts a second bowl and then undergoes a change of sex and is reclassified as a monk, and thereafter obtains it?

It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture.

Venerable one, what is the result if a monk, while possessing an adequate alms-bowl, seeks a second bowl from a male householder or from a member of a householder's retinue, and both of them undergo a change of sex, and thereafter he obtains it? It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture.
