Venerable one, what is the result if a nun, while possessing an adequate alms-bowl, seeks a second bowl from a male householder or from a member of a householder's retinue who is not a relative, and both of them undergo a change of sex, and thereafter she obtains it? It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture.

What is the result if it is solicited before full ordination and completed before full ordination? It is a misdeed. What is the result if it is solicited before full ordination and completed at the moment of full ordination? It is a misdeed. What is the result if it is solicited before full ordination and completed after full ordination? It is a misdeed. What is the result if it is solicited at the moment of full ordination and completed at the moment of full ordination? It is a misdeed. What is the result if it is solicited at the moment of full ordination and completed after full ordination? It is a misdeed. What is the result if it is solicited after full ordination and completed after full ordination? It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture. What is the result if it is solicited after full ordination and completed before full ordination? It is a misdeed.

Venerable one, what is the result if two monks accept one alms-bowl together? It is a misdeed. What if they obtain the bowl? It is a misdeed only.

Venerable one, what is the result if a monk, perceiving a person as a person, accepts a second alms-bowl? It is a misdeed. What if he obtains the bowl? It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture. What is the result if he perceives a person as not a person? It is a misdeed. What if he obtains the bowl? It is a misdeed only. What is the result if he perceives a non-person as a person? It is a misdeed. What if he obtains the bowl? It is a misdeed only. What is the result if he perceives a non-person as a non-person? It is a misdeed. What if he obtains the bowl? It is a misdeed only.

Venerable one, what is the result if a monk searches among a monk's requisites, or gradually seeks a second alms-bowl? There is no transgression.

Venerable one, what is the result if one who has been given a training rule, while possessing an adequate alms-bowl, seeks a second alms-bowl? It is a misdeed. What if he obtains the bowl? It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture.

Venerable one, what is the result if a nun, while possessing an adequate alms-bowl, seeks a second alms-bowl? It is a misdeed. What if she obtains the bowl? It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture.

What is the result if a female trainee (śikṣamāṇā), a novice monk, or a novice nun, while possessing an adequate alms-bowl, seeks a second alms-bowl? It is a misdeed. What if they obtain the bowl? It is a misdeed only. The earlier-arising transgressions and so forth amount to a misdeed — as before.

Summary verse:

Through a messenger, a letter, a hand-signal,
through imputing it to another, through an intermediary,
through madness, through distraction of mind,
through dull sensation — such are the cases that follow.

The venerable Upāli asked the Blessed One, the buddha Bhagavān: Venerable one, the Blessed One has declared that if a monk has a non-relative weave a robe using yarn he himself has solicited, and the robe is completed, it becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture. What is the meaning of that?

Venerable one, what is the result if a monk has a non-relative weaver, contacted through a messenger, weave a robe, and the robe is completed? It is a misdeed. Likewise through a letter, through a hand-signal, through imputing it to another, through an intermediary,

through another's behalf — if a monk has a non-relative weaver weave a robe, what is the result? It is a misdeed.

Venerable one, if a monk has a weaver who is insane weave a robe and the robe is completed, what is the result? It is a misdeed. Likewise, if the weaver is distracted, or enfeebled by illness, or deaf, or feeble-minded, or has gone to live in another place — if any of these, being non-relatives, are commissioned to weave a robe and the robe is completed, what is the result? It is a misdeed.

Summary verse:

A hermaphrodite, a woman, likewise a common robe,
the unsuitable — these seven types together;
then four types: a trainee and a nun.

Venerable one, if a monk commissions a non-relative weaver who is a hermaphrodite to weave a robe, and the robe is completed, what is the result? It is a misdeed. Likewise if the weaver is a woman, or a hermaphrodite, or of indeterminate sex — if these are non-relatives who are commissioned to weave a robe and the robe is completed, what is the result? It is a misdeed.

Venerable one, if a monk commissions a non-relative weaver to weave a shared robe and the robe is completed, what is the result? It is a misdeed.

Venerable one, if a monk commissions a non-relative weaver to weave a robe, and in the course of that the weaver changes sex and becomes a nun, and the monk then receives the robe — what is the result? It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture. If a nun commissions a non-relative weaver to weave a robe, and in the course of that the weaver changes sex and becomes a monk, and the nun then receives the robe — what is the result? It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture.

Venerable one, is there a case where a monk commissions a non-relative weaver to weave a robe, the robe is completed, and yet it does not become a transgression requiring forfeiture? There is a case close to it. It is as follows: the thread of that robe may be suitable but the warp unsuitable; or the warp suitable but the thread unsuitable; or both unsuitable — unsuitable meaning that it contains yak-hair, cattle-hair, wild-animal wool, or goat-hair mixed in, and a non-relative weaver is commissioned to weave it; if the robe is completed, it becomes a misdeed.

Venerable one, if one who has not yet received full ordination sets out the preparatory action of commissioning a non-relative weaver to weave a robe, and the robe is completed before full ordination is received, what is the result? It is a misdeed. If the preparatory action is set in motion before full ordination and the robe is completed at the moment of full ordination, what is the result? It is a misdeed. If the preparatory action is set in motion before full ordination and the robe is completed after full ordination, what is the result? It is a misdeed. If the preparatory action is set in motion at the moment of full ordination and the robe is completed at the moment of full ordination, what is the result? It is a misdeed. If the preparatory action is set in motion at the moment of full ordination and the robe is completed after full ordination, what is the result? It is a misdeed. If the preparatory action is set in motion after full ordination and the robe is completed after full ordination, what is the result? It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture. If the preparatory action is set in motion after full ordination and the robe is completed before full ordination, what is the result? It is a misdeed.

Venerable one, if a monk commissions a weaver who is not a human being, perceiving him as not a human being, to weave a robe, and the robe is completed, what is the result? It is a misdeed. If he perceives the non-human being as human, what is the result? It is a misdeed. If he perceives a human being as not human, what is the result? It is a misdeed. If he perceives a human being as human, what is the result? It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture.

Venerable one, if a monk commissions a non-relative weaver, perceiving him as a non-relative, to weave a robe, and the robe is completed, what is the result? It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture. If he perceives a non-relative as a relative, what is the result? There is no transgression.

If he perceives a relative as a non-relative, what is the result? It is a misdeed. If he perceives a relative as a relative, what is the result? There is no transgression.

Venerable one, if one who has received the training commissions a non-relative weaver to weave a robe, and the robe is completed, what is the result? It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture. Venerable one, if a nun commissions a non-relative weaver to weave a robe, and the robe is completed, what is the result? It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture. If a female trainee, a novice monk, or a novice nun commissions a non-relative weaver to weave a robe, what is the result? It is a misdeed. If the robe is completed, what is the result? It is a misdeed only. As for the prior occurrence of a transgression and so forth, it is a misdeed, just as before.

The venerable Upāli asked the Blessed One, the Buddha: Venerable one, the Blessed One has declared that when a householder or a householder's wife who is not a relative causes a non-relative weaver to weave a robe for a monk, and if the monk, having become aware of certain intentions that had not previously been communicated to him, goes to that non-relative weaver and says: "Venerable weaver, know this: weave this robe for me. Venerable weaver, make this robe well — wide in breadth, long in length, well-beaten at the edges, and well-woven throughout. Venerable weaver, in this way I will give you wages such as food, provisions, and a little something to eat" — and if that monk, having so spoken, provides wages such as food, provisions, and a little something to eat for the completion of the robe, and the robe is completed, it becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture. What is there to be said differently? The remaining text, in accordance with what was taught for the forfeiture applying to commissioning weaving, is to be stated here in the same way for this forfeiture applying to commissioning additional weaving.

What is distinct? Venerable one, if a monk, having become aware of intentions, dispatches a messenger, what is the result? It is a misdeed. If the robe is obtained, what is the result? It is a misdeed only — and so forth, this is to be stated.

Venerable one, if a monk, having become aware of intentions, solicits a non-relative weaver while perceiving him as a human being, what is the result? It is a misdeed. If the robe is completed, what is the result? It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture. If he perceives a human being as not human, what is the result? It is a misdeed. If the robe is completed, what is the result? It is a misdeed only. If he perceives a non-human being as human, what is the result? It is a misdeed. If completed, it is as before. If he perceives a non-human being as a non-human being, what is the result? It is a misdeed. If completed, it is as before.

Venerable one, if a monk, having become aware of intentions, solicits a non-relative weaver while perceiving him as a non-relative, what is the result? It is a misdeed. If the robe is completed, what is the result? It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture. If he perceives a non-relative as a relative, what is the result? There is no transgression. If he perceives a relative as a non-relative, what is the result? It is a misdeed. If he perceives a relative as a relative, what is the result? There is no transgression.

Venerable one, if one who has received the training, having become aware of intentions, solicits a non-relative weaver, what is the result? It is a misdeed. If the robe is completed, what is the result? It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture. Venerable one, if a nun, having become aware of intentions, solicits a non-relative weaver, what is the result? It is a misdeed. If the robe is completed, what is the result? It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture.

Venerable one, if a female trainee, a male novice, or a female novice, having become aware of intentions, solicits a non-relative weaver, what is the result? It is a misdeed. If the robe is completed, what is the result? It is a misdeed only. With regard to the earlier cases beginning with a transgression already having arisen and so forth, it is a misdeed, as before.

Summary verse:
By messenger, by letter, by gesture,
by imputing words to another, through an intermediary,
for one who is mad, for one whose mind is distracted,
for one afflicted by painful feeling, for one who is dull.

The venerable Upāli questioned the Blessed One, the buddha: Venerable one, regarding what the Blessed One declared — that if a monk, having given a robe to another monk, afterward becomes angry, agitated, and furious toward him, and being displeased, either seizes it or causes it to be seized, and says to him, "I will not give you the robe — return it," and if that monk, there being a remainder of the robe, returns it and hands it over, it becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture — venerable one, first: if a monk has given a robe and then causes it to be seized from a messenger, what is the result? It is a misdeed. Likewise, if a nun, having given a robe, causes it to be seized by letter, by gesture, by imputing words to another, through an intermediary, or for the sake of another, what is the result? It is a misdeed. Venerable one, if a monk, having given a robe to a mad monk, then seizes it or causes it to be seized, what is the result? It is a misdeed. Likewise, if he gives a robe to one whose mind is distracted, to one afflicted by painful feeling, to one who is dull, or to one who is deaf, and then seizes it or causes it to be seized, what is the result? It is a misdeed. Venerable one, if a monk gives a robe to outsiders of other traditions — tīrthikas, wandering ascetics (śramaṇas), brahmins, practitioners, and wanderers — and then seizes it or causes it to be seized, what is the result? It is a misdeed. Venerable one, if a monk gives a robe to one who has gone to another destination and then seizes it or causes it to be seized, what is the result? It is a misdeed.

Summary verse:
To laypeople and so forth,
likewise those sharing a common characteristic,
the uncut and the unfit —
the fourth group is the last.

Venerable one, if a monk gives a robe to a layman and then seizes it or causes it to be seized, what is the result? It is a misdeed. Likewise, if he gives a robe to one who has voluntarily undertaken vows, to a hermaphrodite monk, to a neuter, to an expelled nun, to one who has killed her mother, to one who has killed his father, to one who has killed an arhat, to a tīrthika, to one who has entered among the tīrthikas, to one abiding as a thief, to one who was initially incompatible, or to one in whom a transgression has previously arisen, and then seizes it or causes it to be seized, what is the result? It is a misdeed. Venerable one, if a monk gives a robe to a monk who has caused a schism in the Saṅgha and then seizes it or causes it to be seized, what is the result? It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture. Likewise, if he gives a robe to one who with malicious intent has drawn blood from a Tathāgata, to one dwelling apart, or to one who has relinquished the training, and then seizes it or causes it to be seized, what is the result? It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture. Venerable one, if a monk gives a robe to a nun, a female trainee, a male novice, or a female novice, and then seizes it or causes it to be seized, what is the result? It is a misdeed. Venerable one, if a monk gives a robe to a monk and then causes a layman to seize it, what is the result? It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture. Likewise, a hermaphrodite monk, a neuter, an expelled nun, one who has killed her mother, one who has killed his father, one who has killed an arhat, one who has caused a schism in the Saṅgha, one who with malicious intent has drawn blood from a Tathāgata, a tīrthika, one who has entered among the tīrthikas,

one who conceals a theft, one who dwells separately, one who is not permitted to reside, one who from the outset is incompatible, one in whom a transgression has previously arisen, one who has made an acknowledgment, one who has been given a training rule, a monk, a nun, a female trainee, a male novice, or a female novice — if one gives a robe to such a person and then seizes it, or causes it to be seized, what is the result? It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture.

Venerable one, if two monks give a robe to a monk in common, and thereafter one of them seizes it or causes it to be seized, what is the result? It is a misdeed.

Venerable one, if a monk gives a robe to a monk and then seizes it, and in the course of seizing it the robe undergoes a transformation and becomes a nun's robe, and the seizure is accomplished in that state, what is the result? It is a misdeed.

Venerable one, if a nun gives a robe to a nun and then seizes it, and in the course of seizing it the robe undergoes a transformation and becomes a monk's robe, and the seizure is accomplished in that state, what is the result? It is a misdeed.

Venerable one, if a monk gives a robe to a monk that is smaller than a cubit in size, and then seizes it or causes it to be seized, what is the result? It is a misdeed.

Venerable one, is there any case in which a monk gives a robe to a monk and then seizes it, yet it does not become a transgression requiring forfeiture? There is a case nearby. It is as follows: when the cloth of the robe is of permissible fiber but the thread is impermissible, or the fiber is impermissible but the thread is permissible, or both fiber and thread are impermissible — that is, when it is mixed with the wool of a wild yak, ox hair, deer wool, or goat wool, or when it has not been woven. If one gives such a robe as that and then seizes it or causes it to be seized, what is the result? It is a misdeed.

Venerable one, if a monk perceives a fully ordained person as fully ordained, gives him a robe, and then seizes it or causes it to be seized, what is the result? It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture.

If one perceives a fully ordained person as not fully ordained, gives him a robe, and then seizes it or causes it to be seized, what is the result? It is a misdeed.

If one perceives a person who is not fully ordained as fully ordained, gives him a robe, and then seizes it or causes it to be seized, what is the result? It is a misdeed.

If one perceives a person who is not fully ordained as not fully ordained, gives him a robe, and then seizes it or causes it to be seized, what is the result? It is a misdeed.

Venerable one, if one forms the intention to seize from one who is not fully ordained and seizes from one who is not fully ordained, what is the result? It is a misdeed.

If one forms the intention to seize from one who is not fully ordained and seizes from one who is in the process of receiving full ordination, what is the result? It is a misdeed.

If one forms the intention to seize from one who is not fully ordained and seizes after full ordination has been completed, what is the result? It is a misdeed.

If one forms the intention to seize from one who is in the process of receiving full ordination and seizes from one who is in the process of receiving full ordination, what is the result? It is a misdeed.

If one forms the intention to seize from one who is in the process of receiving full ordination and seizes after full ordination has been completed, what is the result? It is a misdeed.

If one forms the intention to seize after full ordination has been completed and seizes after full ordination has been completed, what is the result? It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture.

If one forms the intention to seize after full ordination has been completed and seizes from one who is not fully ordained, what is the result? It is a misdeed.

Venerable one, if one who has been given a training rule gives a robe to a monk and then seizes it or causes it to be seized, what is the result? It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture.

Venerable one, if a monk gives a robe to a nun and then seizes it or causes it to be seized, what is the result? It is a misdeed.

Venerable one, if a nun gives a robe to a nun and then seizes it or causes it to be seized, what is the result?

It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture. If a nun gives a robe to a monk, a novice monk, a female probationer, or a novice nun and then seizes it or causes it to be seized, what is the result? It is a misdeed. For a female probationer, a novice monk, a novice nun, and cases involving a prior transgression and so forth, the misdeed should be stated at length.

Summary verse:

Distraction of mind, and lapse of time,
likewise shared, and nocturnal,
four categories of inopportune —
a nun stands last.

Venerable Upāli asked the Blessed One, the buddha: Venerable one, the Blessed One has declared that if a monk finds a robe that has come from the rains-retreat period — that is, up to and including the ten days beyond the middle month of autumn — and desires it, that monk should take that robe, and having taken it, should keep it until the time for distributing robes. If he keeps it beyond that time, it is a transgression requiring forfeiture. Now, venerable one, is there a case in which a monk keeps a robe that came from the rains retreat beyond the time for distributing robes and yet it does not become a transgression requiring forfeiture? Upāli replied: Yes, there is such a case — namely, if the monk's mind becomes distracted immediately upon obtaining the robe. Venerable one, is there a case in which a monk keeps a robe that came from the rains retreat for the rest of his life and yet it does not become a transgression requiring forfeiture? Upāli replied: Yes — if he dies within the period for distributing robes. Venerable one, if during the period up to the time for distributing robes a monk who holds a robe that came from the rains retreat undergoes a change of sex and becomes a nun, what is the result? It is a misdeed. Venerable one, if during the period up to the time for distributing robes a nun who holds a robe that came from the rains retreat undergoes a change of sex and becomes a monk, what is the result? It is a misdeed. Venerable one, if a monk keeps a robe held in common that came from the rains retreat beyond the time for distributing robes, what is the result? It is a misdeed. Venerable one, is there a case in which a monk keeps a robe that came from the rains retreat beyond the time for distributing robes and yet it does not become a transgression requiring forfeiture? Upāli replied: Yes — if the fiber of the robe has become suitable but the thread is unsuitable, or the thread is suitable but the fiber is unsuitable, or both are unsuitable. What is meant by unsuitable? It is mixed with camel hair, ox hair, wild-animal wool, or goat wool, or it has not been woven. If a robe of that kind is kept beyond the time for distributing robes, it becomes a misdeed. Venerable one, if a monk keeps a robe perceiving that the time for distributing robes has passed, when it has in fact passed, what is the result? It becomes a transgression requiring forfeiture. If he keeps it perceiving that the time has not passed, when it has in fact passed, what is the result? There is no transgression. If he keeps it perceiving that the time has passed, when it has in fact not passed, what is the result? It is a misdeed. If he keeps it perceiving that the time has not passed, when it has in fact not passed, what is the result? There is no transgression. Venerable one, if the king banishes the community without the invitation ceremony having been performed, how should a monk accomplish the receipt of robes from the rains retreat? Upāli replied: Just as one maintains the commitment to the rains retreat as originally undertaken. Should a robe from the rains retreat be called one on which ten nights have elapsed? Or should it not be called one on which ten nights have elapsed? The Blessed One declared: It should not be called one on which ten days have elapsed. Venerable one, may a robe from the rains retreat be treated as an out-of-season robe? Upāli replied: It may not.

Venerable one, what did the Blessed One mean when he declared, arising from the precept he had laid down: "Endure until the time of giving the Dharma robe"? And venerable one, what is the time of giving the Dharma robe? The period near the end is the invitation ceremony; without the invitation ceremony having been performed, it is at all times simply out of season. Venerable one, if a monk who has been given a training-rule sets aside a robe from the rains retreat past the time of giving the Dharma robe, what does that entail? It entails a forfeiture offence. Venerable one, if a nun sets aside a robe from the rains retreat past the time of giving the Dharma robe, what does that entail? It entails a forfeiture offence. With regard to probationary nuns, novice monks, novice nuns, and those who have already incurred a downfall and so forth, the matter is to be explained at length as a wrongdoing.

In summary: the unfit, and groups of four; likewise, interruptions; the vow, the Saṅgha, and space; likewise, the night, and nuns.

Venerable Upāli addressed the Blessed One, the Buddha: Venerable one, the Blessed One has declared that when many monks are dwelling in a wilderness hermitage known to be fearful — known to be dangerous, known to be differently dangerous, known to be worthy of fear — and it is the latter part of the rains retreat, a monk who is a wilderness dweller may, if he wishes, leave any one of the three robes in a household. And if a condition arises for that wilderness-dwelling monk such that he must be separated from that robe and go beyond the boundary, that monk may be separated from the robe and beyond the boundary for up to six nights. If he is separated beyond that, it entails a forfeiture offence. Venerable one, is there a case in which a monk separated from the robe and beyond the boundary for more than six nights does not incur a forfeiture offence? Upāli replied: There is. It is as follows: if the robe's material becomes unfit, and there is an unfit cause — or the cause becomes fit and the material becomes unfit — or both become unfit. The unfit materials are: camel wool, ox hair, mountain-deer wool mixed with goat wool, and unspun material. If one is separated from a robe of such material and goes beyond the boundary after six nights have elapsed, it is a wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if a monk perceives that more than six nights have elapsed and is separated from the robe and goes beyond the boundary, what does that entail? It entails a forfeiture offence. If more than six nights have elapsed but he perceives that they have not elapsed and he is separated, what does that entail? There is no offence. If they have not elapsed but he perceives that they have elapsed and he is separated, what does that entail? It is a wrongdoing. If they have not elapsed and he perceives that they have not elapsed and he is separated, what does that entail? There is no offence.

Venerable one, is there a case in which a monk separated from that robe and beyond the boundary for more than six nights does not incur a forfeiture offence? Upāli replied: There is. It is as follows: if that monk, upon arrival, finds that any one of the eight kinds of interruption is present.

Venerable one, is there a case in which a monk separated from that robe and beyond the boundary for more than six nights does not incur a forfeiture offence? Upāli replied: There is. It is as follows: if that monk has obtained the vow of non-separation from the robe.

Venerable one, if a monk has consecrated and set aside a Saṅgha robe and is then separated from it and goes beyond the boundary for more than six nights, is it called a separation or not a separation? Upāli replied: It is called a separation.

That robe should be forfeited. It does not constitute a forfeiture offence; it is to be confessed as a transgression alone.

Venerable one, if a monk stores a robe in a house and then goes up into the sky and remains beyond six nights, what does that become? It becomes a forfeiture offence.

Venerable one, if a monk stores a robe in a house and, having remained beyond six nights separated from that robe, night falls and he is received as a nun, what does that become? It is a wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if a nun stores a robe in a house and, having remained beyond six nights separated from that robe, night falls and she is received as a monk, what does that become? It becomes a forfeiture offence.

Venerable one, if one who has been given a training precept remains beyond six nights separated from that robe, what does that become? It becomes a forfeiture offence. If a nun brings about the separation, it is a wrongdoing. For a female probationer, a novice monk, and a novice nun there is no downfall. For those in whom a prior downfall has arisen and so forth, it is a wrongdoing.

A summary verse: By messenger, letter, hand-signal, and having another speak on one's behalf, through an intermediary; one who is mad, distracted, overwhelmed by feeling, or mute; a fool, one asleep, one who stammers, one utterly drunk; one in meditative absorption, a traveller abroad, an outsider — such are the cases, just as with a monk.

The venerable Upāli asked the Blessed One, the Buddha: Venerable one, the Blessed One has declared that a monk, when one month of the hot season remains, should seek rains-retreat cloth; and that when half a month of the rains-retreat months remains, he should take it up and hold it. What does it mean that you have declared it a forfeiture offence if a monk seeks rains-retreat cloth before one month of the hot season remains, or holds it after half a month of the rains-retreat months has passed?

Vinaya Uttaragrantha, the eighth fascicle.

Venerable one, if a monk seeks rains-retreat cloth too far from the messenger and obtains the robe, what does that become? It is a wrongdoing. If he holds it for too long, what does that become? It becomes a forfeiture offence. Just as with a messenger, so too with a letter, a hand-signal, having another speak on one's behalf, and through an intermediary — it is the same in each case. In every case where a monk seeks even a little too far and holds it too long, it becomes a forfeiture offence throughout.

Venerable one, if a monk seeks rains-retreat cloth too far from a madman and obtains the robe, what does that become? It is a wrongdoing. If he holds it for too long, what does that become? It becomes a forfeiture offence. Just as with a madman, so too with one who is distracted, one overcome by feeling, a fool, one who is mute, one asleep, one engaged in manual work, one utterly drunk, one in meditative absorption, one residing elsewhere on a journey, and outsiders from beyond this community — tīrthikas, wandering ascetics (śramaṇas), brahmins, wandering practitioners, and roaming mendicants — if the robe is sought too far from them and obtained, what does that become? It is a wrongdoing. If held too long, what does that become? It becomes a forfeiture offence.

A summary verse: Improper cases and defining characteristics, likewise those held in common, those not yet risen, and cotton cloth — in groups of four, just as with a monk.

Venerable one, is there a case in which a monk seeks rains-retreat cloth too far away and holds it too long yet does not incur a forfeiture offence? Upāli replied: There is.

This is the case: if the cloth is of proper fiber but becomes improper through spinning, or if the fiber is improper but becomes proper through spinning, or if both are improper — such is one instance.

As for improper material: if rains-retreat cloth consisting of a great piece of cotton cloth mixed with camel hair, ox hair, deer wool, or goat wool, or that has not been properly woven — if such cloth is sought too far away and held too long, what does that become? It is a wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if a monk has sought rains-retreat cloth in a great piece of cotton too far away, and on the basis of that seeking becomes known as someone who has done so, and then becomes a nun — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if the Blessed One has sought rains-retreat cloth in a great piece of cotton too far away, and on the basis of that seeking becomes known, and then becomes a monk — what does that become? It becomes a forfeiture offence.

Venerable one, if a monk seeks a great piece of cotton cloth held in common as rains-retreat cloth too far away and holds it too long — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if one who has not yet received full ordination has accepted rains-retreat cloth and completes it before full ordination, and then holds it too long — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing.

If one who has not yet received full ordination has accepted it and completes it at the moment of full ordination, and holds it too long — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing.

If one who has not yet received full ordination has accepted it and completes it after full ordination, and holds it too long — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing.

If one who is at the point of full ordination has accepted it and completes it at the point of full ordination, and holds it too long — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing.

If one who is at the point of full ordination has accepted it and completes it after full ordination, and holds it too long — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing.

If one who has already received full ordination has accepted it and completes it after full ordination, and holds it too long — what does that become? It becomes a forfeiture offence.

If one who has already received full ordination has accepted it and completes it before full ordination has been received, and holds it too long — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if someone who had not sought rains-retreat cloth seeks it too far away and holds it too long — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if a piece of silk is sought too far away as rains-retreat cloth in the form of a great piece of cotton cloth, and held too long — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if a monk, perceiving a human as a human, seeks a great piece of cotton cloth as rains-retreat cloth too far away from that person and obtains it, and then holds it too long — what does that become? It becomes a forfeiture offence.

If, perceiving a human as not a human, what does that become? It is a wrongdoing.

If, perceiving a non-human as a human, what does that become? It is a wrongdoing.

If, perceiving a non-human as a non-human, what does that become? It is a wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if a monk, perceiving that more than half a month has passed after the invitation ceremony (pravāraṇā), holds rains-retreat cloth — what does that become? It becomes a forfeiture offence.

If it has passed but one perceives it as not having passed — what does that become? There is no offence.

If it has not passed but one perceives it as having passed — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing.

If it has not passed and one perceives it as not having passed — what does that become? There is no offence.

Venerable one, if monks who had committed to keeping the rains retreat are dispersed by a king, how should those monks perform the invitation ceremony? Just as when those nearby had committed to the rains retreat, so it should be done. How should the rains-retreat cloth be held? Just as when the invitation ceremony was performed, so it should be held.

Venerable one, if one who has given up the training seeks rains-retreat cloth in a great piece of cotton too far away and holds it too long — what does that become?

It becomes a transgression involving forfeiture.

Venerable one, if a nun seeks rains-retreat cloth in a great piece of cotton too far away and holds it too long — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing. If she holds it beyond ten days, it becomes a transgression involving forfeiture. If a trainee nun, a novice monk, a novice nun, or one who has previously incurred a downfall, and so forth, seeks rains-retreat cloth in a great piece of cotton too far away and holds it too long — what does that become? It becomes a wrongdoing.

Summary verse:

By messenger, by letter, by hand-signal,
by imputing the matter to another, by relay —
by one who is mad, distracted,
in pain, or dull-witted.

The venerable Upāli (the close attendant) asked the Blessed One, the Buddha: Venerable one, the Blessed One has declared that if a monk knowingly diverts to an individual for his own use gains that have been dedicated to the Saṅgha, it becomes a transgression involving forfeiture — what does that mean?

Venerable one, if a monk knowingly causes a messenger to divert to an individual for his own use gains that have been dedicated to the Saṅgha — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing. If the gains are received, what does that become? It is a wrongdoing only. Just as with a messenger, so too it is the same whether done by letter, by hand-signal, by imputing the matter to another, or by relay.

Venerable one, if a monk knowingly causes gains that have been dedicated to the Saṅgha by a mad member of the Saṅgha to be diverted to an individual for his own use — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing. If the gains are received, what does that become? It is a wrongdoing only. Just as it is with one who is mad, so too — what does it become with one who is distracted, one who is afflicted by pain, one who is dull-witted, and one who is mute? It is a wrongdoing.

Summary verse:

What is unsuitable and what is unfit,
the four categories of parents, the marks,
laypeople and so forth,
those going and those training — the nun.

Venerable one, if a monk causes a Dharma robe that is dedicated to the Saṅgha but is less than a cubit in extent to be diverted to an individual for his own use — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing. If the Dharma robe is completed, what does that become? It is a wrongdoing only. However, if it is fully a cubit or more in extent, it becomes a transgression involving forfeiture.

Venerable one, is there a case where a monk who causes gains dedicated to the Saṅgha to be diverted to himself even while those gains are being sought does not incur a transgression involving forfeiture? The close attendant replied: There is such a case. It is as follows: if the Dharma robe is fit in terms of the weave but the material is unfit, or if the material is fit but the weave is unfit, or if both are unfit — in those cases. What is unfit is this: a Dharma robe mixed with down of a drum, hair of cattle, wool of wild animals, or wool of goats — if such a robe is caused to be diverted to an individual for his own use, what does that become? It is a wrongdoing. If the Dharma robe is completed, what does that become? It is a wrongdoing only.

Venerable one, regarding what the Blessed One declared — that if a monk knowingly diverts to an individual for his own use gains that have been dedicated to the Saṅgha, it becomes a transgression involving forfeiture — is there a case where a monk who takes a Dharma robe from his mother and father incurs a transgression involving forfeiture? The close attendant replied: There is such a case. It is as follows: if the mother and father have dedicated the Dharma robe to the Saṅgha, and the monk knowingly causes it to be diverted to an individual for his own use — if the Dharma robe is then completed, it becomes a transgression involving forfeiture.

Venerable one, if one who has not yet received full ordination takes the gains of the Saṅgha and those gains are completed before he has received full ordination — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing. If he takes them before receiving full ordination and the gains are completed at the very moment of receiving full ordination — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing. If he takes them before receiving full ordination and the gains are completed after he has received full ordination — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if one takes the gains of the Saṅgha at the very moment of receiving full ordination and they are completed at the very moment of receiving full ordination — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing. If one takes them at the very moment of receiving full ordination and they are completed after receiving full ordination — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing. If one takes them after receiving full ordination and they are completed after receiving full ordination — what does that become? It is a transgression involving forfeiture. If one takes them after receiving full ordination and they are completed before full ordination has been received — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if a monk, perceiving as dedicated that which has been dedicated to the Saṅgha, diverts those gains to himself personally — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing. If the gains are completed, what does that become? It is a transgression involving forfeiture. If one perceives as not dedicated that which has in fact been dedicated — what does that become? There is no transgression. If one perceives as dedicated that which has not been dedicated — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing. If one perceives as not dedicated that which has not been dedicated — what does that become? There is no transgression.

Venerable one, if a monk, knowingly diverting gains dedicated to the Saṅgha to himself personally, on account of that act undergoes a change of sex and then receives them as a nun — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing. Venerable one, if a nun, knowingly diverting gains dedicated to the Saṅgha to herself personally, on account of that act undergoes a change of sex and then receives them as a monk — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing.

Venerable one, if a monk diverts to himself personally gains that have been dedicated to a layman — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing. If the gains are completed, what does that become? It is a wrongdoing only.

Likewise, if he does so with gains dedicated to a laywoman, or to a hermaphrodite, or to an expelled nun, or to one who has killed her mother, or to one who has killed his father, or to one who has killed an arhat, or to one who has caused a schism in the Saṅgha, or to one who with malicious intent has shed the blood of a Tathāgata, or to a non-Buddhist, or to one who has entered a non-Buddhist order, or to one who abides as a thief within the Saṅgha, or to one who abides separately, or to one who refuses to abide, or to one who has made an acknowledgment, or to one who has previously committed a downfall, or to one who was at the outset incompatible, or to one who has been given the training rules and to whom gains have been dedicated — if he diverts those gains to himself personally, what does that become? It is a wrongdoing. If the gains are completed, what does that become? It is a wrongdoing only.

Venerable one, if a monk diverts to himself personally gains dedicated to those abiding in another sphere of existence — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing. If the gains are completed, what does that become? It is a wrongdoing only.

Venerable one, if one who has been given the training rules, while requesting gains dedicated to the Saṅgha, diverts them to himself personally — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing. If the gains are completed, what does that become? It is a transgression involving forfeiture.

Venerable one, if a nun, while requesting gains dedicated to the community of nuns, diverts them to herself personally — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing. If the gains are completed, what does that become? It is a transgression involving forfeiture.

Venerable one, if a monk diverts to himself personally gains dedicated to two monks or to many monks — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing. If the gains are completed, what does that become? It is a wrongdoing only.

Venerable one, if a monk diverts to himself personally gains dedicated to the community of nuns or to the community of both — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing. If the gains are completed, what does that become? It is a wrongdoing only.

Venerable one, if a monk diverts to himself personally gains dedicated to a nun, or to a probationer nun, or to a male novice or a female novice — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing. If the gains are completed, what does that become? It is a wrongdoing only.

Likewise, if a nun diverts to herself personally gains dedicated to a monk, or to a nun, or to a probationer nun, or to a male novice, or to a female novice — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing. If the gains are completed, what does that become? It is a wrongdoing only. Likewise, for a probationer nun, a male novice, a female novice, and those who have previously incurred a transgression, and so on — the wrongdoing is to be stated extensively, as before.

Summary verse:

The unsuitable, the sets of four,
the lapse, and likewise life-long medicines,
night medicines, the same, the mad,
the unconsecrated, the consecrated.

The venerable Upāli asked the Blessed One, the Buddha: Venerable Blessed One, you have declared that the medicines given by the Blessed One for sick monks to be used individually — namely, clarified butter, oil from grains, honey, and raw sugar — may be consecrated by the sick monk himself for seven days, stored, and used as one uses what is stored; and that if one uses them beyond that, it becomes a forfeiture offence. Venerable one, is there a case in which a medicine kept beyond seven days does not become a forfeiture?

Upāli, there is. It is medicine that has been mixed with something unsuitable, such that it is no longer fit for monks to use.

Venerable one, suppose a monk obtains such medicine and, perceiving something that is seven days old as being seven days old, causes the dawn to break while still holding it — what does that become? It becomes a forfeiture offence.

If he perceives something that is seven days old as not being seven days old — what does that become? There is no offence.

If he perceives something that is not seven days old as being seven days old — what does that become? It is a wrongdoing.

If he perceives something that is not seven days old as not being seven days old and causes the dawn to break while still holding it — what does that become? There is no offence.

Venerable one, if a monk incurs a forfeiture in respect of medicine and then lapses — should he confess the transgression?

Upāli, he should confess it.

After confessing the transgression, if he receives it again — does he forfeit it?

Upāli, no he does not.

Venerable one, is there a case in which a monk may keep seven-day medicine for as long as he lives without it becoming a forfeiture offence?

Upāli, there is — provided that it has been formally processed within seven days.

Venerable one, suppose a monk obtains some seven-day medicine and, through keeping it beyond seven days, undergoes a change of ordination status and becomes a nun — what does that become? It becomes a forfeiture offence.

Venerable one, suppose a nun obtains some seven-day medicine and, through keeping it beyond seven days, undergoes a change of ordination status and becomes a monk — what does that become? It becomes a forfeiture offence.

Venerable one, is there a case in which the very same medicine is forfeited and yet that same medicine has also been formally stored?

Upāli, there is — namely, when a monk, having received medicine kept beyond seven days, causes it to exceed the permitted time.

Venerable one, suppose a monk obtains some seven-day medicine and his mind becomes distracted — from what point should he count the seven days?

From the moment his own mind returns to clarity.

Venerable one, regarding timely medicines, medicines for a fixed period, seven-day medicines, and life-long medicines — if one receives these without consecrating them, and then appropriates them for oneself and uses them as stored goods — is that permissible?

Upāli, it is not permissible.

Venerable one, regarding timely medicines, medicines for a fixed period, seven-day medicines, and

life-long medicines — if one consecrates them and then stores them as kept goods and uses them in that condition, is that permissible?

Upāli, it is permissible.

Venerable one, if a monk consecrates medicines that have been relinquished, what comes of that?

It is an offence.

Venerable one, regarding timely medicines, medicines for a fixed period, seven-day medicines, and life-long medicines — if one receives them, consecrates them, and then, once the consecration has expired, stores and uses them, is that permissible?

Upāli, it is not permissible. Timely medicines are to be used within their proper time; they are not to be used once that time has passed. Medicines for a fixed period are to be used within that fixed period; they are not to be used once that period has passed. Seven-day medicines are to be used within the seven days; they are not to be used once the seven days have passed. Life-long medicines are to be used for as long as one lives.

If one uses them beyond their time, what comes of that?

Using them after storing them causes a transgression.

Venerable one, if one consecrates them, what comes of that?

It is an offence.

Venerable one, if the precept has been given and a monk uses seven-day medicine after the seven days have passed, what comes of that?

It incurs a transgression requiring relinquishment.

Venerable one, if a nun uses seven-day medicine after the seven days have passed, what comes of that?

It incurs a transgression requiring relinquishment. For a female trainee it is an offence. For a novice monk and a novice nun there is no transgression. As for those in whom a previous transgression has already arisen and so forth, it should be explained at length as an offence.

The questions asked by Upāli on transgressions requiring relinquishment, drawn from the Vinaya of Kaśmir, are complete.

A summary verse:

Messenger, letter, and gesture;
imputing words to another, and through an intermediary;
a mad person, one whose mind is distracted,
one overcome by feeling, and one who is dull-witted.
Asleep, vomiting, deeply intoxicated,
one in meditative absorption, and one who has gone elsewhere — these are the last.

Venerable Upāli then asked the Blessed One, the buddha: Venerable one, the Blessed One has taught that knowingly speaking a falsehood is a transgression. Venerable one, if a monk, acting as a messenger, speaks a falsehood, what comes of that? It is an offence. Likewise, what if he does so through a written letter, through a gesture, by imputing words to another, or through an intermediary? It is an offence.

Venerable one, if a monk speaks a falsehood to a madman, what comes of that? It is an offence. Likewise, what if he does so to one whose mind is distracted, to one overcome by feeling, to one who is dull-witted, to one who is deaf, to one who is asleep, to one who is occupied with tasks, to one who is deeply intoxicated, to one who has entered meditative absorption, or to one who is dwelling elsewhere? It is an offence.

A summary verse:

Solitude and speech, and groupings of four;
a non-Buddhist adherent, and speaking nothing at all;
marks, and bodily communication;
giving the precept, and a nun.

Venerable one, if a monk, perceiving a secluded place as secluded, speaks a falsehood there, what comes of that? It is an offence. If, perceiving a secluded place as not secluded, he speaks a falsehood, what comes of that? It is an offence. If, perceiving a non-secluded place as secluded, he speaks a falsehood, what comes of that? It is an offence. If, perceiving a non-secluded place as not secluded, he speaks a falsehood, what comes of that? It incurs a transgression.

Venerable one, if a monk from the central region speaks a falsehood to a monk from the central region in the language of a border region, and the monk from the central region does not understand that border-region language, what comes of that? It is an offence.

If a monk from a border region speaks a falsehood to a monk from a border region in the language of the central region, and that border-region monk does not understand the central-region language, what comes of that? It is an offence. If a monk from a border region speaks a falsehood to a monk from the central region in the language of a border region, and the monk from the central region does not understand that border-region language, what comes of that? It is an offence. If a monk from the central region speaks a falsehood to a monk from a border region in the language of the central region, and that border-region monk does not understand the central-region language, what comes of that? It is an offence.

Venerable one, if a monk speaks a falsehood to a human being while perceiving that person as human, what comes of that? It is a transgression. If he speaks a falsehood while perceiving a human being as not human, what comes of that? It is an offence. If he speaks a falsehood while perceiving a non-human being as human, what comes of that? It is an offence. If he speaks a falsehood while perceiving a non-human being as not human, what comes of that? It is an offence.

Venerable one, if a monk asks another monk "Who are you?" and that monk replies "I am a non-Buddhist (tīrthika)" — should one say he has surrendered his training, or that it does not quite amount to that? What comes of it? By having spoken a deliberate falsehood, it is a transgression. Venerable one, if a monk asks another monk "Are you then a non-Buddhist?" and that other monk, rather than speaking, assents by silence, what comes of that? It is an offence.

Venerable one, when a monk is in the midst of speaking a falsehood and, while speaking, the gender changes — if he turns out to be a nun, what comes of that? It is a transgression. Venerable one, when a nun is in the midst of speaking a falsehood and, while speaking, the gender changes — if she turns out to be a monk, what comes of that? It is a transgression.

Venerable one, if a monk asks another monk "Did you see a particular woman?" and that monk, not having seen her, indicates by a bodily gesture that he did see her, what comes of that? It is an offence.

Venerable one, if one who has received the training speaks a falsehood, what comes of that? It is a transgression. Venerable one, if a nun speaks a falsehood, what comes of that? It is a transgression. Likewise, for a female trainee, a male novice, a female novice, and for those who have already incurred a previous downfall and so forth — it is an offence.

Summary verse:

By messenger, by letter, by gesture,
by imputing a name to another, by relay,
the insane, the distracted,
one overcome by feeling, the dull-witted,
one who is asleep, one who is vomiting, one who is thoroughly drunk,
and one entering meditative absorption — these are the last cases.

The venerable Upāli asked the Blessed One, the buddha: "Venerable one, the Blessed One declared that speaking through a human's fault incurs a transgression. If a monk acting as messenger causes another monk to speak through a human's fault, what comes of that? It is an offence. Just as with a messenger, so too through a letter, through a gesture, through imputing a name to another, and through a relay — what comes of that? It is an offence.

Venerable one, if a monk causes a mad monk to speak through a human's fault, what comes of that? It is an offence. Likewise, if one causes a distracted monk, one overcome by feeling, one who is asleep, one who is engaged in manual work, one who is thoroughly drunk, or one in meditative absorption to speak through a human's fault, what comes of that? It is an offence. Venerable one, if a monk causes a person who has gone forth and is residing in another destination to speak through a human's fault, what comes of that? It is an offence.

Summary verse:

Language, body, seclusion,
the gender cases, and the group of four,
laypersons and so forth,
the surrender of training, and the nun.

Venerable one, if a monk from the central region, speaking in the language of a border region, causes a monk from the central region to speak through his own fault, and the monk from the central region does not understand that border-region language, what comes of that? It is an offence.

Venerable one, if a monk from a border region, speaking in the language of the central region, causes a monk from a border region to speak through a human's fault, and the monk from the border region does not understand that central-region language, what comes of that? It is an offence.

Venerable one, if a monk from a border region, speaking in the language of a border region, causes a monk from the central region to speak through a human's fault, and the monk from the central region does not understand that border-region language, what comes of that? It is an offence.

Venerable one, if a monk from the central region, speaking in the language of the central region, causes a monk from a border region to speak through a human's fault, and the monk from the border region does not understand that central-region language, what comes of that? It is an offence.

Venerable one, if a monk causes a person to speak through a human's fault by means of a bodily gesture, what comes of that? It is an offence.

Venerable one, if a monk, perceiving a secluded place as secluded, causes a person to speak through a human's fault, what comes of that? It is an offence. If, perceiving a secluded place as not secluded, he causes a person to speak through a human's fault, what comes of that? It is an offence. If, perceiving a non-secluded place as secluded, he causes a person to speak through a human's fault, what comes of that? It is an offence. If, perceiving a non-secluded place as not secluded, he causes a person to speak through a human's fault, what comes of that? It is a transgression.

Venerable one, if a monk causes a monk to speak through a human's fault, and in the course of that the monk changes gender and becomes a nun, what comes of that? It is an offence. Venerable one, if a nun causes a nun to speak through a human's fault, and in the course of that the nun changes gender and becomes a monk, what comes of that? It is an offence.

Venerable one, if a monk, perceiving one who has received full ordination as having received full ordination, causes that person to speak through a human's fault, what comes of that? It is a transgression. If he perceives one who has received full ordination as not having received full ordination, what comes of that? It is an offence. If he perceives one who has not received full ordination as having received full ordination, what comes of that? It is an offence. If he perceives one who has not received full ordination as not having received full ordination, what comes of that? It is an offence.

Venerable one, if a monk, perceiving a human as a human, causes that person to speak through a human's fault, what comes of that? It is a transgression. If he perceives a human as not a human, what comes of that? It is an offence. If he perceives a non-human as not a human, what comes of that? It is an offence. If he perceives a non-human as a human, what comes of that? It is an offence.

Venerable one, if a monk causes a layperson to speak through a human's fault, what comes of that? It is an offence.

Venerable one, if a monk causes a eunuch to speak through a human's fault, what comes of that? It is an offence. Likewise, if he causes to speak through a human's fault one who is a hermaphrodite, or a eunuch, or one who has two sets of characteristics, or one who has taken up a commitment, or one who has expelled a nun, or one who has not killed his mother, or one who has killed his father, or one who has killed an arhat, or a non-Buddhist adherent (tīrthika), or one who has joined the non-Buddhists, or one who abides in the guise of a thief, or one who does not abide, or one who was originally incompatible, or one in whom a downfall had previously arisen — what comes of that? It is an offence.

Venerable one, if a monk causes to speak through a human's fault a monk who has caused a schism in the Saṅgha, or a monk who with malicious intent has drawn blood from a Tathāgata, or one who resides separately, or one who has surrendered his training — what comes of that? It is a transgression.

Venerable one, if one who has surrendered his training causes a monk to speak through a human's fault, what comes of that? There is no transgression.

Venerable one, if a monk causes a nun, a female probationer, a novice monk, or a novice nun to speak through a human's fault, what comes of that? It is an offence.

Venerable one, if a nun causes a nun to speak through a human's fault, what comes of that? It is a transgression.

Venerable one, if a nun causes a monk, a novice monk, a female probationer, or a novice nun to speak through a human's fault, what comes of that? It is an offence.

As for the female probationer, the novice monk, the novice nun, and the rest — beginning with the case of a prior transgression — the offence should be stated in full detail.

The venerable Upāli then asked the Blessed One, the buddha: Venerable one, what exactly did the Blessed One mean when he declared that if a monk engages in slander it is a transgression? The remaining text is to be set forth in full for slander just as it was stated for causing to speak through a human's fault.

Here ends the Vinayottaragrantha, the ninth fascicle.

Summary verse: Voice, body, and privacy; distinctive marks and groups of four; laypeople and so forth; and surrendering the training — these are the last.

The venerable Upāli then asked the Blessed One, the buddha: Venerable one, what exactly did the Blessed One mean when he declared that a monk who, with the agreement of the Saṅgha and in accordance with the Dharma, is engaged in settling a dispute may also obstruct a communal act?

Venerable one, if a central-region monk, speaking in a frontier dialect, seeks to have a dispute of central-region monks settled with the agreement of the Saṅgha and in accordance with the Dharma, and yet also obstructs the communal act, and if the central-region monks do not seek to understand that frontier dialect, what comes of that? It is an offence.

If a frontier monk, speaking in the central-region dialect, seeks to have a dispute of frontier monks settled with the agreement of the Saṅgha and in accordance with the Dharma, and yet also obstructs the communal act, and if the frontier monks do not seek to understand the central-region dialect, what comes of that? It is an offence.

If a frontier monk, speaking in a frontier dialect, seeks to have a dispute of central-region monks settled with the agreement of the Saṅgha and in accordance with the Dharma, and yet also obstructs the communal act, and if the central-region monks do not seek to understand that frontier dialect, what comes of that? It is an offence.

If a central-region monk, speaking in the central-region dialect, seeks to have a dispute of frontier monks settled with the agreement of the Saṅgha and in accordance with the Dharma, and yet also obstructs the communal act, and if the frontier monks do not seek to understand the central-region dialect, what comes of that? It is an offence.

Venerable one, if a monk, through a bodily act of expression, seeks to have a dispute settled with the agreement of the Saṅgha and in accordance with the Dharma, and yet also obstructs the communal act, what comes of that? It is an offence.

Venerable one, if a monk, while seeking to have a dispute settled with the agreement of the Saṅgha and in accordance with the Dharma, obstructs the communal act, perceiving what is a private assembly as a private assembly, what comes of that? It is an offence.

If he perceives what is a private assembly as not a private assembly, what comes of that? It is an offence.

If he perceives what is not a private assembly as a private assembly, what comes of that? It is an offence.

If he perceives what is not a private assembly as not a private assembly, what comes of that? There is no transgression.

Venerable one, if a monk, while seeking to have a dispute settled with the agreement of the Saṅgha and in accordance with the Dharma, obstructs the communal act, and by that occasion changes sex and becomes a nun, what comes of that? It is an offence.

Venerable one, if a nun, while seeking to have a dispute settled with the agreement of the Saṅgha and in accordance with the Dharma, obstructs the communal act, and by that occasion changes sex and becomes a monk, what comes of that? It is an offence.

Venerable one, if a monk obstructs the settling of a dispute in accordance with the Dharma, while perceiving the settling to be in accordance with the Dharma, what comes of that? It is a transgression.

Venerable one, if a monk obstructs the settling of a dispute in accordance with the Dharma, while perceiving something settled in accordance with the Dharma as not settled in accordance with the Dharma, what comes of that? It is a transgression.

If a monk obstructs the settling of a dispute in accordance with the Dharma, while perceiving something not settled in accordance with the Dharma as settled in accordance with the Dharma, what comes of that? It is a transgression.

If a monk obstructs the settling of a dispute in accordance with the Dharma, while perceiving something not settled in accordance with the Dharma as not settled in accordance with the Dharma, what comes of that? It is a transgression.

Venerable one, if a monk, while seeking to have a dispute settled in accordance with the Dharma with the agreement of the Saṅgha, obstructs the communal act, perceiving those who are fully ordained as fully ordained, what comes of that? It is an offence involving defeat.

If a monk obstructs such a communal act, perceiving those who are fully ordained as not fully ordained, what comes of that? It is a transgression.

If a monk obstructs such a communal act, perceiving those who are not fully ordained as fully ordained, what comes of that? It is a transgression.

If a monk obstructs such a communal act, perceiving those who are not fully ordained as not fully ordained, what comes of that? It is a transgression.

Venerable one, if a monk, while seeking to have a dispute of laypeople settled in accordance with the Dharma, obstructs the communal act, what comes of that? It is a transgression.

Venerable one, if a monk, while seeking to have the disputes of the following settled in accordance with the Dharma, obstructs the communal act — namely those of: a monk who is a hermaphrodite, a pandaka, a person of dual sex, one who has undertaken vows voluntarily, one who has expelled a nun, one who has killed his mother, one who has killed his father, one who has killed an arhat, a tīrthika, one who has joined a tīrthika community, one who dwells by stealth, one who is unable to remain, one who was initially discordant, and one who has previously committed a downfall — what comes of that? It is a transgression.

Venerable one, if a monk, while seeking to have the disputes of the following settled in accordance with the Dharma, obstructs the communal act — namely those of: one who has caused a schism in the Saṅgha, one who with malicious intent has drawn blood from a Tathāgata, one who dwells separately, and those to whom a training has been given — what comes of that? It is an offence involving defeat.

A summary verse:

Through a messenger, a letter, a gesture,
imputing fault to another, through an intermediary,
one who is insane, one whose mind is disturbed,
one who has returned from unconsciousness, and one who has gone away.

Venerable one, if a monk, while seeking to have a dispute settled in accordance with the Dharma, obstructs the communal act through a messenger, what comes of that? It is a transgression. Likewise through a letter, through a gesture, through imputing fault to another, and through an intermediary — what comes of that? It is a transgression.

Venerable one, if a monk obstructs the communal act in the settling of disputes of monks who are insane in accordance with the Dharma, what comes of that? It is a transgression. Likewise in the case of those whose minds are disturbed, those prostrated by illness, those who are senile, those who have returned from unconsciousness, and those who have gone forth and are dwelling elsewhere — if a monk obstructs the settling of their disputes in accordance with the Dharma, what comes of that? It is a transgression.

Venerable one, if a monk, while seeking to have the disputes of nuns settled in accordance with the Dharma with the agreement of the Saṅgha, obstructs the communal act, what comes of that? It is a transgression. If he obstructs such an act concerning probationary nuns and novice nuns, what comes of that? It is a transgression. If a nun obstructs the communal act in the settling of disputes of nuns, what comes of that?

It is a transgression. If a nun obstructs the communal act in the settling of disputes of monks, of novices, of probationary nuns, or of novice nuns, what comes of that? It is a misdeed.

Venerable one, what of the rule that was promulgated: if a monk, while seeking to have a dispute settled in accordance with the Dharma with the agreement of the Saṅgha, obstructs the communal act — it is a transgression. As for probationary nuns, novices, novice nuns, and those in whom a prior transgression has arisen and so forth — those should be discussed at length as misdeeds.

Summary verse:

By messenger, by letter, by gesture,
by imputing through another, by relay —
one who is mad, or whose mind is disturbed,
one overwhelmed by feeling, or one who is dull,
one asleep, one busy with work, one deeply intoxicated,
one in meditative absorption, or one dwelling elsewhere — these are the last.

The venerable Upāli addressed the Blessed One, the Buddha: Venerable one, the Blessed One has declared that if a monk teaches the Dharma to a woman with more than five or six words, it is a transgression — unless a learned man is present. What is to be said about that?

Venerable one, if a monk, without a learned man present, teaches the Dharma to a woman with more than five or six words by means of a messenger — what comes of that? It is a misdeed. Likewise, if he does so by letter, by gesture, by imputing through another, or by relay, without a learned man present, and teaches a woman the Dharma with more than five or six words — what comes of that? It is a misdeed.

Venerable one, if a monk, without a learned man present, teaches the Dharma to a mad woman with more than five or six words — what comes of that? It is a misdeed. Likewise, if he does so to a woman whose mind is disturbed, to a woman overwhelmed by feeling, to one who is dull, to one who is deaf, to one who is asleep, to one who is occupied with work, to one who is deeply intoxicated, to one who is in meditative absorption, or to one dwelling elsewhere, without a learned man present, and teaches her the Dharma with more than five or six words — what comes of that? It is a misdeed.

Venerable one, if a monk, with a learned man present who is mad, teaches the Dharma to a woman with more than five or six words — what comes of that? It is a misdeed. Likewise, if the learned man present is one whose mind is disturbed, overwhelmed by feeling, dull, deaf, asleep, occupied with work, deeply intoxicated, in meditative absorption, dwelling at the frontier, dwelling elsewhere — and the monk teaches a woman the Dharma with more than five or six words — what comes of that? It is a misdeed.

Summary verse:

By language, by intelligibility, by body, by privacy —
likewise food, a maternal relative,
by night, in a group of four only,
one who has given the precepts, and a nun.

Venerable one, if a monk from the central lands, without a learned man present, teaches the Dharma with more than five or six words to a woman from the central lands in the language of the frontier regions, and that woman from the central lands does not understand the frontier language — what comes of that? It is a misdeed. If a monk from the frontier regions teaches the Dharma with more than five or six words to a woman from the frontier regions in the language of the central lands, without a learned man present, and that woman from the frontier regions does not understand the language of the central lands — what comes of that? It is a misdeed. If a monk from the frontier regions teaches the Dharma with more than five or six words to a woman from the central lands in the language of the frontier regions, without a learned man present, and that woman from the central lands does not understand the frontier language — what comes of that? It is a misdeed. If a monk from the central lands teaches the Dharma with more than five or six words to a woman from the frontier regions in the language of the central lands, without a learned man present, and that woman from the frontier regions does not understand the language of the central lands — what comes of that? It is a misdeed.

Venerable ones, consider this: if a monk from the central lands makes himself understood in the frontier language and teaches the Dharma with more than five or six words to a person from the central lands and a woman, and that person from the central lands does not understand the frontier language — what comes of that? It is a misdeed. If a person from the frontier regions makes himself understood in the language of the central lands and teaches the Dharma to a person from the frontier regions, and that person from the frontier regions does not understand the language of the central lands — what comes of that? It is a misdeed. If a person from the frontier regions makes himself understood in the frontier language and teaches the Dharma with more than five or six words to a person from the central lands, and that person from the central lands does not understand the frontier language — what comes of that? It is a misdeed. If a person from the central lands makes himself understood in the language of the central lands and teaches the Dharma with more than five or six words to a person from the frontier regions and a woman, and that person from the frontier regions does not understand the language of the central lands — what comes of that? It is a misdeed.

Venerable ones, consider this: if a monk teaches the Dharma with more than five or six words to a woman by means of bodily gestures, without a learned man present — what comes of that? It is a misdeed.

Venerable ones, consider this: if a monk perceives a secluded place as secluded and teaches the Dharma with more than five or six words to a woman without a learned man present — what comes of that? It is an offense entailing a fall. If he perceives a secluded place as not secluded and teaches the Dharma with more than five or six words to a woman — what comes of that? There is no transgression. If he perceives a non-secluded place as secluded and teaches the Dharma with more than five or six words to a woman — what comes of that? It is a misdeed. If he perceives a secluded place as not secluded and teaches the Dharma with more than five or six words to a woman — what comes of that? There is no transgression.

Venerable ones, consider this: if a monk teaches the Dharma with more than five or six words to a woman who makes herself understood — what comes of that? It is a misdeed. Likewise, if a monk teaches the Dharma with more than five or six words to a paṇḍaka or a hermaphrodite who makes themselves understood — what comes of that? It is a misdeed.

Venerable ones, consider this: if a monk teaches the Dharma with more than five or six words to a paṇḍaka female without a learned man present — what comes of that? It is a misdeed.

Venerable ones, consider this: if a monk teaches the Dharma with more than five or six words to a woman without a learned man present, and in the course of that teaching her sex changes so that she becomes a nun — what comes of that? There is no transgression. Venerable ones, consider this: if a nun teaches the Dharma with more than five or six words to a man without a learned man present, and in the course of that teaching his sex changes so that he becomes a monk — what comes of that? There is no transgression.

Venerable ones, consider this: if a monk, without a learned man present, teaches the Dharma with more than five or six words to a woman, perceiving her as a woman — what comes of that? It is an offense entailing a fall. If he perceives a woman as not a woman — what comes of that? There is no transgression. If he perceives one who is not a woman as a woman — what comes of that? It is a misdeed. If he perceives one who is not a woman as not a woman and teaches the Dharma with more than five or six words — what comes of that? There is no transgression.

Venerable ones, consider this: if a monk, without a learned man present, teaches the Dharma with more than five or six words to a human woman, perceiving her as human — what comes of that? It is an offense entailing a fall. If he perceives a human as not human — what comes of that? It is a misdeed. If he perceives a non-human as human — what comes of that? It is a misdeed.

If he perceives one who is not human as not human — what comes of that? It is a misdeed.

Venerable ones, consider this: if a monk, performing a permissible act, teaches the Dharma with more than five or six words to both a learned man and a woman together, perceiving them as human — what comes of that? There is no transgression. If he teaches the Dharma perceiving a human as not human — what comes of that? It is a misdeed. If he teaches the Dharma perceiving a non-human as human — what comes of that? There is no transgression. If he teaches the Dharma perceiving a non-human as not human — what comes of that? It is a misdeed.

Venerable ones, consider this: if a monk who has received the training teaches the Dharma with more than five or six words in the domain of women without a learned man present — what comes of that? It is an offense entailing a fall.

Venerable ones, consider this: if a nun teaches the Dharma with more than five or six words to a man without a companion nun present — what comes of that? It is an offense entailing a fall.

As for a female probationer, a novice monk, a novice nun, and those who have previously committed transgressions and so forth — these are to be set forth extensively as misdeeds.

Summary verse:

One who is mad, or of disturbed mind,
one afflicted by feeling, gone elsewhere,
one who has given the training, and so on — language,
marks, and the nun —

The venerable Nyeba Khor inquired of the Blessed One, the buddha: "Venerable one, the Blessed One has declared that it is an offense entailing a fall for a monk to recite Dharma in unison with a person who has not received full ordination — what exactly is that?"

Venerable ones, consider this: if a monk recites Dharma in unison with a person who has not received full ordination and who is mad — what comes of that? It is a misdeed. Likewise, if he recites Dharma in unison with one of disturbed mind, one incapacitated by feeling, one who is simple-minded, one who is mute, or one who dwells in a different realm — what comes of that? It is a misdeed.

Venerable ones, consider this: if a monk recites Dharma in unison with one who has given the training — what comes of that? It is an offense entailing a fall.
