If he recites Dharma in unison with a person who has not received full ordination and who is a woman — what comes of that? It is a misdeed. Likewise, if he recites Dharma in unison with a paṇḍaka (a person of indeterminate sex), with one of two signs, or with a female paṇḍaka — what comes of that? It is a misdeed.

If he recites Dharma in unison with a nun who has been expelled, one who has killed her mother, one who has killed her father, one who has killed an arhat, one who has wounded a Tathāgata with malicious intent, one who has caused a schism in the Saṅgha of monks, one who has gone over to non-Buddhists, one who has entered the way of non-Buddhists, one who dwells as a thief, one who is not to be housed, one who has previously committed transgressions, or one who from the very beginning is of incompatible character — what comes of that? It is an offense entailing a fall.

Venerable ones, consider this: if a monk from the central region recites Dharma in the dialect of the borderlands in unison with a person from the central region, and that person from the central region does not know that dialect of the borderlands — what comes of that? It is a misdeed. If one from the borderlands recites Dharma in the dialect of the central region in unison with a person from the borderlands, and that person from the borderlands does not know the dialect of the central region — what comes of that? It is a misdeed. If one from the borderlands recites Dharma in the dialect of the borderlands in unison with a person from the central region, and that person from the central region does not know the dialect of the borderlands — what comes of that? It is a misdeed. If one from the central region recites Dharma in the dialect of the central region in unison with a person from the borderlands, and that person from the borderlands does not know the dialect of the central region — what comes of that? It is a misdeed.

Venerable ones, if a monk recites Dharma in unison with a person who has not received full ordination, and during that recitation a name arises such that it comes to be the name of a nun — what comes of that? It is a misdeed. Venerable ones, if a nun recites Dharma in unison with a person who has not received full ordination, and during that recitation a name arises such that it comes to be the name of a monk — what comes of that? It is a misdeed. Venerable ones, if a nun recites Dharma in unison with a person who has not received full ordination, and during that recitation a name arises for that unordained person and that name becomes fully established — what comes of that? It is a misdeed.

Venerable ones, if one recites Dharma in unison with a person who has not received full ordination while perceiving that person as one who has not received full ordination — what comes of that? It is a misdeed. If one perceives a person who has not received full ordination as one who has received full ordination — what comes of that? It is a wrong deed. If one perceives a person who has received full ordination as one who has not received full ordination — what comes of that? It is a wrong deed. If one perceives a person who has received full ordination as one who has received full ordination — what comes of that? It is a wrong deed.

Venerable ones, if a monk recites Dharma in unison with a human being while perceiving that being as human — what comes of that? It is a misdeed. If one perceives a human as not human — what comes of that? It is a wrong deed. If one perceives a non-human as not human — what comes of that? It is a wrong deed.

Venerable ones, if a monk recites Dharma in unison with a nun — what comes of that? It is a wrong deed. If he recites in unison with a female trainee (śikṣamāṇā), a male novice, or a female novice — what comes of that? It is a misdeed.

Venerable ones, if a nun recites Dharma in unison with a person who has not received full ordination — what comes of that? It is a misdeed. If a nun recites Dharma in unison with a monk — what comes of that? It is a wrong deed. If she recites in unison with a female trainee, a male novice, or a female novice — what comes of that? It is a misdeed. In the case of a female trainee, a male novice, or a female novice, where a misdeed has already occurred and so forth, it should be declared a wrong deed.

Venerable ones, if one who has been given a training rule recites Dharma in unison with a person who has not received full ordination — what comes of that? It is a misdeed.

Summary verse:

A messenger, a written letter, a hand-signal,
imputing an offense to another, through an intermediary,
one who is mad, one whose mind is disturbed,
one overcome by sensation, one who is dull-witted,
one who is deaf, one who is asleep, one who is exhausted,
one who is thoroughly intoxicated,
one in meditative absorption, one who has gone elsewhere —
these are the last categories.

Then the venerable Upāli and his retinue asked the Blessed One, the Buddha: Venerable Blessed One, you have said that whatever monk declares to a person who has not received full ordination a transgression involving remaining in a bad state is a misdeed, except when the Saṅgha has granted permission — what is the meaning of this?

Venerable ones, if a monk declares to a person who has not received full ordination a transgression involving remaining in a bad state by means of a messenger — what comes of that? It is a wrong deed. Likewise, if he does so by written letter, by hand-signal, by imputing it to another, or through an intermediary — what comes of that? It is a wrong deed.

Venerable ones, if a monk declares to a mad person a transgression involving remaining in a bad state — what comes of that? It is a wrong deed. Likewise, if he does so to one whose mind is disturbed, one who is tormented by sensation, one who is dull-witted, one who is deaf, one who is asleep, one who is occupied with manual work, one who is thoroughly intoxicated, one who is in meditative absorption, or one who is dwelling in another place — what comes of that? It is a wrong deed.

Summary verse:

Voice, body, and solitude itself —

likewise, the four marks, appointment, and the individual themselves, likewise the meal, and the nun.

Venerable ones, if a monk who speaks the central dialect declares a transgression involving remaining in a bad state to a person dwelling in the central region, in the border dialect, and that person dwelling in the central region also does not understand that border dialect — what comes of that? It is a wrong deed. If one who speaks the border dialect declares a transgression involving remaining in a bad state to a person dwelling on the border, in the central dialect, and that person dwelling on the border also does not understand that central dialect — what comes of that? It is a wrong deed. If one who speaks the border dialect declares a transgression involving remaining in a bad state to a person dwelling in the central region, in the border dialect, and that person dwelling in the central region also does not understand that border dialect — what comes of that? It is a wrong deed. If one who speaks the central dialect declares a transgression involving remaining in a bad state to a person dwelling on the border, in the central dialect, and that person dwelling on the border also does not understand that central dialect — what comes of that? It is a wrong deed.

Venerable ones, if a monk declares a transgression involving remaining in a bad state by means of bodily gesture — what comes of that? It is a wrong deed.

Venerable ones, if a monk, perceiving a secluded place as secluded, declares a transgression involving remaining in a bad state there — what comes of that? It is a wrong deed. Venerable ones, if he perceives a secluded place as not secluded and declares a transgression involving remaining in a bad state — what comes of that? It is a wrong deed. If he perceives a non-secluded place as secluded and declares a transgression involving remaining in a bad state — what comes of that? It is a wrong deed. If he perceives a non-secluded place as non-secluded and declares a transgression involving remaining in a bad state — what comes of that? It is an actual downfall.

Venerable ones, if a monk, perceiving a person who has not received full ordination as one who has not received full ordination, declares a transgression involving remaining in a bad state to that person — what comes of that? It is an actual downfall. If he perceives a person who has not received full ordination as one who has received full ordination and declares a transgression involving remaining in a bad state — what comes of that? There is no downfall. If he perceives a person who has received full ordination as one who has not received full ordination and declares a transgression involving remaining in a bad state — what comes of that? It is a wrong deed. If he perceives a person who has received full ordination as one who has received full ordination and declares a transgression involving remaining in a bad state — what comes of that? There is no downfall.

Venerable ones, if a monk, perceiving a human being as a human being, declares a transgression involving remaining in a bad state to that person — what comes of that? It is an actual downfall. If he perceives a human being as not a human being and declares a transgression involving remaining in a bad state — what comes of that? It is a wrong deed. If he perceives a non-human being as a human being and declares a transgression involving remaining in a bad state — what comes of that? It is a wrong deed. If he perceives a non-human being as a non-human being and declares a transgression involving remaining in a bad state — what comes of that? It is a wrong deed.

Venerable ones, if a monk who, in the course of declaring a transgression involving remaining in a bad state to a person who has not received full ordination, undergoes a change of gender and becomes a nun — what comes of that? It is an actual downfall. Venerable ones, if a nun who, in the course of declaring a transgression involving remaining in a bad state to a person who has not received full ordination, undergoes a change of gender and becomes a monk — what comes of that? It is an actual downfall.

Venerable ones, there is the statement that the Blessed One declared: "Any monk who declares a transgression involving remaining in a bad state to a person who has not received full ordination commits an actual downfall, except when the Saṅgha has authorized it." With respect to that, venerable ones — does it follow that even a monk who has been authorized by the Saṅgha commits an actual downfall if he declares a transgression involving remaining in a bad state to a person who has not received full ordination?

The persons in one's immediate circle are these four. What are the four? There is the person to whom one may declare both the extent of a transgression and the extent of one's household connections. There is the person to whom one may declare the extent of a transgression but not the extent of one's household connections. There is the person to whom one may declare the extent of one's household connections but not the extent of a transgression. And there is the person to whom one may declare neither the extent of a transgression nor the extent of one's household connections.

Who is the person to whom one may declare both the extent of a transgression and the extent of one's household connections? It is someone who, by means of minor transgressions, exposes those who have a fixed number of households. Who is the person to whom one may declare the extent of a transgression but not the extent of one's household connections? It is someone who, by means of minor transgressions, exposes those who have no fixed number of households. Who is the person to whom one may declare the extent of one's household connections but not the extent of a transgression? It is someone who, by means of major transgressions, exposes those who have a fixed number of households. Who is the person to whom one may declare neither the extent of a transgression nor the extent of one's household connections? It is someone who, by means of major offences, exposes those who have no fixed number of households.

Now, concerning the person to whom one may declare both the extent of a transgression and the extent of one's household connections: one should declare to only those households one knows about only that transgression one knows about. If one declares beyond that, it becomes an actual downfall. Concerning the person to whom one may declare the extent of a transgression but not the extent of one's household connections: one should declare to all households only that transgression one knows about. If one declares beyond that, it becomes an actual downfall. Concerning the person to whom one may declare the extent of one's household connections but not the extent of a transgression: one should declare all transgressions to only those households one knows about. If one declares beyond that, it becomes an actual downfall. Concerning the person to whom one may declare neither the extent of a transgression nor the extent of one's household connections: one should declare all transgressions to all households, and in doing so there is no downfall.

Venerable ones, if a monk were to declare his own transgression involving remaining in a bad state to a person who has not received full ordination — what comes of that? It is an actual downfall. Venerable ones, if a monk were to declare a nun's transgression involving remaining in a bad state to a person who has not received full ordination — what comes of that? It is an actual downfall. Likewise, if one were to declare to a person who has not received full ordination a transgression involving remaining in a bad state of any category from a change of gender up to and including one with an incompatible condition — what comes of that? It is an actual downfall. Venerable ones, if a monk were to declare to a person who has not received full ordination a transgression involving remaining in a bad state of a monk who has caused a schism in the Saṅgha — what comes of that? It is an actual downfall. Likewise, if a monk who has maliciously drawn blood from a Tathāgata, or a monk who dwells separately, were to declare a transgression involving remaining in a bad state to a person who has not received full ordination — what comes of that? It is an actual downfall. Venerable ones, if a monk were to declare a nun's transgression involving remaining in a bad state to a person who has not received full ordination — what comes of that? It is an actual downfall. Venerable ones, if a monk were to declare a transgression involving remaining in a bad state while being a monk who has been given a training — what comes of that? It is an actual downfall. If a nun were to declare a nun's transgression involving remaining in a bad state to a person who has not received full ordination — what comes of that? It is an actual downfall. Venerable ones, if one who has been given a training were to declare a transgression involving remaining in a bad state to a person who has not received full ordination — what comes of that? It is an actual downfall. Likewise, a female trainee, a novice monk, a novice nun —

For prior occurrences of downfalls and the like, one should declare the wrongdoing at length.

The venerable close retinue asked the Blessed One, the Buddha: "Venerable Blessed One, you have declared that if a monk were to speak to a person who has not received full ordination about a superhuman quality (uttarimanuṣyadharma) — even truthfully — it constitutes a downfall. What exactly does this mean?" The remainder of the truthful declaration of a superhuman quality — apart from what is designated as a buddha or the Teacher — is to be distinguished by means of a defeat. Thus it should be understood.

The Vinayamūla. The tenth fascicle.

Summary verse:

Through a messenger, through a letter, through a hand-signal,
Through slander of another, through an intermediary,
Through a madman, through a mentally disturbed person,
Through one overcome by sensation, through a dull-witted one,
Through one who is asleep, through one occupied with manual work, through one deeply intoxicated,
Through one in meditative absorption, through one who has gone elsewhere — these are the last.

The venerable close retinue asked the Blessed One, the Buddha: "Venerable Blessed One, you have declared that if a monk who has previously made something properly legitimate then afterward says: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — it constitutes a downfall. What does this mean?"

"Venerable one, suppose a monk has previously made something properly legitimate, and then afterward a messenger comes and says: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Likewise, if it is conveyed through a letter, through a hand-signal, through slander of another, or through an intermediary — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable one, suppose a monk has previously made something properly legitimate, and then afterward says to those who are insane: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Likewise, if said to those who are mentally disturbed, to those overcome by sensation, to those who are dull-witted, to those who are deaf-mutes, to those who are asleep, to those occupied with manual work, to those deeply intoxicated, to those in meditative absorption, or to those who have gone and are dwelling elsewhere — 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable one, suppose a monk has previously made something properly legitimate with respect to the acquisition belonging to insane monks, and then afterward says: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Likewise, with respect to the acquisition belonging to those who are mentally distracted and those who are dwelling elsewhere — these too should be declared at length."

Summary verse:

Through language, through the body, through seclusion,
Through a monk's name, and through groups of four,
Through an unsuitable householder, and through one who may not,
Through a later acquisition, and that monk.

"Venerable one, suppose a monk of the central region has previously made something properly legitimate in the dialect of a border region, and then afterward speaks to monks of the central region as follows: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — and the monks of the central region do not understand that border dialect — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Suppose one from a border region has previously made something properly legitimate in the dialect of the central region, and then afterward speaks to monks of the border region: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — and the monks of the border region do not understand the dialect of the central region — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Suppose one from a border region has previously made something properly legitimate in the dialect of the border region, and then afterward speaks to monks of the central region: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — and the monks of the central region do not understand that border dialect — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Suppose one from the central region has previously made something properly legitimate in the dialect of the central region, and then afterward speaks to monks of the border region: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — and the monks of the border region do not understand the dialect of the central region — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable sir, suppose a monk has previously made something properly legitimate, and then afterward indicates by bodily gesture the following: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable sir, suppose a monk has previously made something properly legitimate, and then afterward, perceiving a secluded place as secluded, says: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable sir, suppose a monk has previously made something properly legitimate, and then, perceiving a secluded place as not secluded, says: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Perceiving a non-secluded place as secluded, he says: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Perceiving a non-secluded place as non-secluded, he says: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — what comes of that?"

It is a transgression.

"Venerable sir, suppose a monk has previously made something properly legitimate with respect to the nuns' Saṅgha's acquisition, and then afterward says: 'The noble ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable sir, suppose a nun has previously made something properly legitimate with respect to the monks' Saṅgha's acquisition, and then afterward says: 'The noble ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable sir, suppose a monk says: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — and by reason of that designation he turns out to be a nun — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable sir, suppose a nun says: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — and by reason of that designation she turns out to be a monk — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable sir, suppose a monk, perceiving the fully ordained as fully ordained, says: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — what comes of that?"

It is a transgression.

"Perceiving the fully ordained as not fully ordained, he says: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Perceiving the not fully ordained as fully ordained, he says: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Perceiving the not fully ordained as not fully ordained, he says: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable one, suppose a monk first renders someone suitably praised, and afterward says to laypeople: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

Likewise, the same applies when he says this with respect to monks who have taken vows, to eunuchs, to hermaphrodites, to those with both characteristics, to those who have killed their mothers, to those who have killed their fathers, to those who have killed an arhat, to those who have caused a nun to be expelled, to tīrthikas, to those who have joined the tīrthikas, to those who live by stealth, to those who have previously committed a transgression, and to those who from the outset are unsuitable — saying: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable one, suppose a monk first renders someone suitably praised, and afterward says that to those monks who are creating schism in the Saṅgha, to those who with malicious intent have drawn blood from the Tathāgata, to those who are living apart, and to those who have been given a training — saying: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — what comes of that?"

It is a transgression.

"Venerable one, suppose a monk first renders someone suitably praised, and afterward says concerning the acquisition belonging to laypeople: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

Likewise, the same should be stated at length concerning the acquisition belonging to those monks who have taken vows and to those who from the outset are unsuitable.

"Venerable one, suppose a monk says concerning the acquisition belonging to those who are creating schism in the Saṅgha: 'The venerable ones have dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — what comes of that?"

It is a transgression.

Likewise, when a monk says concerning the acquisition belonging to those who with malicious intent have drawn blood from the Tathāgata and to those who are living apart — saying: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — what comes of that?"

It is a transgression.

"Venerable one, is there a case where a monk has first rendered someone suitably praised, and afterward says: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — and yet no transgression results?"

There is a case approaching that. It is as follows: the robe-cloth is suitable in terms of its fibers but unsuitable in terms of its warp threads; or it is suitable in terms of its warp threads but unsuitable in terms of its fibers; or it is unsuitable in both respects. What is unsuitable means that it is mixed with wool from a dog, from a cow, from a wild deer, or from a goat, or that it has not been processed. When someone says, on account of such robe-cloth: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable one, suppose the Saṅgha has come to obtain a robe-cloth one cubit in size that was not requested. A monk, having first rendered someone suitably praised, then says: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable one, suppose a monk says on behalf of acquisitions other than the acquisition of robe-cloth — saying: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable one, suppose the Saṅgha has given a robe to a certain monk even though it did not come into his presence. Afterwards, someone comes and says: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable one, suppose someone, having first rendered another suitably praised by conferring a training precept, then says: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — what comes of that?"

It is an offense entailing expiation.

"Venerable one, suppose a nun, having first rendered someone suitably praised, then says: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — what comes of that?"

It is an offense entailing expiation.

"Venerable one, suppose a female trainee, a novice, a female novice, or one who has previously incurred a transgression — any of these, having first rendered someone suitably praised, then says: 'The venerable ones have, out of friendly regard, dedicated the Saṅgha's acquisition to an individual' — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

Summary verse: Through a messenger, written word, or hand-sign; through attributing words to another, or through an intermediary; for one who is mad, or mentally disturbed; for one overcome by pain, or dull-witted; for one who is mute, asleep, occupied with work, thoroughly intoxicated, in meditative absorption, or one who has gone elsewhere — these are the cases.

The venerable Upāli then asked the Blessed One, the Buddha: "Venerable one, Blessed One, you have declared that when a monk reciting the Prātimokṣa sūtra at each half-month recitation says: 'What use is it to recite the Prātimokṣa sūtra when there are these trifling, minor rules of training that cause monks to feel remorse, displeasure, harm, injury, and distress?' — and thereby denigrates the training, it is an offense entailing expiation. Regarding that: venerable one, suppose a monk denigrates the training through a messenger — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Similarly, what if it is done through a written word, a hand-sign, through attributing words to another, or through an intermediary — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable one, suppose a monk denigrates the training to a madman — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Similarly, what if done to one whose mind is disturbed, one overcome by pain, one who is dull-witted, one who is mute, one who is asleep, one who is occupied with work, one who is thoroughly intoxicated, one who is in meditative absorption, or one who is dwelling elsewhere — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

Summary verse: By speech, by body, or in seclusion; likewise by characteristic mark; to householders and others; to nuns — the training is given.

"Venerable one, suppose a monk of the central region denigrates the training to a monk of the central region, speaking in the dialect of a border region, and the monk of the central region does not understand that border dialect — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Suppose a monk of a border region denigrates the training to a monk of a border region, speaking in the dialect of the central region, and the monk of the border region does not understand the central dialect — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Suppose a monk of a border region denigrates the training to a monk of the central region, speaking in a border dialect, and the monk of the central region does not understand that border dialect — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Suppose a monk of the central region denigrates the training to a monk of a border region, speaking in the central dialect, and the monk of the border region does not understand the central dialect — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable One, suppose a monk denigrates the training by means of a physical gesture — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable One, suppose a monk denigrates the training, perceiving a secluded place as secluded — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Suppose he denigrates the training, perceiving a secluded place as not secluded — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Suppose he denigrates the training, perceiving a non-secluded place as secluded — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Suppose he denigrates the training, perceiving a non-secluded place as non-secluded — what comes of that?"

It is an offense entailing a formal meeting of the Saṅgha.

"Venerable One, suppose one who has denigrated the training is recognized as such and is taken back as a nun — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable One, suppose a nun who has denigrated the training is recognized as such and is taken back as a monk — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable One, suppose a monk denigrates the training of a layperson — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

Likewise, it is a wrongdoing if one denigrates the training of a monk who has made a commitment, of a eunuch, of a pandaka, of a nun who has been expelled, of one who has not denigrated, of one who has denigrated, of an arhat, of a tīrthika, of one who has entered among the tīrthikas, of one who dwells in concealment, of one against whom a prior offense has arisen, or of one who was originally incompatible.

"Venerable One, suppose a monk denigrates the training involving causing a schism in the monastic Saṅgha, drawing blood from a Tathāgata with malicious intent, living in a separate faction, or the training that has been conferred — what comes of that?"

It is an offense entailing a formal meeting of the Saṅgha.

"Venerable One, suppose a monk denigrates the training pertaining to a conjured form — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable One, suppose a monk denigrates the training of a nun, a probationary nun, a novice monk, or a novice nun — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Suppose a nun denigrates the training of a nun — what comes of that?"

It is an offense entailing a formal meeting of the Saṅgha.

"Suppose a nun denigrates the training of a monk, a probationary nun, a novice monk, or a novice nun — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable One, suppose one who has conferred a training denigrates that training — what comes of that?"

It is an offense entailing a formal meeting of the Saṅgha.

In the case of a probationary nun, a novice monk, a novice nun, and one against whom a prior offense has arisen and so forth, it is a wrongdoing.

Summary verse:

Supernormal power, body, seclusion, dialect,
gravel, roots, blue mud,
flowers, fish scales, lodging and measure,
meat, flowers, leaves,
fruit, bark — the second —
marks and the fourfold grouping, excellence,
single syllables and the sevenfold group are the results.

The venerable Nearby Circle asked the Blessed One, the Buddha: "Venerable One, the Blessed One has declared that if one destroys a community of seeds or a dwelling place of beings, it is an offense entailing a formal meeting of the Saṅgha — what does that mean exactly?"

"Venerable One, suppose a monk intends by supernormal power to fell a tree — what comes of that?"

There is no offense.

"Venerable One, suppose a monk destroys a tree by means of a physical gesture — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable One, suppose a monk, perceiving a secluded place as secluded, orders that a tree be destroyed — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing only — and the fourfold grouping is to be stated in full.

"Venerable One, suppose a monk of the central region, speaking in the dialect of a border region, instructs a monk of the central region to destroy a tree, and that monk of the central region does not understand that border dialect — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing — and likewise the fourfold grouping is to be stated in full.

"Venerable One, suppose a monk scrapes off stone grit — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable One, suppose a monk pulls out a seed whose root has not yet been planted — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable One, suppose a monk scrapes off green mud or grey mud — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable One, suppose a monk stirs up mud and thereby dislodges fish scales and then throws them onto dry ground — what comes of that?"

It is an offense entailing expiation. The same applies to water plants.

"Venerable One, suppose a monk drives living beings from their habitat, burns it, or renders it unsuitable — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable One, suppose a monk destroys birds' nests, drives birds from their nests, or renders them unsuitable — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable One, suppose a monk causes a mole to be scattered about or its flesh to be extracted — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable One, suppose a monk peels off withered bark from a tree — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable One, suppose a monk plucks withered flowers, fruits, or leaves — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable One, suppose a monk is in the act of bending a tree and, while doing so, is recognized as a monk — what comes of that?"

It is an offense entailing expiation.

"Venerable One, suppose a nun is in the act of bending a tree and, while doing so, is recognized as a nun — what comes of that?"

It is an offense entailing expiation.

"Venerable One, suppose a monk, perceiving a seed as a seed, renders it unsuitable — what comes of that?"

It is an offense entailing expiation.

"Suppose a monk, perceiving a seed as something that is not a seed, renders it unsuitable — what comes of that?"

There is no offense.

"Suppose a monk, perceiving something that is not a seed as a seed, renders it unsuitable — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Suppose a monk, perceiving something that is not a seed as something that is not a seed, renders it unsuitable — what comes of that?"

There is no offense.

"Venerable One, suppose a person who has not received full ordination makes preparations to kill a living being, and that living being is killed while the one who gave the order has not yet received full ordination — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Suppose preparations were made by one who has not received full ordination, and the killing occurs while someone is in the process of receiving full ordination — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Suppose preparations were made by one who has not received full ordination, and the killing occurs after full ordination has been completed — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Suppose preparations were made while someone is in the process of receiving full ordination, and the killing occurs while in the process of receiving full ordination — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Suppose preparations were made while in the process of receiving full ordination, and the killing occurs after full ordination has been completed — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Suppose preparations were made after full ordination has been completed, and the killing occurs after full ordination has been completed — what comes of that?"

It is an offense entailing expiation.

"Suppose preparations were made after full ordination has been completed, and the killing occurs before full ordination was completed — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable One, suppose a monk speaks thus: 'If this tree were cut down it would be good, and there would be no offense' — and another person then cuts it down — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable One, suppose a monk swallows something whole without chewing it — what comes of that?"

It is a wrongdoing.

"Venerable One, is there a case in which a monk may consume fruits that have not been rendered suitable without incurring an offense?"

There is something close to that: if one thinks of seeds that are not seeds as not having the character of seeds, one may then consume them.

"Venerable One, is there a case in which a monk may seek to use the five kinds of seed that have been rendered suitable by fire, yet still incur an offense?"

There is something close to that: if the five kinds of seed have been rendered suitable and then are placed on the ground, placed in a vessel, or placed on a rack, and a sprout has subsequently grown from them, then an offense would be incurred.

The summary verse:

Through messenger, letter, hand-gesture,
Through charging another by means of an intermediary,

Through a madman, through one whose mind is disturbed, through one afflicted by feeling, through a traveler, through laypeople and the like, through one who has given up the training, and through a nun.

"Venerable One, if a monk instructs a messenger to destroy a plant, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "Likewise, if he does so by letter, by hand-gesture, by charging another by means of an intermediary, or by instructing through an intermediary to destroy it, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

"Venerable One, if a monk instructs a madman to destroy a plant, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "Likewise, if he instructs one whose mind is disturbed, one overcome by feeling, one who is dull-witted, one who is deaf, or one who is staying elsewhere to destroy a plant, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

"Venerable One, if a monk directly and in person instructs a layman to destroy a plant, what does that entail?" An offense that entails expiation. "Likewise, if he does so to a laywoman, to a eunuch, to an expelled nun, to a matricide, to a patricide, to one who has killed an arhat, to one who has caused a schism in the Saṅgha, to one who has drawn the Tathāgata's blood with malicious intent, to a non-Buddhist adherent, to one who has entered a non-Buddhist sect, to one who is dwelling in concealment, to one who is dwelling apart, to one who is not to be kept in residence, to one in whom a prior offense has arisen, to one who was incompatible from the outset, to one who has made a declaration, or to one who has given up the training — if any of these is instructed directly and in person to destroy a plant, what does that entail?" An offense that entails expiation.

"Venerable One, if one who has given up the training destroys a green plant, or instructs another to destroy it, what does that entail?" An offense that entails expiation. "Venerable One, if a fully ordained nun destroys a green plant or instructs another to destroy it, what does that entail?" An offense that entails expiation. "Venerable One, if a female novice destroys a green plant or instructs another to destroy it, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. As for one in whom a prior offense has arisen and the rest, these are offenses, as before. For novice monks and novice nuns, there is no offense.

The summary verse:

By speech, by body, by solitude,
By marks, by groups of four,
By laypeople and the like,
By those appointed and those not appointed.

The venerable Upāli asked the Blessed Buddha: "Venerable One, what was meant when the Blessed One declared that mocking or ridiculing another constitutes an offense that entails expiation?"

"Venerable One, if a monk who has been ordained in the central region mocks or ridicules, in the dialect of the borderlands, a monk from the central region, and that monk from the central region does not understand that borderland dialect, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "If a monk from the borderlands mocks or ridicules, in the dialect of the central region, a monk from the borderlands, and that monk from the borderlands does not understand that central dialect, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "If a monk from the borderlands mocks or ridicules, in a borderland dialect, a monk from the central region, and that monk from the central region does not understand that borderland dialect, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "If a monk from the central region mocks or ridicules, in a central dialect, a monk from the borderlands, and that monk from the borderlands does not understand that central dialect, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

"Venerable One, if an ordained monk mocks or ridicules another by means of a bodily gesture, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

"Venerable One, if an ordained monk, perceiving a solitary place as not solitary, mocks or ridicules another there, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "If he perceives a solitary place as solitary and mocks or ridicules another there, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "If he perceives a non-solitary place as non-solitary and mocks or ridicules another there, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "If he perceives a non-solitary place as non-solitary and mocks or ridicules another there, what does that entail?" An offense that entails expiation.

"Venerable One, if an ordained monk, in the course of mocking or ridiculing, comes to be identified by a mark —

"What if it turns out to be a nun? An offense has been committed. Venerable One, if an ordained monk, in the course of mocking or ridiculing, comes to be identified by a mark as a nun, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "Venerable One, if a fully ordained monk mocks or ridicules one who has received full ordination, perceiving her as fully ordained, what does that entail?" An offense that entails expiation. "If he perceives one who is fully ordained as fully ordained, what does that entail?" An offense that entails expiation. "If he perceives one who has not received full ordination as fully ordained, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "If he perceives one who is fully ordained as not fully ordained, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "Venerable One, if a fully ordained monk mocks or ridicules a layperson, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "Venerable One, if a fully ordained monk mocks or ridicules one who has voluntarily claimed to be a monk, a hermaphrodite, a eunuch, a nun who has been expelled, one whose mother has died, one whose father has died, one who has killed an arhat, a tīrthika, one who has entered among the tīrthikas, one who lives by stealth, one in whom a previous transgression has arisen, or one who was unsuitable from the very beginning, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "Venerable One, if a fully ordained monk mocks or ridicules a monk who causes a schism in the Saṅgha, one who, with malicious intent, draws blood from a Tathāgata, one who lives apart, or one who has returned the training, what does that entail?" An offense that entails expiation. "Venerable One, if a fully ordained monk mocks or ridicules those who have been designated to perform the Saṅgha's appointed duties — those appointed and those not appointed excepted — what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

A summary verse:

By messenger, by letter, by gesture,
By attributing names to another, by intermediary,
By one who is mad, by one mentally disturbed,
By one overcome by sensation, by one who is confused,
By one who is asleep, by one who is distracted, by one utterly intoxicated,
By one in meditative absorption, by one dwelling elsewhere — these are the last.

The Vinayamūla, the eleventh fascicle, the last.

"Venerable One, if a fully ordained monk mocks or ridicules a messenger, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "Likewise, by letter, by gesture, by attributing a name to another, or through an intermediary, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "Venerable One, if a fully ordained monk mocks or ridicules a monk who is mad, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "Likewise, if he mocks or ridicules one whose mind is disturbed, one overcome by sensation, one who is confused, one who is deaf, one who is asleep, one occupied with manual work, one who is utterly intoxicated, one in meditative absorption, or a monk dwelling elsewhere, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "Venerable One, if a monk mocks or ridicules a nun, a probationer (śikṣamāṇā), a novice monk, or a novice nun, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "Venerable One, if a fully ordained nun mocks or ridicules a nun, what does that entail?" An offense that entails expiation. "If she mocks or ridicules a monk, a probationer, a novice monk, or a novice nun, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "Venerable One, if one who has returned the training, having received ordination, mocks or ridicules, what does that entail?" An offense that entails expiation. "If she mocks or ridicules a probationer, a novice monk, a novice nun, one in whom a previous transgression has arisen, and so forth, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

A summary verse:

Transgression, and likewise body and speech,
Silence, mark,
Laypeople and the like,
The group of four — these are the last.

This was requested of the Blessed One, the Buddha, by the Venerable Upāli and his retinue.

"Venerable One, when the Blessed One said that wearing an instruction in one's ear constitutes an offense that entails expiation — what does that mean, Venerable One?"

"Venerable One, if a monk, for reasons other than a transgression, delivers a message in speech that causes it to be worn in the ear, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

"Venerable One, if a monk causes an instruction to be worn in the ear by means of a bodily gesture, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

"Venerable One, if a monk from a central region causes an instruction to be worn in the ear by addressing a central-region monk in a border-region dialect, and that central-region monk does not understand that border-region dialect, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "If one from a border region causes an instruction to be worn in the ear by addressing a border-region monk in a central-region dialect, and that border-region monk does not understand the central-region dialect, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "If one from a border region causes an instruction to be worn in the ear by addressing a central-region monk in a border-region dialect, and that central-region monk does not understand the border-region dialect, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "If one from a central region causes an instruction to be worn in the ear by addressing a border-region monk in a central-region dialect, and that border-region monk does not understand the central-region dialect, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

"Venerable One, if a monk causes an instruction to be worn in the ear through silence, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

"Venerable One, if a monk, while a nun's instruction is being worn in his ear, undergoes a change of marks and becomes a monk, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "Venerable One, if a nun, while a monk's instruction is being worn in her ear, undergoes a change of marks and becomes a nun, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

"Venerable One, if a monk causes a layperson's instruction to be worn in the ear, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "Likewise, if he causes to be worn in the ear an instruction belonging to one who has undertaken vows, to a eunuch, to a hermaphrodite, to a suspended nun, to a matricide, to a patricide, to one who has killed an arhat, to a non-Buddhist adherent, to one who has entered among non-Buddhist adherents, to one who has taken up a position of concealment, to one who was initially discordant, or to one in whom a previous transgression has arisen — what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

"Venerable One, if a monk causes an instruction to be worn in the ear of one who is creating a schism in the Saṅgha, of one who with malicious intent has drawn blood from a Tathāgata, of one who is living separately, or of one who has been given a training rule — what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

"Venerable One, if a monk, perceiving a fully ordained person as fully ordained, causes an instruction to be worn in the ear, what does that entail?" An offense that entails expiation. "If he causes an instruction to be worn in the ear of a fully ordained person while perceiving that person as not fully ordained, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "If he causes an instruction to be worn in the ear of one who is not fully ordained while perceiving that person as fully ordained, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "If he causes an instruction to be worn in the ear of one who is not fully ordained while perceiving that person as not fully ordained, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

Through a messenger, through a letter, through hand-signs,
Through accusing another and through intermediaries,

Summary verse:

What is improper, the person,
What is not permitted, and the excess —
The mark, just the set of four,
Body, speech, and likewise solitude —
Laypeople and so forth
Being obstructed — that is the last.

The venerable Nearby Retinue asked the Blessed One, the Buddha: "Venerable Blessed One, you have declared that any monk who lays down or has laid down a Saṅgha (saṃgha) bed or seat that is fully enclosed, or a mat, a cushion, a board, or a low stool without concealing it, or having laid it out without covering it, or without storing it, or without entrusting it to a monk who is present before departing — apart from such exceptions, commits an offense that entails expiation. What did you mean by that? Venerable One, is there a case where a monk lays down or has laid down a Saṅgha bed or seat, without concealing it, without covering it, and without entrusting it, yet still being present there would not constitute an offense that entails expiation?" Nearby Retinue said: "It is as follows: when the bedding has become suitable as to kin yet the cause has become unsuitable as to kin, or when it has become suitable as to cause yet unsuitable as to kin, or when both have become unsuitable — what is unsuitable refers to bedding mixed with felt from elephant-hide, ox-hair, wild-animal wool, or goat-wool, or any such bedding that has not been designated. If one lays it down or has it laid down without concealing it, without covering it, without storing it, and then departs from there, it becomes an offense.

"Venerable One, if a monk lays down or has laid down an individual person's bed or seat without concealing it, without covering it, without entrusting it, and then departs from there, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "Venerable One, if a monk lays down or has laid down a Saṅgha chair or stool without concealing it, without covering it, without storing it, and then departs from there — is there a case where it would not constitute an offense that entails expiation?" Nearby Retinue said: "Indeed there is: if it is eight of the Sugata's finger-breadths or less, that is permitted."

"Venerable One, if a monk lays down or has laid down a Saṅgha bed or seat without concealing it, without covering it, without storing it, and without entrusting it to a monk who is present, and having departed from there undergoes a change of status and becomes a nun, what does that entail?" An offense that entails expiation. "Venerable One, if a nun lays down or has laid down a Saṅgha bed or seat without concealing it, without covering it, without storing it, and without entrusting it, and having departed from there undergoes a change of status and becomes a monk, what does that entail?" An offense that entails expiation.

"Venerable One, if a monk lays down or has laid down a Saṅgha bed or seat without concealing it, without covering it, without storing it, and without entrusting it to a monk who is present, and then departs beyond the boundary perceiving it to be beyond the boundary, what does that entail?" An offense that entails expiation. "If he departs beyond the boundary perceiving it to be within the boundary, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "If he departs within the boundary perceiving it to be beyond the boundary, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

"If he departs within the boundary perceiving it to be within the boundary, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

"Venerable One, if a monk lays down or has laid down a Saṅgha bed or seat without concealing it, without covering it, and then entrusts it to a monk who is present by means of a bodily gesture and departs, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

"Venerable One, if a monk from the central region entrusts a Saṅgha bed or seat to a monk from the central region using the dialect of a border region and then departs, and that monk from the central region does not understand the dialect of the border region, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "If a border monk entrusts a Saṅgha bed or seat to a border monk using the dialect of the central region and then departs, and that border monk does not understand the dialect of the central region, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "If a border monk entrusts a Saṅgha bed or seat to a monk of the central region using the dialect of the border region and then departs, and that monk from the central region does not understand the dialect of the border region, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "If a monk from the central region entrusts a Saṅgha bed or seat to a border monk using the dialect of the central region and then departs, and that border monk does not understand the dialect of the central region, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

"Venerable One, if a monk lays down or has laid down a Saṅgha bed or seat without concealing it, without covering it, and then entrusts it to one he perceives to be an isolated place when it is indeed an isolated place and departs, what does that entail?" An offense entailing a downfall (pārājika). "If he perceives an isolated place as not isolated, what does that entail?" An offense entailing a downfall. "If he perceives a non-isolated place as isolated, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "If he departs perceiving a non-isolated place as non-isolated, what does that entail?" There is no downfall.

"Venerable One, if a monk lays down or has laid down a Saṅgha bed or seat without concealing it, without covering it, without storing it, and without entrusting it to a layperson, and then departs, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "Similarly, if he departs without entrusting it to a monk who has accepted a precept, to a novice, to a eunuch, to a nun who has been expelled, to one who has not died, to one whose father has died, to one who has slain an arhat, to a non-Buddhist adherent, to one who has entered among non-Buddhist adherents, to one dwelling as a thief, to one who does not dwell there, to one who has previously committed a downfall, or to one who is initially incompatible, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

"Venerable One, if a monk departs without entrusting it to one who causes a schism in the Saṅgha, or to one who has, with malicious intent, drawn blood from a Tathāgata, or to one who lives apart, or to one who is in the midst of giving up the training, what does that entail?" An offense entailing a downfall.

"Venerable One, is it the case that if a monk lays down or has laid down a Saṅgha bed or seat without concealing it, without covering it, without storing it, and without entrusting it to a monk who is present, he still does not incur a downfall if he leaves it in the vicinity?" There is a vicinity: that is, if he does not abandon that bed or seat carelessly, and if any one of eight obstacles has arisen for going to or remaining in that monastery.

A summary verse:

One who is mad, or whose mind is disturbed,
one overcome by sensation, or dull-witted,
one fallen asleep, or seized by hardship, or thoroughly intoxicated,
and one who has entered meditative absorption and then gone elsewhere — these are the last.

"Venerable One, if a monk lays down or has laid down a Saṅgha bed or seat without concealing it, without covering it, without storing it, and departs without entrusting it to a monk who is mad, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "Similarly, if he departs without entrusting it to a monk whose mind is disturbed, to one overcome by sensation, to one who is dull-witted, to one who is deaf and mute, to one who has fallen asleep, to one occupied with manual work, to one who is thoroughly intoxicated, to one who has entered meditative absorption, or to one who is dwelling elsewhere, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

"Venerable one, if a monk entrusts it to a nun and then departs, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "If he entrusts it to a probationer nun or to a novice nun and departs, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

"Venerable one, if a nun spreads out a bed or seat belonging to the Saṅgha, or has one spread out, and then departs without putting it away, without storing it, and without entrusting it to a nun who is present, what does that entail?" A transgression has been committed.

"Venerable one, if a monk departs without entrusting it to a monk, a probationer nun, or a novice nun, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

"Venerable one, if one who has received the training spreads out a bed or seat belonging to the Saṅgha, or has one spread out, and then departs without putting it away, without storing it, and without entrusting it to a monk who is present, what does that entail?" A transgression has been committed. "With respect to probationer nuns, novices, novice nuns, and those in whom an earlier transgression has occurred and so forth — an offense has been committed."

Then the venerable Upāli (the close attendant) asked the Blessed One, the buddha: "Venerable one, what is the meaning of what the Blessed One has declared — that apart from certain circumstances, a transgression is committed by any monk who spreads out a grass mat or a leaf mat in the assembly hall of the Saṅgha, or has one spread out, and then departs without gathering it up, without having it stored, and without entrusting it to a monk who is present?" The remainder of the text — concerning what is impermissible, what is foremost in eight finger-breadths of the Sugata, and excepting the surplus — is to be set forth in full in the same way as for beds and seats.

Then the venerable Upāli asked the Blessed One, the buddha: "Venerable one, what is the meaning of what the Blessed One has declared — that apart from certain circumstances, a transgression is committed by any monk who, overcome by anger and agitation and with a displeased mind, expels a monk from the assembly hall of the Saṅgha, or has him expelled?"

"Venerable one, if a monk, angered, has a monk expelled through a messenger, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "Likewise, if he has him expelled or caused to be expelled through a written message, through a gesture, by imputing it to another, or through an intermediary, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

Summary verse:
