Thus was this asked of the Buddha, the Blessed One, by the venerable Upāli and his attendant retinue.

"Venerable one, the Blessed One has declared that a transgression occurs when a monk, knowingly arriving late, makes use of his coming as a pretext and then, having pushed past monks who were seated earlier in the assembly hall of the Saṅgha, sits or lies down on a seat. What is the meaning of this ruling?"

"Venerable one, if a monk pushes past an insane monk and then sits or lies down on a seat, what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. Likewise, if he pushes past a monk whose mind is disturbed, one afflicted with feeling, one who is dim-witted, one who is deaf, one who is asleep, one who is occupied with work, one who is thoroughly intoxicated, one who has entered meditative absorption, or a monk who is staying in another dwelling, and then sits or lies down on a seat — what does that entail? An offense has been committed.

Summary verse:
A person, and the Saṅgha,
likewise the night, and a group of four,
laypeople and the like,
the improperly ordained, the probationer, the nun.

"Venerable one, if a monk pushes past monks who were seated earlier in the assembly hall of a private individual and then sits or lies down on a seat — what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "Venerable one, if a monk pushes past monks who were seated earlier in places other than the assembly hall of the Saṅgha and then sits or lies down on a seat — what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "Venerable one, if a monk pushes past monks who were seated earlier in the assembly hall of the Saṅgha and then sits or lies down on a seat, and during the night it turns out to be a nun — what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "Venerable one, if a nun pushes past nuns who were seated earlier and then sits or lies down on a seat, and during the night it turns out to be a monk — what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

"Venerable one, if a monk, perceiving fully ordained monks as fully ordained, pushes past them and then sits or lies down on a seat — what does that entail?" A transgression has been committed. "If, perceiving fully ordained monks as not fully ordained, he does so — what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "If, perceiving those not fully ordained as fully ordained, he does so — what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "If, perceiving those not fully ordained as not fully ordained, he does so — what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

"Venerable one, if a monk pushes past a layperson and then sits or lies down on a seat — what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. Likewise, if he pushes past a monk who has accepted the training, a neuter, a hermaphrodite, or one against whom a transgression has previously arisen, and then sits or lies down on a seat — what does that entail? An offense has been committed.

"Venerable one, if a monk pushes past a monk who is causing a schism in the Saṅgha, one who with malicious intent has shed the blood of a Tathāgata, one who is living separately, or one who has been given a training rule, and then sits or lies down on a seat — what does that entail?" A transgression has been committed.

"Venerable one, is there a case in which a monk who pushes past monks who were seated earlier in the assembly hall of the Saṅgha and then sits or lies down does not commit a transgression?" There is such a case: when those nearby are present and the lodging has become unsuitable — that is, when the lodging is unfit for the full performance of the acts of the monks.

"Venerable one, if a monk pushes past a nun, a probationer nun, a novice monk, or a novice nun and then sits or lies down on a seat — what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "Venerable one, if a nun pushes past a monk, a probationer nun, a novice monk, or a novice nun and then sits or lies down on a seat — what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

"Venerable one, when a training rule has been given, if a monk, knowing that other monks are already present in the assembly hall of the Saṅgha, pushes in after them, forces his way by weight, and then sits or lies down on a seat — what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "Venerable one, if a nun, in a lodging for the rains retreat, knowing that other nuns are already present, pushes in after them, forces her way by weight, and then sits or lies down on a seat — what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. As for a probationer nun, a novice monk, a novice nun, and cases where an offense has already occurred beforehand — these constitute a wrongdoing.

Summary verse:
A person and the Saṅgha,
likewise a night and groups of four,
having rendered a place unsuitable,
and being covered from above — these are the last.

The venerable Upāli asked the Blessed One, the Buddha: "Venerable one, the Blessed One has declared that whatever monk, knowing that a chair or the foot of a chair will fall upon the space overhead above the assembly hall of the Saṅgha, yet forces it down by weight and sits or lies down — commits an offense. What is that?" "Venerable one, if a monk, knowing that a chair or the foot of a chair will fall upon the space overhead above a private individual's assembly hall, forces it down by weight and then sits or lies down — what does that entail?" A wrongdoing. "Venerable one, if a monk, in places other than the Saṅgha's assembly hall, knowing that a chair or the foot of a chair will fall upon the space overhead, forces it down by weight and then sits or lies down — what does that entail?" A wrongdoing. "Venerable one, if a monk, knowing that a chair or the foot of a chair will fall upon the space overhead, forces it down by weight and then sits or lies down, and in doing so undergoes a change of sex and becomes a nun — what does that entail?" A wrongdoing. "Venerable one, if a nun, knowing that something will fall upon the space overhead, forces it down by weight and then sits or lies down, and in doing so undergoes a change of sex and becomes a monk — what does that entail?" A wrongdoing. "Venerable one, if a monk, perceiving space overhead where something will fall, forces down a chair or the foot of a chair by weight and then sits or lies down — what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "If one perceives what is space as not space — what does that entail?" No offense. "If one perceives what is not space as space — what does that entail?" A wrongdoing. "If one perceives what is not space as not space — what does that entail?" No offense. "Venerable one, is there a case in which a monk, knowing that a chair or the foot of a chair will fall upon the space overhead, sits or lies down and yet does not commit an offense?" There is such a case: when those nearby are present and the lodging has become unsuitable — that is, when it is unfit for monks to make full use of. "Venerable one, is there a case in which a monk sits or lies down using a chair or the foot of a chair and yet does not commit an offense?" There is such a case: when the top of the assembly hall is covered by a large flat stone, or covered by a small flat stone, or covered by something convex, or covered by a flat board, or it is a tent structure, or one sits or lies down on ground that is at a lower level. "Venerable one, when a training rule has been given, if one forces down a chair or the foot of a chair by weight and sits or lies down — knowing that it will fall upon the space overhead — what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "Venerable one, if a nun, knowing that a chair or the foot of a chair will fall upon the space overhead, forces it down by weight and then sits or lies down — what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. As for a probationer nun, a novice monk, a novice nun, and those where an offense has already occurred beforehand — these force their way down by weight and sit or lie down —

— what does lying down entail?" An offense has been committed.

The venerable attendant retinue then put a question to the Buddha, the Blessed One: "Venerable one, the Blessed One has declared that if a monk knowingly sprinkles water containing living creatures on grass, on dung, or on the ground, or causes another to do so, it is an offense that entails a downfall. What exactly is the scope of that ruling?" As for sprinkling grass, dung, or the ground with water containing living creatures, and causing another to do so — the remainder is to be analyzed in accordance with the explanation of downfalls as set out in the Vibhaṅga.

Summary verse:
By messenger, by letter, by gesture,
by imputing a name to another, by intermediary,
the deranged, the mentally disturbed,
the incapacitated — these cases are concluded.

The venerable attendant retinue then put a question to the Buddha, the Blessed One: "Venerable one, the Blessed One has declared that if a monk undertakes the construction of a large temple, he is to examine the ground beginning from the perception of however much space is needed for the doorway recess, the foundations, and the area of light, and is to build it with two or three courses of stone along with the rubble fill — and that to build higher than that is an offense entailing a downfall. What is the scope of that ruling?"

"Venerable one, to begin with: if a monk causes more than two or three courses above the foundation to be built by a messenger — what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "Likewise, if by letter, by gesture, by imputing a name to another, or by intermediary — what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

"Venerable one, if a monk causes a deranged person to build more than two or three courses above the foundation — what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "Likewise, if he causes one mentally disturbed, one incapacitated by illness, an imbecile, someone deaf-mute, or someone dwelling elsewhere to build more than that — what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

Summary verse:
A large building, a building for a person, likewise one begun by another,
the matter of characteristics, that of a quarter-share,
the giving of the training, and the case of a nun.

"Venerable one, if a monk undertakes to build some other Saṅgha property apart from a large temple and builds more than two or three courses above the foundation — what does that entail?" It is solely an offense entailing a downfall. "Venerable one, if a monk undertakes to build a personal temple and builds more than two or three courses above the foundation — what does that entail?" An offense has been committed.

"Venerable one, is there a case where a monk builds a Saṅgha temple and, even though he builds more than two or three courses above the foundation, no downfall ensues?" — "There is, attendant. For instance, if the temple is built entirely of stone, or of brick, or entirely of wood, or if it is covered with thatch, or covered with flat stones and flat boards, or if the patron donor has said 'Take note of where you have reached' — indeed, if the donor says 'Note where you have finished' — then take note of that."

"Venerable one, if a monk completes what another has begun above two or three courses — what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. "Venerable one, if a monk begins above two or three courses and does not complete it — what does that entail?" An offense has been committed. If he becomes mentally disturbed or dies, the ruling is the same.

"Venerable one, if, while a monk is building above two or three courses, a change of status occurs and he becomes a nun, and then she completes the work — what does that entail?" It is an offense entailing a downfall. "Venerable one, if, while a nun is building above two or three courses, a change of status occurs and she becomes a monk, and then he completes the work — what does that entail?" It is an offense entailing a downfall.

Here ends the twelfth fascicle of the text called the Minor Section of Scriptural Inquiry.

This twelfth fascicle contains incomplete scriptural inquiry — the Vinaya text entitled "The Twelfth Fascicle of the Minor Section of Scriptural Inquiry Containing Questions and Answers up to the Section on Building Two or Three Courses High, Being the Second of the Two Parts of the Offenses Entailing a Downfall That Occur in the Superior Text or the Elder Text Alone." Together with it stands the fifty-third fascicle, which contains the completed scriptural inquiry known as "Completed Inquiry of the Superior Text," comprising the first four sections of the Vibhaṅga offenses, the completed inquiries of the seventeen bases, and eleven sections including those beginning with the Vinaya-administering section — both having been translated during the time of the translator monk Nāgadhvaja and his colleagues. However, the edition containing the incomplete inquiry did not become widely available in Tibet, and in earlier times many errors in practice arose based on corrupt manuscripts of the completed inquiry. Later, when the Elder Darma Sengge, residing at Öl-gö Monastery in Tö, compiled the four-section canon together with the nuns' Vibhaṅga, the great spiritual friend Jangchub Sengge of Ü-Tsang sent searchers to the monasteries of Ü and Tsang seeking the incomplete inquiry manuscript. Subsequently, having found a small copy at Samyé Chimpu, and having sent word through the monk Śīlaguṇa to the great Vinaya-holder, the Śākya monk Vaśitā Śīla, requesting him to come, he arrived, though the manuscript copy was found to be extremely defective on the outer pages. Many manuscript fees were paid and it was brought, and all doubts about every discrepancy in practice were resolved there and then. Having accomplished this through great hardship, and though the former exemplar was exceedingly corrupt, he afterwards caused it to spread and flourish widely, and brought benefit to all beings.

Here some people say that the incomplete text is the Elder Text and the completed text is the Superior Text — but this is incorrect, for the Sanskrit term uttara can be rendered both as "elder" and as "superior." Although both versions, the incomplete and the completed, exist, this incomplete inquiry is extremely pure as regards its Indian manuscript source, and the commentary by Ācārya Geleg Shényen has now appeared as a commentary on the incomplete version. If there is a discrepancy between the two, one should rely on the incomplete inquiry.

The reason why corruptions arise between the two versions of the inquiry is as follows, as stated in the commentary on the scriptural transmission inquiry: After the Teacher passed away, a brahmin king arose who was called Gyal Shé, hostile to the teaching. He caused many harms to the teaching — he demolished stūpas, burned the assembly halls of the Saṅgha, killed monks, and heaped up the scriptural volumes of the Buddha's word and burned them. Thereupon, gradually, monks collected from various regions and places all the volumes of the three scriptural collections and preserved what had been damaged and destroyed — but the volume of the Elder Text was not found. They then heard that in the land of Kashgar a monk was reciting the Elder Text, and so they went to that land, approached him, and said: "We have heard that you recite the Elder Text — please recite it for us." He replied: "Some of it I remember, and some I do not." Then they told him: "Recite exactly as much as you do remember." And he recited it just as he recalled it. The monks thought: "For now let us collect the text, and later we shall compare and analyze it word by word and meaning by meaning," and so it was written down. But afterwards, distracted by immediate affairs for a long period of time, they did not arrange it properly as it should have been. Other monks in other lands also came to recite it differently. For this very reason, the originally complete base became impaired, and so in this Elder Text the recitations are of many kinds and the meanings are of many kinds.

For this reason, in the exposition of the Venerable Yönten Ö on the Vinaya, when repeatedly inquired, some say it appears thus and so. Therefore, since the version known as the Great Request of the noble Upāli appears with the recitation complete, the Elder Text with its complete request is the properly collected original base; and since that original base is without impairment, it is thoroughly pure. But the version with the incomplete request — whether it appeared in the minds of certain monks of the central lands, or whether it is famed to have been found later among ash in which it had not been consumed by fire — is also held to be the unimpaired original text, and so it too is exceedingly pure.

Therefore, this version established through such purity and the labor of many difficulties came to be the crown jewel of the great elder masters of the Tibetan tradition who are learned and disciplined in the vows — it is a scriptural recitation with the complete request, compiled from the great ruined cities of the central lands by the great elders and other learned ones. And Mchims Thamsched Mkhyen Pa, whose maṇḍala of knowledge spread across the sky of all that is to be known — holding the mother of the Tripiṭaka, the Vinaya, and the Sūtra collections — whose rays of renown illumined all directions without exception, had a copy invited from the great temple of La Stod Ol Rgod and erected it at the glorious temple of Narthang, the source of the Teachings. Taking that as a copy, the Śākya monk Jampal Yangkyi erected it at the glorious temple of Narthang. And there the incomplete upper recitation of the Elder Text — the very beginning of the complete request of the excellent text obtained from a Kashmiri monk — was erected as the head of this edition.

That same copy was subsequently invited so that the teaching of the Victors might flourish and benefit and happiness might arise for sentient beings. It was properly erected at the glorious Guntang Temple — the dwelling of the holy learned and accomplished ones of the eastern region, where the chief master of the teaching of the glorious community of the Victors resides.

This concludes the translation. The following are translator's notes.

The summary verse at the opening is a mnemonic condensing the cases treated in the passage. Its terms are terse and elliptical by design: "three animals" refers to the three animal-transformation scenarios; "three passages" to the oral, excretory, and urinary orifices; "woman departed" to the bisected or decapitated woman cases; "severance" to the waist-severing case; "holding and repeated" to holding and repeated-use scenarios elaborated elsewhere in the text; "tooth, not touching, sow" to further cases in subsequent sections. This is standard Vinaya mnemonic verse and no single reconstruction of all terms is certain.

The phrase rendered "as much as is required for a jewel to pass from a tooth" uses "tooth" (so) as a technical Vinaya anatomical landmark for the boundary of the oral passage; the precise anatomical referent is a matter of traditional Vinaya commentary.

The text is the Vinayottaragrantha, the "Uttaragrantha" section of the Mūlasarvāstivāda Vinaya, here in its classical Tibetan translation. The legal reasoning throughout follows Mūlasarvāstivāda scholastic categories for the pārājika rule on unchastity.  The summary verse is a mnemonic rubric listing the classificatory headings that structure the surrounding casuistry: another's formed limb (as a site of insertion), the two bodily passages, the two organs, two grades of impairment, and the obstruction cases, organized into groups of six and four. The Tibetan is terse and quasi-technical, as is typical of Vinaya verse summaries; the rendering follows the most natural syntactic reading while preserving the list structure.

The term rendered "formed limb" (gzhan gyi yan lag skyes) refers to a genitalia-like protrusion or limb of another being that can serve as a site, as the surrounding prose context makes clear.

The cases involving change of status (monk becoming laywoman, nun becoming monk mid-act) reflect a classical Vinaya discussion of whether the defeat attaches to the act or to the status at the moment of completion; the text affirms defeat in both cases.  The summary verse near the middle of the passage is a mnemonic gāthā enumerating categories of cases treated in the surrounding Vinaya discussion. The rendering follows the apparent referents from context: "the group of eighty" refers to the monastic assembly (ubhayasaṃgha) of eighty; "the sign" refers to the identifying mark or recognition of a person's monastic status; and "groups of seven" refers to the sets of seven ordination-related cases. The verse is terse and formulaic, and some enumeration details may belong to a longer running commentary not fully recoverable from this excerpt alone.

The distinction between moving a precious stone "from a small place to a large place" versus "from a large place to another place" as determinative of defeat versus grave offense reflects a standard Vinaya principle: displacement that fully removes an object from its owner's control completes the act of theft and constitutes a defeat, while partial or incomplete displacement yields only a grave offense.  The term rendered throughout as "customs post" is the Tibetan for a toll or customs checkpoint, and "goods subject to customs duty" refers to merchandise liable to be taxed or assessed at such a post. The legal distinctions here — wrongdoing, serious offense, and defeat — follow the standard Vinaya gradations of offense severity. The phrase "it is as the object itself stands" in the dice-from-the-sky case means the ruling depends on the actual assessed value of the dice, following the general rule for theft by value. The question of "retrieval by supernatural power" having no offense is a well-attested Vinaya ruling that miraculous means lie outside the scope of the theft rule. The repeated case structures follow the matrix-style (mātṛkā) format standard in Vinaya abhidhamma analysis.  The "summary verse" at the opening is a mnemonic gāthā listing the topics covered in this section of the Vinaya dialogue; it is in condensed verse form and some items are laconic. "The islands — for the sake of others, these are not downfalls" likely refers to cases involving ferry-crossings or territories treated elsewhere in the text where acting on behalf of others removes the downfall.

"King Peacock" (རྒྱལ་པོ་རྨ་བྱ་བས་བྱས་པ) refers to King Mayūra or a ruler associated with the Maurya dynasty (the Tibetan rma bya, "peacock," being a rendering of Maurya); this is a standard reference in Vinaya texts to the origin of the kārṣāpaṇa coin.

"Theft of the Dharma" (ཆོས་རྐུ་བ) is a technical Vinaya category referring to plagiarism or misappropriation of teachings; it does not incur a pārājika defeat because the Dharma is not property with a conventional monetary value.

The thresholds of māṣaka, kākaṇi, and kārṣāpaṇa reflect the monetary system underlying the pārājika rule on theft; the exact equivalences given here follow the Mūlasarvāstivāda Vinaya tradition.  The three continents named — western Cattle-Enjoyment (Aparagodānīya), eastern Videha (Pūrvavideha), and northern Unpleasant Sound (Uttarakuru) — are the traditional Buddhist cosmological continents. Uttarakuru is described in Vinaya literature as a place where the concept of ownership does not apply, hence the ruling that theft per se cannot occur there, though the intention to steal still generates an offence.

The term rendered "accepted" (yongs su bzung ba) in the final set of permutations refers to whether an item has been formally received or taken into one's possession. The fourfold schema mirrors the standard Vinaya pattern of perception-versus-reality combinations seen earlier in the text.  The summary verse is a mnemonic condensing the preceding case categories. Its terse formulation — "three animal births," "craving as mother — a group of four," "two wombs," "a caretaker," and "for perception — also a group of four" — refers to the enumerated sub-cases in the surrounding Vinaya discussion and is rendered as closely as the compressed Tibetan allows. The full referents of each item would require access to the broader commentary.

The phrase rendered "the existence of action accompanied by defilement" translates "zag pa dang bcas pa'i las kyi srid pa," a technical Abhidharma/Vinaya expression denoting karmically conditioned existence driven by afflicted action, here used allegorically for "father" in a doctrinal gloss on the pārājika rule concerning parricide.  The summary verse is a mnemonic gāthā condensing the preceding casuistic cases. The phrase "two states of mind" refers to the doubt-cases; "two regarding self" to the self-killing cases; "four concerning the arhat" to the four arhat permutations; "two thieves" to the two thief scenarios; "other perceptions" to the perception-mismatch cases; "seven as a group" and "the house" likely refer to further groupings that follow in the text. The verse is highly compressed and its referents are fully intelligible only within the broader textual sequence.

The term rendered "action without interval" translates the Tibetan for what Sanskrit calls ānantarya — the five heinous acts (killing one's mother, father, or an arhat; wounding a buddha; causing a schism in the Saṅgha) that lead without interval to rebirth in the lowest hell. "Proximate fault" renders ānantarya-sabhāga, a category of offenses analogous in gravity to the five.  The phrase rendered "one who resides by stealth" translates a technical Vinaya term for someone who has concealed themselves within the monastic community without legitimate ordination.

The names in the penultimate paragraph are epithets and names of previous buddhas: Vipaśyin is the first of the seven buddhas of antiquity; "one with the crown protrusion" refers to a buddha distinguished by the uṣṇīṣa mark; "destroyer of cyclic existence" and "protector of all" are epithets; Kanakamuni and Kāśyapa are the fifth and sixth of the seven buddhas of antiquity. The passage concerns whether claiming to be a śrāvaka of one of these past buddhas constitutes a defeat, since those buddha-dispensations have ended.

The summary verse is a mnemonic verse enumerating the categories of persons relevant to the preceding case analyses; the translation renders it as closely as the condensed form allows, though the verse's terse style leaves some items open to varying interpretation.  The phrase rendered "secondary afflictions" translates ཉེ་བའི་ཉོན་མོངས་པ, which in Abhidharma context refers to the upakleśas, the subsidiary or derivative afflictions; this rendering is standard in scholastic literature.

The summary verse is a mnemonic gāthā indexing the cases treated in the preceding section; the translation follows the sequence of topics as they appear in the prose.  The place names Wandering Mountain, Bird-Shelter, Vulture's Peak, and Pale Forest are Tibetan renderings of specific locations in the Rājagṛha region associated with the Buddha's teaching activity. Vulture's Peak is the well-known Gṛdhrakūṭa; the others are less certain as to their Sanskrit equivalents, and the Tibetan is treated literally here.

The phrase rendered "superhuman quality" throughout is a standard rendering of the Vinaya technical term for extraordinary spiritual attainments claimed to others, corresponding to Sanskrit uttaramanuṣyadharma. The expression "final offense" renders the Tibetan mtha' 'gyur, signifying the gravest category of defeat, a pārājika.  The passage works through an elaborate matrix of dialect permutations between "central region" monks and "border region" monks, a standard Vinaya device for exploring when communication is or is not intelligible and thus when culpability attaches. The final summary verse is incomplete as transmitted; the Tibetan breaks off mid-verse, so the translation reproduces only what is present in the source.

The term rendered "final offense" corresponds to the Tibetan "mthar 'gyur," literally "becoming ultimate," a technical Vinaya term for the complete or consummated form of the pārājika defeat. "Serious offense" renders "nyes pa sbom po," the heavy offense just short of full defeat. "Wrongdoing" renders "nyes byas," a lesser infraction. These distinctions are doctrinally significant throughout.

"One who was originally of contrary nature" renders "dang po mi mthun pa can," a technical Vinaya category referring to one ordained while possessing a disqualifying condition from the outset.  The phrase rendered "a state of theft" translates a term indicating one who is maintaining a position under false pretenses — that is, one who continues to live as a monk while knowing himself to be disqualified, a recognized technical category in Vinaya literature.

The phrase "one who from the outset is of incompatible nature" refers to someone who was unsuitable for ordination from the beginning due to a disqualifying condition.

"Paṇḍaka" is retained as a standard Vinaya technical term for a person of indeterminate or ambiguous sex, a recognized canonical category.

The summary verse is a mnemonic and its referents are deliberately compressed; the translation preserves the compressed form as verse.

The final question is cut off in the source passage and the translation preserves the abrupt ending accordingly.  The summary verse is a mnemonic gāthā listing categories treated in the preceding and following case analysis. The rendering follows the standard Vinaya format for such summary verses, which are intentionally terse; each term stands for a full case-group. "Marks" renders mtshan, which in the context immediately following refers to sex characteristics. The phrase "attains the marks of a monk" or "attains the marks of a man" refers to a rare Vinaya scenario in which a person undergoes a change of sex characteristics — a recognized edge case in Indian Buddhist legal literature.

"Remainder for the Saṅgha" renders dge 'dun lhag mar 'gyur, the standard Tibetan for saṃghāvaśeṣa, a formal offense requiring formal Saṅgha procedure but not entailing expulsion, in contrast to a pārājika defeat.

The distinction between "one intervening object" cases yielding a grave offense and the "no intervening object perceived as no intervening object" case yielding a saṃghāvaśeṣa follows the logic that direct, unobstructed bodily contact with full correct perception constitutes the graver, classifiable offense category, while contact through a covering reduces the severity. This is consistent with standard Vinaya commentarial analysis.  The term rendered "Saṅgha-remainder offense" throughout is saṃghāvaśeṣa in Sanskrit — a technical Vinaya category designating an offense requiring formal transaction before the Saṅgha for rehabilitation, falling short of a pārājika defeat. The Tibetan "dge 'dun lhag mar" literally means "with a Saṅgha remainder," i.e., one's monastic status is not fully forfeited.

The phrase "woman who belongs to another form of existence" translates "'gro ba gzhan pa'i bud med," which refers to a woman who is a being of a different class of existence — such as a goddess, nāga, or similar non-human female — rather than a human woman. This is a genuine doctrinal category in Vinaya analysis.

The summary verse is a mnemonic condensing the offense-types analyzed in the preceding dialogue. The line "from one place to appear in another" renders "gzhan las gzhan phyir sprul pa," meaning the monk employs a magical emanation or disguise to approach elsewhere.

The phrase rendered "a woman who has not yet been born" is unusual and may refer to a woman still in the womb or to a non-manifested being; Vinaya commentaries treat this as an edge-case analysis of the bodily-contact rule's scope.  The term rendered "paṇḍaka" refers to a person of indeterminate or ambiguous sex or gender. The Tibetan uses two forms: "ma ning" (masculine form) and "ma ning mo" (feminine form), indicating the text distinguishes between male-type and female-type paṇḍakas.

The phrase rendered "make love with me" is literally "let us share together" or "let us divide together," a Vinaya euphemism for solicitation of sexual intercourse. The euphemistic register is intentional in the original.

The summary verse preceding the second section of case-analysis is a mnemonic gāthā listing the categories to be analyzed; its placement and function are characteristic of the Mūlasarvāstivāda Vinaya's scholastic format.  The summary verse is a mnemonic verse enumerating the topics covered in the preceding section of Vinaya analysis. The precise referents of each enumerated item would require the full surrounding context of the Vinaya text to identify with certainty, and so the verse is translated according to its surface meaning without imposing a specific identification of each item.

The term rendered "one who speaks in jest" translates the Tibetan for one who makes witty or playful utterances; the doctrinal point is that such speech, unlike nirvāṇa, arises and ceases and is subject to karmic consequence, which is why the contrast with nirvāṇa is drawn.

The phrase "pandaka" in the preceding continuity passage is retained from the prior translation for consistency; it refers to a person of indeterminate or anomalous gender.  The terms "capable" and "not capable" render "dbang ba" and "mi dbang ba" in the context of householders involved in matrimonial negotiations; they likely refer to a person who is of age or legally empowered to consent to a marriage versus one who is not, such as a minor or someone under guardianship. This is a standard Vinaya distinction and is rendered according to that technical sense.

The final two lines present a compressed summary statement about agency in the act of go-between, likely indicating that the same individual can be both the subject of being conveyed as a message recipient and the one who has already been conveyed in a prior transaction. This is a genuinely elliptical passage and the rendering follows the most plausible reading.  The verse summary enumerates categories of persons involved in the go-between offense. The terms rendered "person of indeterminate sex" and "female of indeterminate sex" translate ma ning and ma ning mo respectively, technical Vinaya terms for individuals of ambiguous or indeterminate sex (Sanskrit paṇḍaka and related categories); the precise boundaries of these categories vary across Vinaya traditions, but "indeterminate sex" is the standard scholarly rendering.

The final sentence is incomplete in the source passage, breaking off mid-thought; this is a genuine textual boundary, not a translation issue.  The phrase rendered "uncovered structure" refers to a building without a complete roof or enclosure, as distinct from a properly roofed cell; this is a recognized Vinaya category.

The passage "if one has parted from life, or dies while still a householder…" and the list that follows describes circumstances under which the offense either does not apply or is transformed, because the subject is no longer a valid member of the Saṅgha or never was. The list is left somewhat open, as the Tibetan text breaks off mid-enumeration.

The "paṇḍaka" (rendered from Tibetan ma ning) refers to a person of indeterminate or ambiguous sex, a recognized Vinaya category disqualified from ordination.  The phrase rendered "seizes the training itself as a loan" is syntactically compressed and the precise transgression being described is elliptical; it likely refers to borrowing or misappropriating something connected to the training, framed as a case parallel to the surrounding rules about ordination status — this is a genuinely uncertain detail in the source.

The term rendered "one who has an owner" refers to a monk who has a lay patron or proprietor commissioning a building on the Saṅgha's behalf; this is a standard Vinaya technical category.

"A Saṅgha remainder" renders the Tibetan term for the class of offense (saṅghāvaśeṣa in Sanskrit) that requires formal communal proceedings but does not constitute a root defeat; "merely an offense" renders the lighter category (duṣkṛta, a misdeed).  The summary verse in the middle of the passage functions as a mnemonic condensation of preceding topics. Its four categories are terse and the precise referents of each phrase depend on the surrounding prose context; the rendering here follows the most natural reading given that context.

The case where perception of a "non-secluded place as non-secluded" yields only a Saṅgha remainder (rather than the heavier "grave offense") is notable. This appears doctrinally deliberate: accusation in a non-secluded setting where witnesses are potentially present is treated as a lesser infraction, meriting a Saṅgha remainder rather than a defeat-class grave offense.

The phrase rendered "having rendered himself invisible" is literally "making it so that he himself is not apparent," referring to the use of drugs or mantras to conceal oneself before making the accusation.  The Saṅgha remainder offense referred to throughout is the saṅghāvaśeṣa, a category of monastic transgression in which the Saṅgha retains authority over the offender, as opposed to a pārājika defeat which entails expulsion. The text is a detailed casuistic Vinaya analysis of the false accusation rule, systematically varying the status of the accused and the mental perception of the accuser.

The summary verse is terse and the Tibetan is formulaic; the rendering given conveys its organizational function, which is to signal that the preceding categories will be elaborated more fully elsewhere.

The phrase rendered "intermediate-sex person" translates ma ning, which in Vinaya contexts denotes a person of indeterminate or non-binary biological sex, distinct from the hermaphrodite (za ma). Commentarial tradition treats these as distinct categories of persons whose Vinaya status requires special consideration.

The passage ends mid-sentence, as the source text supplied concludes there.  The text distinguishes carefully among five outcome categories: defeat (pārājika), Saṅgha remainder (saṃghāvaśeṣa), grave offence (thullaccaya, rendered here as "grave offence"), transgression (āpatti, rendered "transgression" where it refers to a minor offence category), and what is to be individually confessed (pratideśanīya). The term rendered "female trainee" is dge slob ma, which in Vinaya usage denotes the śikṣamāṇā, a woman in the two-year probationary training between novice and full ordination; this is distinguished throughout from the fully ordained nun.  The summary verse is a mnemonic verse listing topics to be elaborated: admonishment, petition, the foolish, the mindful, sitting, appointment, intent, and open space. These are standard Vinaya section-headings compressed into verse form, and the translation reflects that compressed, technical character.

The procedural sequence in this passage reflects standard Mūlasarvāstivāda Vinaya protocol for addressing a saṅghabheda (schism-causing) monk: a preliminary friendly admonishment must precede any formal fourfold act for the formal act to be canonically valid. The term rendered "act of expulsion" translates bskrad pa'i las, the formal act of dismissal or expulsion, which is a prerequisite procedural step in this Vinaya tradition before the schism-admonishment procedure is validly initiated. Just as the Bodhi tree now standing at Bodh Gayā neither is nor is not the same tree beneath which the Buddha attained awakening, so too this stupa is intended as the next generation of stupa — dependently arising according to the conditions of its environment, and ceaselessly proclaiming the Dharma for the benefit of all sentient beings.
